Epilogue

sei bhakta dhanya, ye nā chāḍe prabhura caraṇa  
sei prabhu dhanya, ye nā chāḍe nija-jana  
durdaive sevaka yadi yāya anya sthāne  
sei ṭhākura dhanya tāre cule dhari’ āne

Glorious is that devotee who does not give up the shelter of his Lord, and glorious is that Lord who does not abandon His servant. If by chance a servant falls down and goes somewhere else, glorious is that master who captures him and brings him back by the hair.

—Cc., *Antya* 4.46–47

I offer thousands of prostrated obeisances to my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada. Prabhupada certainly was merciful to me, who was undeserving of his kindness. I tried to escape his mercy many times during his presence on this planet, but at each turn, he grabbed me by the hair and pulled me back. He is thus the glorious master mentioned in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. I aspire to become a worthy and eternal servant of such a glorious master.

## Preface

The ocean of the pastimes of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu is immeasurable and unfathomable. Who can take the courage to measure that great ocean? It is not possible to dip into that great ocean, but its sweet mellow flavor attracts my mind. I thereby stand on the shore of that ocean to try to taste but a drop of it.

—Cc., *Adi* 12.94–95

My humblest obeisances to the pink and soft lotus feet of my Guru Mahārāja, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, an empowered preacher-teacher-warrior. As the pastimes of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu are like an ocean, so the pastimes of His confidential servitor, Śrīla Prabhupāda, are also oceanic. Prabhupāda fearlessly uprooted atheism, pseudo-theism, nihilism, and scientific “word-jugglery,” and boldly led countless souls back to Godhead.

The two holy *dhāmas* of Navadvīpa and Vṛndāvana descended with Prabhupāda. Within those *dhāmas* reside Śrī Śrī Gaura-Nitāi and Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa. Whoever met Prabhupāda also met his Lords, knew Their fragrance, and heard Their desire.

I sat with Prabhupāda in 1972 in Manila, the Philippines, when he was scheduled to speak in the Hotel Intercontinental’s Grand Ballroom. As he waited to start, Prabhupāda toyed with a paper flag on a wooden stick. It broke. Prabhupāda laughed like a child, as if saying, “Just see the nature of this material world.” He then told me: “My desire is that all living beings in the universe go back to Godhead. You please help me with this desire.”

To fulfill his compassionate desire, Prabhupāda, depending on Krsna’s mercy, organized ISKCON, wrote books for worldwide distribution, and trained his students to preach. I was fortunate enough to be one of Prabhupāda’s students. Many others had more association with Prabhupāda, but although my physical association with him was limited, the exchanges were intimate because many took place with few other devotees present. In addition, Prabhupāda kindly gave me his shelter when I was in difficulty. That was his causeless mercy upon me and I thank him again and again for not kicking me away.

I hope the devotees find pleasure in reading these anecdotes about my exchanges with Prabhupāda. At the end of most anecdotes, I have written a personal reflection to show some of what I learned from each exchange. I realize, however, that even the teachings he directed toward me are beyond my scope to deeply understand. Yet I, as his disciple, without claiming to predicate his mind, nevertheless felt the need to extract lessons from each episode, limited as my capacity might be. As for the Lord, so also for His empowered devotee:

ahaṁ hi pṛṣṭo ’ryamaṇo bhavadbhir  
ācakṣa ātmāvagamo ’tra yāvān  
nabhaḥ patanty ātma-samaṁ patattriṇas  
tathā samaṁ viṣṇu-gatiṁ vipaścitaḥ

O *ṛṣis*, who are as powerfully pure as the sun, I shall try to describe to you the transcendental pastimes of Viṣṇu as far as my knowledge is concerned. As the birds fly in the sky as far as their capacity allows, so do the learned devotees describe the Lord as far as their realization allows.

—*Bhāg*. 1.18.23

While writing my remembrances, I referred to Prabhupāda as “Prabhupāda” and not as “Śrīla Prabhupāda.” This was generally our habit when addressing or speaking about Prabhupāda, so I wrote it this way to maintain authenticity. To facilitate easier reading, I dispensed with proper etiquette, and generally referred to my Godbrothers and Godsisters without the more formal “dasa,” “dāsī,” or “Prabhu,” and to my wife simply as “Jagattāriṇī.” The *Conversations* books also follow this standard.

I wrote mostly from notes I started compiling in 1985, and when my memory contradicted information found in the Folio program, I deferred to Folio. By the mercy of Folio, I was also able to add segments of Prabhupāda's lectures and room conversations that were especially meaningful to me or that helped establish Prabhupāda’s mood in a particular place. I lightly edited Prabhupāda’s words when necessary to make his spoken words flow more like written text,

Also, I added information that did not directly refer to Prabhupāda when it clarified circumstances or described the transitions in my life. Since Prabhupāda had to deal with those transitions, I thought them relevant.

This book does not retell, except in passing, many great and well known incidents in Prabhupāda’s preaching. Rather, it narrates apparently insignificant incidents wherein Prabhupāda compassionately worked to save an insignificant soul by his actions, example, and words. Although I have recorded these few incidents, we could have written an entire book on each day of Prabhupāda’s association, because each was a continuous flow of meaningful events. As Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī related about Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu: “Even Anantadeva, who possesses thousands of mouths, cannot fully describe the ecstatic transformations that Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu experienced in a single day. What can a creature like me describe of those transformations? I can only give a hint of them, as if showing the moon through the branches of a tree.” (Cc., *Antya* 17.64–65)

If, in fulfilling my desire to glorify Prabhupāda, I have offended any Vaiṣṇavas, I beg their forgiveness, for I depend upon the tolerance and mercy of the Vaiṣṇavas. I wrote these vignettes to purify my heart, and to feel closer to Prabhupāda, and I will consider this book successful if the reader experiences through it the compassionate association of Prabhupāda, my glorious master.

Still others donated valuable time and worked hard in conceiving, writing, polishing, and preparing *My Glorious Master* for publication and distribution. Durāśaya dāsa, an old Hong Kong preaching veteran, and Savitrī-devī dāsī uncovered the magazine and newspaper articles used in the book.

Svarga-devī dāsī, Sundarānanda dāsa, and Ananda Vṛndāvana-devī dāsī helped the publishing from Vṛndāvana, and Aniruddha dāsa helped from Australia.

Dina Bandhu dāsa, Puṇḍarika Vidyānidhi dāsa, Bindu Mādhava dāsa, Jambavan dāsa, Jīva dāsa, Veda Vyāsa dāsa, Mārkaṇḍeya dāsa and Bhakta Lyle all provided computer-related assistance.

Braja Bihārī dāsa, amid responsibly carrying out his other important duties, underwent unlimited anxiety and toiled hard to do the layout and act as production coordinator to insure My *Glorious Master* would be a suitable offering to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s lotus feet.

My final thanks goes to Jagattāriṇi-devī dāsī, for her sensitive suggestions as I prepared this manuscript, and for her “through thick-and-thin” support during most of my Kṛṣṇa conscious life.

Without the association, support, and affection of so many kind Vaisnavas, how could we go on?

## New York City, 1968

New York City, 1968

## Beginning the Journey from Buffalo to Disciple

Jim Kurtain and I hitchhiked down from Buffalo, New York to New York City. Our purpose was clear: we wanted to see Swamijī—A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami—the Founder-*ācārya* of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. We were both students at the State University of New York at Buffalo and had both begun chanting at a free university course given in mid-January of 1968. Now we had become interested enough to thumb our way to New York City.

Swamijī had just returned from India where he had gone to recover from a heart problem. He landed first in San Francisco and then traveled on to New York. He had only been there for a few days when Rūpānuga, our teacher at the free university course, suggested we travel to New York to meet him.

The early March weather was cold and snow had blanketed the fertile upstate farmland. We left on a Friday morning and covered most of the 250 miles to New York City by late afternoon. Our final ride dropped us at the upper tip of Manhattan,so we rushed aboard a subway, headed downtown, and jumped off in Greenwich Village, the West Village. We knew that Swamijī’s Friday night *kīrtana* and class began at 7 P.M. and as we were already late, we ran cross-town to the East Village and arrived at “Matchless Gifts,” 26 Second Avenue.

*Kīrtana* had already ended by the time we arrived and Swamijī was speaking from the raised platform the New York devotees had built for him. I stepped into the storefront, sat down and stared. It was the first time I saw my spiritual master.

\* \* \*

Several months before making this trip to New York City, I went to visit my parents in Rockaway, New York. A letter arrived from a university friend named Eric, who told me he had enrolled me in the Bhakti-Yoga Meditation course that started in January 1968. I became quite happy while reading his letter, for I considered myself a seeker of truth.

A few days later, I watched bearded Rāyarāma on television describe how he met Prabhupāda. He said he was walking down Second Avenue one day and met the Swami. I thought, “Things like that never happen to me,” but my time was getting closer.

Then again a few days later, while visiting a friend, we watched Les Crane interview Swamijī in San Francisco. The devotees chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa with arms raised in the air. Then Les Crane asked Swamijī if he was liberated. Swamijī replied, “Yes.” He then waved his hand toward his Haight-Ashbury band of disciples who sat on a carpet at his feet and added, “And all my disciples are also liberated.” Les Crane’s final question to Swamijī was about his age. Prabhupāda smiled widely and said, “Seventy. And I still have all my teeth.”

After I returned to Buffalo, I found myself chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa in a meeting room in Norton Hall, the student union. It wasn’t the first time I had chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa—but this was different from the 1967 “Be-in” in a park in Buffalo where I had drummed a guitar case and sung the *mantra.* The incense and candles burning on the altar, the Persian carpet, and the forty university students sitting in various postures on the floor added an Eastern flavor, but the real difference was Rūpānuga, an initiated disciple of Swamijī, who was bringing Kṛṣṇa consciousness to our university. After Rūpānuga led us in chanting, he began his lecture. I agreed with his philosophical thrust that material gain cannot satisfy us, and I decided to take up chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa as he advised: for fifteen minutes twice every day, morning and evening. Before leaving that first class I reflected, “I wanted meditation and I got a religion instead. Wouldn’t it be my luck.”

Despite my luck, by the next class I thought of myself as a devotee. The Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* had incredible potency, and it prompted me to compare all my previous attempts at self-realization to a dog’s chasing his tail. A voice prompted me from within, “You’ve never completed anything. Keep chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and complete this.” As the weeks passed, I bought beads and switched over to a *prasādam* diet.

Rūpānuga told me, “Whatever you like to do, you can do in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.”

“What about listening to records?”

“You can listen to Kṛṣṇa conscious records,” he said. Interested, I inquired how many Kṛṣṇa conscious albums were out. His reply, with full confidence: “One.”

“One! I have a hundred and fifty albums that I play one after another!”

I nevertheless purchased the “one” album and played it continuously. In the privacy of my attic apartment, I flipped the album again and again. Amazingly, this one album satisfied me. Gradually I began to decipher Swamijī’s Indian accent: “On the cover of the record album …” and, “There is no need to understand the language of the *mantra* … even a child can take part … and dance in ecstasy.”

By the time I arrived at 26 Second Avenue in March and was face to face with Swamijī, I fully understood his accent. “I already knew him,” was my initial reaction to his presence. Seeing him was like meeting someone familiar, like a grandfather.

## Serving the Devotees

I was not able to concentrate on Swamijī’s words, and I remember nothing of his lecture. Yet I glimpsed his great power. For the weekend, I lived in the temple, slept on the floor, and awoke at 5:30 A.M. with the other devotees. The devotees engaged me in exotic but odd tasks, like writing out longhand the *Easy Journey to Other Planets* manuscript so it could be edited, getting gesso and other art supplies for Jadurāṇī, and searching the East Village gourmet food stores for Tiger’s brand milk for Swamijī. The errand to purchase milk for Swamijī especially filled me with happiness. I chanted as I walked through St. Mark’s Place and felt a deep, previously unexperienced, joy.

## A First Feast

That Sunday, Swamijī held a feast, an initiation, and an installation of Jagannātha Deities. I sat in the temple room and chanted with the devotees. It was the first time I had ever heard a *mṛdaṅga.* As the *kīrtana* continued, Robert, one of the devotees to be initiated, carried in a huge Jagannātha Deity and placed Him on the altar. I was shocked. First African drum beats and now a totem! What kind of group was this? But as I looked at Jagannātha’s smiling face, I relaxed and smiled with Him.

Swamijī initiated three new devotees: Bob became Vāmanadeva dāsa, Jay became Jayādvaita dāsa, and a second Jay became Jaya Nārāyaṇa dāsa. In addition, Haṁsadūta and his wife Himavatī sat in at the fire ceremony to consecrate their marriage. Swamijī both performed the ceremonies and lectured.

During his lecture, Swamijī explained that he was a *sannyāsī,* a renunciate, and traditionally should have nothing to do with marriages and marriage ceremonies, but he was duty-bound to perform his disciples’ weddings because they were completely dependent on him.

\* \* \*

I observed these esoteric, culturally un-American events taking place on Second Avenue with amazement. I felt simultaneously aloof, as one feels when watching a documentary movie about a distant land, and intimate, for I had already tasted results from chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. I imagined myself sitting shaven-headed and cross-legged before the sacrificial fire and receiving initiation from Swamijī. But I knew I wasn’t ready; I needed something more.

Jaya Nārāyaṇa, as it turned out, was writing a school paper on Swamijī and the Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees. His initiation experience was part of his paper. The following morning, his purpose fulfilled, he disappeared onto Second Avenue and was never heard from again. Jayādvaita dāsa Brahmacārī and Vāmanadeva dāsa fared much better.

## Transcendence and Goodness

Swamijī’s lecture continued. He spoke about Prahlāda Mahārāja and told the story of the boy devotee who was harassed by his atheistic father and later saved by Lord Nṛsiṁha-deva. At the end of his lecture, I raised my hand and asked Swamijī my first question. It was not submissive. “In your class, you mentioned that devotees were in the mode of goodness. I thought that devotees were transcendental to the mode of goodness.” Prabhupāda’s answer was to the point: “Yes, devotees are transcendental. But they generally act in the mode of goodness.”

\* \* \*

I had only been chanting for several months and neither understood the meaning of “transcendental” nor “the mode of goodness.” Yet I already thought myself learned and therefore offered my understanding to Prabhupāda. His straightforward answer satisfied me. Later, I understood that surrender places a devotee under Kṛṣṇa, not under the modes of nature, but that does not allow him to act whimsically in the name of being transcendental. Rather, when not specifically requested by Kṛṣṇa to act otherwise, pure devotees naturally act in goodness. Thus, rather than rejecting the mode of goodness as an impure product of *māyā,* devotees embrace the mode of goodness as the platform from which to step off to transcendence.

## Flowers Into Water

After the lecture, initiation, marriage, fire ceremony, and Sunday feast, I was asked to carry and dispose of the holy flower remnants inthe East River. Although I didn’t like the prospect of walking through the slums to the river, I accepted the service.

Before throwing the flower remnants into the murky water, I offered my obeisances on the nearby pavement. I threw the flowers in and watched them ebb and flow in the water. Then I returned, chanting the entire time, to 26 Second Avenue. Again I felt joy in chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and depending on Him.

## To Bow or Not to Bow?

The following morning, as Jim and I were about to begin our trip home, Brahmānanda asked us if we would like to accompany Swamijī on his morning walk. We both agreed.

We were told to wait for Swamijī outside the storefront. Suddenly, the vestibule door opened and Swamijī, effulgent and bundled in warm clothing for the morning coolness, stepped onto the dull and dirty, glass-speckled sidewalk. Swamijī looked me in the eye and I bowed to offer my obeisances. I perceived in Swamijī’s glance his desire that I not bow down on the filthy New York street, but I wasn’t bold enough to heed my perception.

## To Stop or Not to Stop?

The four of us—Swamijī, Gaurasundara, Jim, and I—headed silently uptown on Second Avenue. The streets were nearly deserted. Swamijī walked briskly along Second Avenue, not heeding the red lights, but simply glancing left then right before crossing.

\* \* \*

I had been curious to see how Swamijī would deal with the street lights: would he follow the letter of the law and “stop on red,” or be practical and cross as traffic permitted? Without a moment’s hesitation, Swamijī chose the practical course. I was satisfied to see this and understood that Kṛṣṇa consciousness is practical.

## Democrats and Republicans

Our walk continued in silence toward Eighth Street, St. Mark’s Place. Swamijī then headed east several blocks to Avenue B, turned into Tompkins Square Park, and began walking the circular walkway opposite St. Bridget’s Church. After completing the round several times, Swamijī headed back toward Second Avenue, pulling his three young followers in his wake. On Second Avenue, close to the temple, Swamijī chose to break the silence. Turning across the avenue toward a wall lined with Democratic Party posters for the upcoming 1968 presidential elections, Swamijī stopped, pointed his cane, and looked at us, “What are these Democrats and Republicans?”

My mental reaction was typical of the ‘60s: “There’s no difference between these parties. They are both cheaters.” But I remained silent, unsure of how to answer. Gaurasundara began explaining the difference between the parties based on their stance on the Vietnam War. I remember feeling foolish as I realized I was twenty-one years old and had no idea as to the difference, even on a superficial level, between the Democrats and the Republicans. Years later I understood that feeling a fool before one’s spiritual master is appropriate and that my spiritual master was personally instructing me, even during this initial morning walk.

## Back to Buffalo

Swamijī resumed his silence for the rest of the walk, and we arrived back at 26 Second Avenue.

Jim and I stood and watched as Gaurasundara opened the door. This allowed both Swamijī and him to pass through the anteroom and through the courtyard to Swamijī’s second story apartment. Nothing of external significance had occurred during the walk. Swamijī had uttered only six words the entire time, but by the time we got back to 26 Second Avenue, I had decided to become his disciple.

Jim and I offered our obeisances, went in and ate our breakfast of “heavenly porridge,” picked up our bags, and left for Buffalo.

## Montreal, June—1968

Montreal, June—1968

## A Matter of Choice

I had been chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa since January, and in April 1968 I was the first to move into our newly acquired Buffalo temple. The temple, ISKCON’s seventh, was a rickety, white, two-story house on La Salle Street, near the university. When university classes were just about to end in April, I received a letter from a girlfriend suggesting we go to Cape Cod together for the summer. I informed Rūpānuga that I would be leaving for Cape Cod with my girlfriend, but that I would be back in September. Rūpānuga sat me down, looked me in the eye, and told me that it was time for me to make my choice: did I want to become a devotee or not? I quickly decided to stay with the devotees and I wrote my friend explaining that I was unable to accompany her.

Later that summer, Rūpānuga approached me as I pulled weeds in the temple garden. Swamijī was now in Montreal and was holding an initiation ceremony on Sunday. Rūpānuga asked if I wanted to become initiated. My reply was that I would think about it.

At that time, I had no idea of the commitment involved in accepting a spiritual master. As I pulled weeds, a voice inside advised that initiation would help me continue chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and stop me from leaving Kṛṣṇa consciousness. After five minutes of deliberation, I informed Rūpānuga that I would like to take initiation.

I had a few dollars to put toward my bus fare. Rūpānuga gave ten more, a few friends chipped in a bit, and the remainder came from the temple’s collection box. I was ready to go.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda had been unable to obtain permanent residency status while physically residing in the U.S., so he flew to Montreal, Canada, where his disciples had recently established a temple. As soon as Prabhupāda arrived, devotees began visiting him, and, as several aspiring disciples were ready for their vows, Prabhupāda decided to hold an initiation ceremony. Brahmānanda, acting as Prabhupāda’s secretary, telephoned Rūpānuga from Montreal and asked whether or not I was ready for initiation. Rūpānuga had passed on his question to me.

## Good Advice for Avenue du Parc

Although Kīrtanānanda originally opened the Montreal temple, it was now being managed by Haṁsadūta and Himavatī. The temple was in an old, second-story bowling alley on Avenue du Parc, and by the time I arrived, most of the bowling lanes had been removed and the wood reused to construct Prabhupāda’s *vyāsāsana,* an altar, and room dividers to separate the hall into several make-shift rooms. The temple room was quite large. Its walls were decorated with Brijbasi prints imported from India and purchased on Saint Mark’s Place in New York’s East Village. There were also paintings of Viṣṇu, Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa, Pañca-Tattva, and Lord Caitanya’s *saṅkīrtana*—all done by New York ISKCON’s art department. A large, blue banner with the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* sewn in gold letters hung prominently. Several devotees—Brahmānanda, Śivānanda, Puruṣottama, Harināma, and others—had already arrived to visit Swamijī.

I remember seeing Pradyumna standing before a mirror, meticulously applying *tilaka* and then exclaiming softly to himself, *“Vaiṣṇava*!” Jaya Patāka would be the first awake at 4:30 am. and he would leave the others asleep until 6:00. Gopāla Kṛṣṇa (then Gopal), would come straight from work every day, still wearing his business suit, and offer respects by bowing down to each picture in the temple room.

Swamijī stayed in an apartment a few blocks from Avenue du Parc. Someone asked me if I would be willing to wash Swamijī’s clothes. I immediately agreed. Each day I walked to his apartment, took his saffron *dhotī, kurtā,* and top-piece, and hand-washed them in the bathtub.

\* \* \*

Rūpānuga had offered me good advice before leaving: “Take every opportunity to personally serve Swamijī.” I was ready, therefore, and when the chance came, I grabbed it.

## Swamijī and Prabhupāda

One day, Puruṣottama, Prabhupāda’s secretary, took me aside and told me that “Swamijī” was not a first-class name. He said that “Gurudeva” was third-class, “Swamijī” was second-class, and “Prabhupāda” was first-class. Before long, we were all calling Swamijī “Prabhupāda.” Although it took some getting used to, “Prabhupāda” soon began to sound correct.

\* \* \*

I wasn’t sure where Puruṣottama received his information, but Swamijī accepted our calling him “Prabhupāda.” Within a few months, an article appeared in *Back to Godhead* magazine explaining the meaning of Prabhupāda as “at whose feet all masters sit,” and explaining that Swamijī had graciously accepted our gesture of affection by allowing us to address him by that title.

## Not Unto Kṛṣṇa

Prabhupāda spoke on *Bhagavad-gītā* in the Montreal temple every Monday, Wednesday, and Friday night. Tuesday and Thursday evenings were more intimate, with Prabhupāda meeting the devotees in his apartment. We sat on the floor before his low desk and Prabhupāda, dressed in a pink turtleneck shirt, was effulgent, peaceful, powerful, and tireless.

At one meeting, Prabhupāda read the last verse of Chapter Nine, *man-manā bhava mad-bhakto mad-yājī māṁ namaskuru:* “Always think of Me,” Kṛṣṇa says, “and become My devotee. Worship Me and offer Me your obeisances.” As he read, he suddenly appeared angry.

Prabhupāda’s own *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is* had not yet been published, so he was reading from Dr. Radhakrishnan’s edition. Prabhupāda said that Dr. Radhakrishnan had translated the verse properly, but his commentary was a Māyāvādī interpretation and stood in contrast to Kṛṣṇa’s own words in the verse. Dr. Radhakrishnan had commented that “It is not Kṛṣṇa to whom one must surrender,” but that Kṛṣṇa was directing Arjuna, and all living entities, to surrender to the “unborn, unmanifest within Kṛṣṇa.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda did not view the philosophical wrangling of Māyāvādīs, materialists, or atheists with intellectual distance and detachment. He deplored their philosophical “word-jugglery” because it misled innocent souls from the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa and tangibly damaged their spiritual lives. Strong criticism of opposing philosophies was more a deep personal concern for Prabhupāda than an intellectual exercise.

## The Perfect Teacher

While lecturing, Prabhupāda called for Pradyumna, who had begun studying Sanskrit, to locate a verse in the *Bhagavad-gītā* he carried for reference. “I think it is in Chapter Seven,” Prabhupāda said.

Pradyumna replied, “I think it’s in Chapter Four.”

Prabhupāda didn’t say anything, but when Pradyumna called out that he had found the verse, and after Prabhupāda had him read both the Sanskrit and the English translation, Prabhupāda asked softly, “What chapter was it in?”

“Seven,” Pradyumna sheepishly replied.

\* \* \*

Kṛṣṇa could have directly informed Indra that He was his master, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, but instead He chose to indirectly instruct Indra about His position by allowing him to witness the lifting of Govardhana Hill. Similarly, Prabhupāda wished to curb the scholar Pradyumna’s freshly blossoming pride by allowing him to search for the verse in the wrong chapter.

## Entering the Sacrificial Arena

One hour before the initiation ceremony, I was tying a *dhotī* around my waist for the first time. My fear that it would certainly fall off did not stop my self-conscious walk across the temple room, through the loud *kīrtana* of *mṛdaṅga* and *karatālas,* to offer my obeisances to Prabhupāda, who sat on the floor before an earth mound decorated with colored zigzag designs. The wood for the fire, a pot of ghee, and a ladle were by his side.

Brahmānanda tied the reddish wood neckbeads around our necks and devotees read from *śāstra* in Sanskrit or English from the four corners of the sacrificial arena.

## A Dog’s Good Quality

Prabhupāda lectured on the meaning of our neckbeads, which he likened to a collar signifying us as Kṛṣṇa’s dogs. “A dog,” Prabhupāda said, “has one good quality: he is loyal to his master.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda generally did not praise dogs, but he found their quality of loyalty ideal.

## The Temples of Vṛndāvana

Prabhupāda then spoke about Vṛndāvana. I had already heard of Vṛndāvana from the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* He said that the temples in Vṛndāvana that had been started by the Gosvāmīs were now in the hands of rascals who did not maintain them properly. He said, “We should get them back and care for them.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda, as a long-time resident of Vṛndāvana, had seen the poor standard of temple maintenance, and had even witnessed temple caretakers selling the Deity’s property. His instruction was to right the wrong of these improprieties.

## An Early Request for Relief from Management

“I am an old man,” Prabhupāda said, “and I can leave at any moment. You young men please take responsibility to manage this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement and push it throughout the world.”

\* \* \*

As early as 1968 Prabhupāda wanted relief from the management responsibilities of ISKCON. He wanted his disciples to take up that burden. The assurance that ISKCON would continue, even in those early days, was dependent on the sincerity and cooperation among Prabhupāda’s disciples to properly manage his mission.

## Becoming a Viṣṇujana

The lecture was over and Prabhupāda chanted on our beads before the fire, before the Vaiṣṇavas, and before our eyes. When he finished chanting on one devotee’s set of beads, he called that devotee forward and requested him to recite, and promise to follow, the four rules. He then showed, with his own hands, how to chant on the beads, teaching us to hold the beads between our thumb and second finger, to chant one *mantra* per bead, and to avoid chanting on the head bead. He then handed us our beads and gave us our spiritual names.

“Your name is Viṣṇujana dāsa,” Prabhupāda said to me as he handed me my beads.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda wore my beads around his neck as he chanted on them, and he held them in his hand before he placed them into my outstretched hands. When I received my beads from him they were still warm from his touch.

I retained the name Viṣṇujana for about an hour. A devotee then came to me with this message: “Prabhupāda said there’s already a Viṣṇujana. Your name is Bhūrijana.”

Three other *brahmacārīs* were initiated: Dayal Nitāi dāsa, Ananta dāsa, and Nayanābhirāma dāsa.

## Guru-dakṣiṇā

Prabhupāda performed the fire sacrifice, and when it was over, I noticed Nayanābhirāma offer Prabhupāda a delicately carved, silver drinking glass. A devotee then explained to me that a new initiate was required to offer a gift of appreciation to his spiritual master as *guru-dakṣiṇā.* It was the first time I had heard that information, and I was embarrassed because I had nothing to offer. I then thought of my $20 return bus fare and offered that money as *dakṣiṇā* at Prabhupāda’s lotus feet.

## Hearing the Name

On an ensuing evening, the devotees gathered in Prabhupāda’s apartment for *Bhagavad-gītā* class. At the end of the class, Prabhupāda dropped a piece of fruit into his mouth and distributed a piece of the remaining fruit to each devotee. One by one the devotees left, but I stayed behind, making it look as if I had not yet received my piece of fruit. Prabhupāda looked at me questioningly. I said, “I’m not sure of my name.”

“Bhoo-ree-jan.”

With my heart pounding, I quickly offered my obeisances and left.

\* \* \*

I had stayed behind because I wanted to hear my new name pronounced directly by my spiritual master. I received the impression, however, that Prabhupāda did not think my sentiment necessary.

## Of Worms and Māyā

Prabhupāda maintained his usual format of *kīrtana*-lecture-*kīrtana* during both morning and evening lectures in Montreal. He sang the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra* each morning before his lecture. Sometimes he also chanted *“govinda jaya jaya, gopāla jaya jaya, rādhā-ramaṇa hari, govinda jaya jaya.*” The devotees would dance to his sweet singing with their arms raised in the air.

At the end of his lectures, he usually called for questions. I asked two questions on that visit to Montreal. The first one was about worms and their particular characteristic of taking up two separate lives when cut in half. “How is it possible that one living entity can become two?” Prabhupāda’s answer was simple: a second spirit soul enters the cut-off worm.

The second question alluded to the philosophical fact that a spirit soul cannot avoid his constitutional position of service to Kṛṣṇa. He either serves directly in the spiritual world or indirectly through *māyā* in the material world. I asked Prabhupāda why we should bother endeavoring to serve Kṛṣṇa if we are already serving Him through His *māyā* energy. Prabhupāda replied that serving Kṛṣṇa directly of our own will brings the soul happiness, and that serving Kṛṣṇa indirectly and unwillingly through His *māyā* brings only misery.

\* \* \*

Although I had previously read it, I did not remember that Prabhupāda had already answered my question about direct and indirect service in his purport to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 1.3.38:

Only the pure devotees can know the transcendental name, form and activities of Lord Kṛṣṇa due to their being completely freed from the reactions of fruitive work and mental speculation. The pure devotees have nothing to derive as personal profit from their unalloyed service to the Lord. They render incessant service to the Lord spontaneously, without any reservation. Everyone within the creation of the Lord is rendering service to the Lord indirectly or directly. No one is an exception to this law of the Lord. Those who are rendering service indirectly, being forced by the illusory agent of the Lord, are rendering service unto Him unfavorably. But those who are rendering service untoHim directly under the direction of His beloved agents are rendering service unto Him favorably. Such favorable servitors are devotees of the Lord, and by the grace of the Lord they can enter into the mysterious region of transcendence by the mercy of theLord. But the mental speculators remain in darkness all the time. As stated in *Bhagavad-gītā,* the Lord Himself guides the pure devotees toward the path of realization due to their constant engagement in the loving service of the Lord in spontaneous affection. That is the secret of entering into the kingdom of God.

Neither of my questions were very philosophical, penetrating, or practical. They were simply the doubts of a struggling conditioned soul whose intelligence was grappling with apparent philosophical contradictions caused by his birth and education in Western culture. Prabhupāda answered both questions to my satisfaction.

## Humility

Once, as Prabhupāda sat on his *vyāsāsana* after class, an Indian gentleman dressed in suit and coat stood before him. He asked Prabhupāda which languages he knew. Prabhupāda replied Hindi, Bengali, and English.”Prabhupāda, you also speak Sanskrit,” I offered. Prabhupāda looked at me and replied, “No. I don’t know Sanskrit.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was a published author and scholar, but he was extremely humble about his accomplishments. Yet when required, he could be the proudest of warriors on behalf of his Guru Mahārāja and Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

## Back to Buffalo and a Letter

I went back to Buffalo after a week in Montreal. Since I had given Prabhupāda my return bus fare as *guru-dakṣiṇā,* I had to hitchhike back. A few weeks after my return, I wrote Prabhupāda a letter in which I asked over twenty questions. I wanted to know why Jesus said, “Father, why have You forsaken me?” at the crucifixion. I wanted to know what a devotee should do if he misses Ekādaśī. I asked whether the Supersoul was the same as the Holy Ghost of Catholicism and whether we should fast totally on Ekādaśīs or only from grains. And I asked why we observe Ekādaśī at all.

He answered some of the questions in a two-page letter, which I subsequently lost. He complimented me on my intelligence and admonished the senior devotees for being unable to satisfy my inquiries.

Prabhupāda said that we observe Ekādaśī because it is the eleventh day of the moon. The proper observance of Ekādaśī, he said, is to maximize chanting and reading. And, he said, it is better to completely fast, but added that if that was too difficult, then we should eat as little as was practical.

He mentioned that there is no such thing as a Holy Ghost—all ghosts are unholy—and that Ekādaśī should be observed on the following day if we accidentally miss it.

The letter also contained some esoteric points, but I no longer remember their context. Prabhupāda mentioned that rain falling on the head of a serpent during the *svāti* constellation would cause the appearance of a jewel on a serpent’s head and that the Sanskrit language is called *devanāgarī* because Sanskrit is spoken by the demigods.

Rūpānuga accepted Prabhupāda’s admonition that senior devotees should be able to answer such questions as a personal reprimand. Yet he and everyone else were excited about Bhūrijana’s letter from Prabhupāda, especially about the part concerning jewels on the heads of snakes. As was the standard in those early days, my letter was retyped and carbon copies were dutifully sent to each of the six other ISKCON centers.

## New York City, Late Summer—1968

New York City, Late Summer—1968

## Again in New York

In late August 1968, I took advantage of the school break to visit my parents in New York City. My grades had improved since I had become a devotee, and as my parents thought my newfound Kṛṣṇa consciousness was a passing fad, they reluctantly accepted my changed lifestyle. They didn’t like me burning incense in the house, and I kept my door closed to the unholy odors of their cooking. Outside of these inconveniences, they had nothing tangible to complain about.

I used to visit the 26 Second Avenue temple from my parent’s home, sometimes staying overnight. On one visit I met Mukunda, Gurudāsa, Śyāmasundara, and their wives. They had come from San Francisco via Montreal and were on their way to London to open ISKCON’s first center there.

During their visit we heard good news: Prabhupāda had been given permanent resident status by the American Immigration office and would soon be arriving in New York. We felt the expectancy and joy of children freshly informed that Christmas, with holidays and gifts, had just been rescheduled for the following week. The devotees painted over the “Matchless Gifts” signboard that still adorned the entrance to 26 Second Avenue, transforming it into “The International Society for Krishna Consciousness.” Prabhupāda’s imminent arrival inspired me to shave my head for the first time.

\* \* \*

A devotee’s personal inspiration and commitment to advancement in Kṛṣṇa consciousness is his wealth. Rūpānuga, the leader of our fledgling Buffalo temple, did not once express that my spending time with my parents would cause me to “bloop” or fall down. Rather, he trusted my inspiration to become a devotee and my own commitment to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Similarly, Prabhupāda never pushed me or forced me to shave my head. Rather, he inspired me to please him. Based on his inspiration, shaving my head followed naturally.

Yet Kṛṣṇa consciousness and surrendered service to our spiritual master is not limited to our inspiration or our being dealt with in a congenial or respectful way. We must also serve when we are not feeling inspired. An advanced disciple is prepared to serve his *guru* unhesitatingly, without personal consideration, regardless of any difficulties. Selfless service to the spiritual master is a prerequisite for attaining selfless service to Śrī Śrī Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.

## Prabhupāda’s Arrival in New York

Prabhupāda entered the 26 Second Avenue temple and lectured to the devotees who had gathered to greet him. He began by relating his travel experience in coming to New York—it had taken him one hour to fly from Montreal to New York, and then two hours to battle the traffic from New York’s JFK airport to the Lower East Side—and he mocked the so-called advancement of modern civilization.

\* \* \*

We all laughed along with Prabhupāda at the foolishness of the modern materialists, the “*karmīs.*” They were always making technological advances, but they could never foresee the repercussions that only caused greater inconvenience or even danger. Prabhupāda worked to detach us from our conditioned view of “advanced,” contemporary society.

## Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa and Prabhupāda in Brooklyn

Rādhāṣṭamī fell on a Sunday during Prabhupāda’s visit. The devotees planned to go with Prabhupāda to the Brooklyn Botanical Gardens and hold *kīrtana.* Indirā dāsī and her sister, Ekayani dāsī, were to be dressed up during the outing as Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa. “We should treat them just as we would Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa,” the temple president told us.

Prabhupāda arrived by car while most of the devotees rode the subway. (I wasn’t sure how “Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa” made their way, but later I found out that they too took the subway.) The overcast sky suddenly cleared as Prabhupāda arrived and illuminated the park with his presence.

The devotees sat around Prabhupāda, and Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa sat nearby on a blanket. Prabhupāda led the *kīrtana* and the devotees chanted and danced with their hands in the air.

I brought my 35-mm Kowa SLR camera, but I had no film. I was bubbling with anxiety. I really wanted to take pictures, so I left the *kīrtana* to scour the New York streets for a shop open on Sunday that sold film. I ran and ran, finally found the film, and rushed back. I had been gone half an hour and the *kīrtana* was going strong. I snapped some photos. One was included in the original *ISKCON Handbook*—Prabhupāda chanting with Indirā and Ekayani behind him dressed as Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa—published in 1969 by ISKCON Press.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda took part in the entire Rādhāṣṭamī festival without saying a word about “Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.” The devotees even offered them food and accepted their remnants as *mahā-prasādam.* Months later, I heard that Prabhupāda said it was not bona fide and that we should not do such things.

## Disciples Speak

After the *kīrtana,* Prabhupāda requested his senior disciples one by one to preach. Brahmānanda, Rūpānuga, Dāmodara, Jadurāṇī, and others stood and spoke.

\* \* \*

Only devotees were in listening range, so we understood that Prabhupāda was examining his students. I watched Rūpānuga stand and speak, his hands folded in sincerity, and I daydreamed what I would say if Prabhupāda called on me. I decided I would walk to the young black couple standing a hundred yards away, watching us curiously, and invite them to come and meet Prabhupāda. But Prabhupāda didn’t call on me. I wasn’t sure if I was relieved or disappointed.

## Prabhupāda at the Movies

One afternoon the following week, we learned that Prabhupāda would come down from his apartment and watch Dāmodara’s 8-mm movie of our Rādhāṣṭamī expedition. A single chair was placed in the temple room for Prabhupāda and a movie screen was set up. Prabhupāda sat and watched the uncut, unedited, silent movie. After smiling and enjoying the movie with his disciples, he went back upstairs.

\* \* \*

A parent smiles with pleasure at a child’s first attempts to walk or speak. Prabhupāda was similarly pleased at 26 Second Avenue. And as childish antics are no longer pleasing to a parent when the child has grown up because the parent expects more, similarly, Prabhupāda gradually expected more from his disciples.

Prabhupāda’s early pastimes at 26 Second Avenue had long ago ended, yet Prabhupāda still reciprocated easily with his disciples’ simple attempts to please him. His mood was congenial, and he accepted whatever the devotees offered. Gradually, however, as the movement gained strength and the devotees began to mature in their Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Prabhupāda pushed his disciples to advance, distribute more books, and to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness throughout the world.

Around this time, Prabhupāda also told his film-maker disciple, Dāmodara, to make a movie of the *Bhagavad-gītā.* He said if Dāmodara would make such a film, he would personally direct it.

## The Good Old Days of Kṛṣṇa Consciousness

Using whatever excuse I could think of, I managed to bypass two sets of guards and enter Prabhupāda’s 26 Second Avenue apartment while he was speaking with several devotees. Kīrtanānanda Swami, Hayagrīva, Umāpati, and Gaurasundara sat on the floor. Prabhupāda sat behind his low desk on a woolly, Nepali carpet.

**Umāpati:** This is just like the old days, Prabhupāda.

**Prabhupāda:** There are no old days in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s words made everyone laugh, but it was, in fact, a heavy statement. A disciple may enjoy the sweet and even transcendental remembrances of the past, but the increased organization, rules, and regulations that Prabhupāda places upon his disciples does not allow them to sit back and muse nostalgically about “the good old days.” The new days may not be entirely to our liking, but we should nevertheless somehow keep serving Prabhupāda.

Umāpati Swami remembers this same incident with one addition: Prabhupāda added, “We are all eternal” to his comment.

## Dr. Frog, R.I.P.

On Prabhupāda’s request, Gaurasundara brought out an eleven-by-sixteen-inch card on which he was inking the story “Dr. Frog.” Everyone present, including Prabhupāda, laughed at Gaurasundara’s comic rendition of Dr. Frog attempting to estimate the size of the ocean from his well by puffing out his chest: “Do you mean the ocean is three times the size of my well?”

\* \* \*

A standard piece of ISKCON jargon is “puffed up.” Its derivation can be traced to a frog puffing himself up inside his well and finally bursting. We all laughed at Dr. Frog’s antics, but our tendency to be “puffed up” did not burst with the demise of Dr. Frog, Ph.D.

Gaurasundara’s comic strip was later printed in *Back to Godhead* magazine.

## Folk Wisdom from Prabhupāda

During this meeting Prabhupāda mentioned two gems of folk wisdom, both of which I heard him repeat:

1\. If something is auspicious, do it immediately. If something is inauspicious, put it off.  
2\. Something is better than nothing.

\* \* \*

Following the first gem guarantees that we won’t miss opportunities to better ourselves or to help others. A second point of this gem applies when circumstances force us to act inauspiciously. When such a situation arises, we should delay as long as possible, hoping for a favorable change. The second gem teaches that even a slight gain is better than no gain at all. I have found both sayings helpful in both material and spiritual dealings.

## Medium Cows

While Prabhupāda was in Montreal, Kīrtanānanda and Hayagrīva, after having left ISKCON for some time, returned and offered him their newly acquired land in West Virginia. Prabhupāda named it New Vrindaban. Now Prabhupāda began advising them how to develop it.

**Kīrtanānanda:** What kind of cows should we get? Should we get the best quality cows? Or cows of the lowest quality?  
**Prabhupāda:** Medium.

\* \* \*

The devotees were inexperienced in tilling land and caring for cows. Prabhupāda therefore advised a middle of the road approach. A few months later, Prabhupāda visited New Vrindaban and gave further instructions on how to develop the land. He wanted New Vrindaban centered around worshiping Kṛṣṇa in seven major temples, caring for cows, and farming—simple living and high thinking.

## A Personal Instruction for Happiness

Our *darśana* ended and the devotees offered their obeisances and left. I remained behind because I wanted to say something privately to Prabhupāda. When all the devotees had gone and I too was near the door, I turned and asked Prabhupāda how I could serve him. I longed for a personal instruction.

“Take this Kṛṣṇa consciousness and be happy. *Jaya.*”

\* \* \*

The word *“jaya*” seemed to flow from Prabhupāda’s mouth slowly, like thick honey. I have added this instruction to others Prabhupāda gave me that I have yet to fulfill. I therefore beg the Vaiṣṇavas to please bless me that I can one day fulfill all of Prabhupāda’s instructions, including this one.

Soon after this meeting with Prabhupāda in his 26 Second Avenue apartment, I returned to Buffalo for my final university semester. Prabhupāda also left New York, stopping briefly in Santa Fe, and then traveling onward to preach in Seattle.

## Buffalo to Chapel Hill, 1968—69

Buffalo to Chapel Hill, 1968—69

## Rūpānuga’s Empowered Preaching

I lived in the temple and attended classes during the final semester of my senior year at the University of Buffalo. Every evening after studying in the university library, I would go home to the temple and be greeted by Rūpānuga, his wife, their five-year-old son, and the other students or friends who had joined. *Prasādam,* especially the hot *dāl,* always tasted incredible after a day at school. Later, we would all gather around the big gas stove outside the temple room and savor “banana nectar”—milk with mashed bananas, spiced with cinnamon, cloves, coriander, and vanilla beans sizzled in ghee before being added. We *brahmacārīs* would then carry our sleeping bags into the temple room, crawl in, and fall asleep.

\* \* \*

Rūpānuga’s Buffalo preaching success was unique and outstanding. Our La Salle St. temple was lively, with university students dropping by for morning *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* classes, thrice-weekly evening *Bhagavad-gītā* classes, and our Sunday feast. (It was my service to telephone all the people listed in our guestbook and invite them to the feast.) On Tuesdays and Thursdays we held a Bhakti-Yoga Class at the student union.

Rūpānuga continued his program for years. By the time I left Buffalo, Trivikrama, Jagadīśa, and Prahlādānanda had joined. Rūpānuga’s accredited course on the *Bhagavad-gītā* attracted Bhagavān and Kṛṣṇa Bhāmini dāsī to join. Guṇagrāhi and Kuśakratha soon followed, and Romapāda had already received his first BTG. Ayodhyapati, Lakṣmimoni dāsī, Narottamānanda, Nityānanda, Tejīyas, Madira dāsī, Satyavrata, Muktakeśa, Lokavarṇottama, and many others joined the Buffalo temple after I left. In addition, two other successful temples—Detroit and Toronto—were the direct result of Rūpānuga’s Buffalo preaching success.

The mood in Buffalo, and throughout the movement at this time, was to follow Prabhupāda’s fearless preaching spirit. Prabhupāda had directly inspired us by his example and words. He wrote to Brahmānanda in 1967:

I have read your statements regarding opening centers. I am not in agreement with Mr. Altman that we are expanding very thinly. In my opinion, a single sincere soul can maintain a center. You know I started the center at 26 Second Avenue alone. I took the risk of 200.00 dollars per month for the rent. At that time there were no assistants. Mukunda was at that time a friend, but there was no responsibility for him for maintaining the center. Gradually Kirtanananda and Hayagriva joined, but they did not take any responsibility. Still I was maintaining the establishment simply depending on Krishna and then Krishna sent me everything—men and money. Similarly, if a sincere soul goes out and opens a center in any part of the world, Krishna will help him in all respects. Without being empowered by Krishna, nobody can preach Krishna consciousness. It is not academic qualification or financial strength that helps in these matters, but it is sincerity of purpose which helps us always. Therefore, I wish that you will remain in charge of New York, let Satsvarupa be in charge of Boston, let Mukunda be in charge of San Francisco, let Janardana be in charge of Montreal. Let Nandarani and Dayananda be in charge of Los Angeles, and let Subala das be in charge of Santa Fe. In this way you will follow my example as I did in the beginning at 26 Second Ave. That is preaching, cooking, writing, talking, chanting, everything one man’s show. I never thought about the audience. I was prepared to chant if there was no man to hear me. The principle of chanting is to glorify the Lord and not to attract a crowd. If Krishna hears nicely then He will ask some sincere devotees to gather in such place. Therefore, be advised that thousands of centers may be started if we find out a sincere soul for each and every center. We do not require more men to start. If there is one sincere soul that is sufficient to start a new center. … Let us remain sincere to Krishna and His bona fide representative and we are sure to carry out our mission successfully. Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

## A Kīrtana for Prabhupāda’s Pleasure

Kṛṣṇa consciousness was thriving in Buffalo. Trivikrama, Jagadīśa, and I took jobs as taxi drivers to support the temple. We would often drive all night, when there was less traffic and most of the fares were long distance rides. Trivikrama made friends withMr. Montana, the owner and radio dispatcher, and we would all say “Haribol” to him on the air and hear his broadcasted reply, “Haribol.” Hearing “Haribol” again and again on the radio encouraged us, and we’d talk about spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness by setting up our own taxi company complete with saffron cabs.

Attending university classes also took on a preaching dimension and many students wanted to know about “the far out thing I was into” that had caused me to change my habits, give up listening to rock music, and shave my head. By the time I graduated, the Buffalo temple was well-established.

We Buffalo devotees taped one of our *kīrtanas* and sent it to Prabhupāda with a letter. The *mṛdaṅga* and *karatālas* were soft and the melody sweet. The devotee men sang first as a group and the women responded. The *kīrtana* began slow, sped up, and again slowed. A guitar accompanied us. Prabhupāda replied to my letter:

My Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of January 13, 1969 along with the kirtan tapes and pictures of the Buffalo temple. I cannot tell you how very much I enjoyed listening to this wonderful tape recording. All of the super excellent qualities of kirtan were present on this tape and it was thus a great joy to hear it. On this tape Rupanuga has set an example for all house holders because there was singing on this of Hare Krishna by all of his family members. It was all sounding very nicely, and I am going to show this tape to the Sankirtana Party here in Los Angeles so they may take example from such nice kirtan.  
I am very pleased that you are graduating from college in a few days and are anxious to engage your labors in spreading this Krishna consciousness movement.Also, I request you to study carefully the Bhagavad-gita As It Is because there is so much important service that an intelligent boy such as yourself can do in preaching work and spreading our movement around the world. You have a very good example from your Godbrother, Rupanuga, and so continue to help him as you are doing, and it shall be very successful for you.  
So thank you again for your nice letter along with the tape. I hope this meets you in very good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda liked our *kīrtana* tape! “All of the super excellent qualities of *kīrtana* were present.” As Prabhupāda felt joy listening to our *kīrtana,* we felt joy in Prabhupāda’s pleasure. Prabhupāda glorified Rūpānuga for spearheading the Buffalo preaching and for engaging his wife and son in chanting the *mahā-mantra.*

Prabhupāda also encouraged me to use my energy in preaching, and he told me to purify my intelligence by studying *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is.* This is our most basic preaching book, and even now, twenty-six years later, I study and teach *Bhagavad-gītā.*

## From Student to Student-teacher

I appeared for my graduation ceremony wearing cap, gown, and *tilaka.* My school performance had drastically improved after becoming a devotee, and I had made the Dean’s honor list for both of my final semesters. My parents could not help being satisfied.

Just after graduation, I heard that Patita-Uddharana’s mother had offered ISKCON the use of her home for three months to get the preaching started in Chapel Hill, North Carolina. With Rūpānuga’s permission, I went to open ISKCON’s thirteenth center. I informed Prabhupāda of the plan in a letter and he replied as follows:

My Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated February 3, 1969, and I’m so pleased and encouraged to note the contents. Your idea of going to North Carolina is excellent because there are so many students there, and you are a college graduate, so I think that you are just the devotee to do the job nicely. The only thing is that I do not think it is necessary for you to go there alone, so immediately you may contact Tosana Krishna in Santa Fe temple, and he is also willing to go there to be your assistant. If the two of you find the place suitable, then for some time myself and Purusottama will go there to make the center fortified. So you may write immediately to Tosana Krishna in this connection, and the both of you may start on this important new endeavor.  
Regarding the seeds which you have sent, yes, they are genuine tulasi seeds.  
Thank you again for your most encouraging letter, and I shall be awaiting further news from you in this matter.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

We had sent Prabhupāda some seeds to make sure they were actually *tulasī.* We asked Prabhupāda about almost everything because we knew nothing. Prabhupāda was always willing to respond to our appeals.

## Prabhupāda’s Simple Preaching Success Formula

I flew to North Carolina within a few weeks of my graduation, six months after initiation, and one year after first contacting the devotees. Due to Prabhupāda’s fanning efforts, he had the movement’s preaching spirit ablaze. The devotees, thinking that the entire world would soon be Kṛṣṇa conscious, were revolutionary missionaries in Prabhupāda’s service. Just after I arrived in Chapel Hill, Prabhupāda wrote me:

My Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I am very happy to be in receipt of your nice letter of March 6, 1969, and thank you very much for it. Yes, you are two ideal ones for preaching there, and I can understand that things are going very nicely. This is very much encouraging to me. Hold kirtanas and serve prasadam, and gradually people will come, more and more. That is the program. You are both sincere students and very intelligent boys, and Krishna will supply you all facility for your preaching work if you try sincerely.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda gave Toṣaṇa Kṛṣṇa and me the simple formula for preaching Kṛṣṇa consciousness: *kīrtana* and *prasādam.* After preaching for two months from Patita-Uddharana’s mother’s house, we rented a house closer to the University of North Carolina. We would stand every day on Main Street in Chapel Hill, chant Hare Kṛṣṇa, and distribute sweets. We supported our preaching by working as house painters, construction workers, bricklayer’s assistants, and other odd jobs. We also formed a university-sponsored Bhakti-Yoga Club and held a weekly (and popular) Saturday night feast. Toṣaṇa Kṛṣṇa silk-screened some posters and we hung them around the university. Our preaching based on Prabhupāda’s instructions expanded.

## Māyā Strikes Back

But soon disaster struck. It was the 1960s and the Vietnam War was raging. I had just graduated from university and was therefore now eligible for the draft. When the letter came from the United States Government—”Greetings”—I was ordered to appear for a pre-induction physical examination. I immediately wrote Prabhupāda and he replied quickly:

My Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. You will be pleased to know that one devotee, Karttikeya, has already received his 4-D ministerial status from the draft, and I am enclosing herewith carbon copy of the letter I have sent to your draft board. So we shall save you from the draft. If you stick to the study of our books and continue as a preacher, there will be no difficulty for you to get 4-D classification.

## Kṛṣṇa and the Draft Board

My local draft board was in old-fashioned Durham, North Carolina, home of Pall Mall, Duke of Durham, and Raleigh cigarettes. The entire town reeked of tobacco. Prabhupāda’s letter requesting that I be given ministerial exemption from the draft was not accepted by the conservative local draft board, but I had my own plan how Kṛṣṇa would save me from the draft. Many of my uncles and cousins were doctors and they provided me with letters explaining my myriad of illnesses, including a chronic cough, bronchitis, and asthma. My feet were flat, my eyesight poor, and one of my legs was shorter than the other. I was also anemic.

They read my letters when I went for the physical, but the doctors didn’t seem impressed. I began to get nervous. They saw my short-cropped hair, neckbeads, and chanting beads. After thrusting a needle into my arm and pulling out some blood they said, “Blood count sufficient.” They considered my legs, lungs, feet, and eyes. Not great, but good enough. The other young, jockey-short-clad draftees were marching through the array of doctors and passing the medical examination. My anxiety increased. I had no more letters left, nothing to help me. Bhūrijana dāsa, Hare Kṛṣṇa devotee, in the army! Shipped off to Vietnam!

They weighed me, measured me, peeped into my mouth to check my teeth. “This is ridiculous,” I thought. “I’m passing the physical. Is Kṛṣṇa going to help me?” The chief doctor, standing with a military man on his right, read through my file. He tapped the purple stamp pad and raised the official stamp of approval in his hand. The words, “You passed, son,” were about to escape his mustached mouth. “Kṛṣṇa, help!” I was screaming internally. Thinking that fortune favors the bold, I began to rant and rave. “You can’t take me! I’m a Hare Kṛṣṇa! I’m not fit for the army! I’m a vegetarian! It’s against my religion! I demand to see a psychiatrist! I demand to see a psychiatrist! I demand to see a psychiatrist! You’re not taking meeeee!”

The doctor stared at me and ordered me to step out of line. “I demand to see a psychiatrist.” Reluctantly, he agreed and my appointment was set for the following Tuesday.

On Tuesday, I did not go alone. I shaved my head and wore my robes. The army corporals acting as orderlies outside the psychiatrist’s office were impressed. They smiled as I walked through. I also smiled. I carried Lord Jagannātha, Baladeva, and Subhadrā in my bag.

The psychiatrist was balding and serious over his glasses. He sat in a swivel chair at a desk. When I walked into the room, he motioned toward a couch and suggested I lie down and relax while he asked me some questions.

“No, I will sit on the floor,” I said, plopping myself down on the carpet. His eyes widened as I pulled a brocade cloth from my bag and carefully set it out before me on the carpet. By the time I had extracted my small carved deities of Jagannātha, Subhadrā, and Balarāma, I had captured his full attention. I pulled out a pair of *karatālas* and started singing:

nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale  
śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine

namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe  
nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe

śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu nityānanda  
śrī-advaita gadādhara śrīvāsādi-gaura-bhakta-vṛnda

hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare  
hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare

When I thought the doctor had benefited sufficiently from the *mahā-mantra,* I sang, *jagannātha swami nayana pathagame bhavatu me.* Then I put away the *karatālas* and pulled out my *japa* beads Sitting before the deities, back straight, eyes closed, I chanted loudly on my beads: *hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa, kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare/ hare rāma hare rāma, rāma rāma hare hare.*

The psychiatrist leaned forward. He rubbed the first and second fingers of his left hand together as if fingering invisible beads. “I see you are chanting on beads. You know that when you go into the army—and they are going to take you into the army—they are going to take away your beads.” Then he repeated it again, for emphasis, “You know they will take away your beads.”

This was my cue: “No! They will not take away my beads.” I spoke strongly, keeping my voice loud, flat, and mechanical. Then I repeated, “They will not take away my beads.”

The psychiatrist studied my face for a few moments. Pointing to Jagannātha, Baladeva, and Subhadrā, he inquired about their identity. “That is God,” I replied. The psychiatrist squinted, as if trying to scan my mind. I put my hand back in my bead bag and softly chanted *japa.* He stared at me for another two minutes, jotted some notes on the papers on his desk, and said finally,”All right, you can go. Take a seat outside.”

The orderlies were still smiling. They were clearly on my side. A bell rang and one uniformed orderly raced into the psychiatrist’s office. He quickly returned with some papers and waved me over. He whispered, “He’s written that there’s nothing wrong with you, but that you’re not suitable for the army.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa saved me from the draft!

## Drops of Written Nectar in Chapel Hill

During my stay in Chapel Hill, I received several instructive letters from Prabhupāda. Here are some excerpts:

Observing Gaura-Pūrṇimā

Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. Regarding Lord Caitanya’s birth date, it is March 4, and you may celebrate it as elaborately as is possible for you. Immediately you should contact Brahmananda in New York, and ask him to send you a few chapters of Teachings of Lord Caitanya which you will return afterwards. So on this day you may read from these chapters. To celebrate you should have a picture of Lord Caitanya on the altar and decorate Him with flowers very nicely. Chanting and lecturing may go on. In these lectures you should explain very carefully Lord Caitanya’s teachings that we are eternal servitors of Krishna, and it is not that we are ourselves the Supreme Lord.

\* \* \*

*The Teachings of Lord Caitanya* had not yet been published. The simple Gaura-Pūrṇimā ceremony as recommended by Prabhupāda was complete and fully satisfying.

Remaining brahmacārī

Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I am pleased to note that you have decided to remain as brahmacari because it will be the most “undiverting” for your Krishna consciousness. Actually this is correct. If one is able to remain as brahmacari, that is first class because so much botheration of household life is immediately avoided. But Krishna consciousness is not limited that one must be brahmacari to execute, or one must be sannyasi to execute. Spiritual means that there is no conditions whatever, so in any position one may be in, he can chant Hare Krishna, preach Krishna consciousness, and pray to Krishna to engage him more and more in His transcendental loving service.

\* \* \*

When I decided to remain *brahmacārī,* I was twenty-two years old. Prabhupāda, however, as the expert and experienced teacher, somehow extracted a pure motive from my immature decision to remain undiverted in my Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He also accurately understood that I was too young to make such a lifelong decision. Therefore, he encouraged me by explaining the benefits of *brahmacārī* life, but he also carefully explained that Kṛṣṇa consciousness is fully transcendental and not limited by time, place, circumstance, or *āśrama.*

Questions about Jagannātha masks and lotus feet

Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. Regarding the Jagannatha mask, it is not bona fide. The footprint of Krishna is all right. You can keep It by the throne and while taking care of the Deities, you also wash the Foot, wipe It, and put It back. Generally this is done with a little sandalwood pulp. There are markings on the chest and feet of the Personality of Godhead. That is the distinction between Him and ordinary living entities. Just like in our palms there are certain marks for a particular person indicating his fame and fortune. Similarly the Supreme Personality of Godhead has got certain signs on His Lotus Feet which are unique for Him only, and they are not to be found in ordinary living entities. In Vaikuntha all the inhabitants have exactly the same bodily features as Narayana, but Narayana is made distinct by the marks on His chest and lotus feet. The Goddess of Fortune recognizes Her husband by these symptoms. That is the version of devotees.

\* \* \*

We had received the masks and the foot impression from a young American traveler who had recently returned from India. Prabhupāda’s discrimination was our only insurance to avoid mistakes while worshiping Kṛṣṇa.

## An Adventure at Woodstock

Toṣaṇa Kṛṣṇa, Vṛndāvana Candra (who had recently come to assist us), and I heard about Woodstock, the upcoming giant music festival and decided to attend and preach.

We hitchhiked from North Carolina to New York City and then on to Woodstock. We had no idea what to expect when we arrived.

Tens of thousands of young and hip citizens of East Coast America had converged on Woodstock’s barren farm fields. Although tickets were sold for the event, no one was prepared for the size of the turnout. The promoters gave up demanding payment or checking tickets. They were moved by the spirit of the ‘60s and they opened the gates to all. Throngs of young men and women channeled through and became part of the “happening.” This was *the* event and they were part of it. Countless gaily dressed hippie pilgrims milled around and danced, or sat absorbed in the intoxications of the moment and the music from the main stage.

They had traveled on Kali’s call to Woodstock. Their mood was accepting, so they accepted us with our shaved heads and Indian dress. We held *kīrtana* in the evenings and many joined in the chanting. During the day, we handed out incense and peacock feathers and asked for donations. We also looked for opportunities to preach.

One evening I attempted to chant on the main stage by disguising myself as a follower of Swami Satcidananda, the bearded and trendy Uptown New York *haṭha-yoga guru.* As he and his followers marched up the ramparts to the stage, I followed behind waving my bundle of peacock feathers up and down, looking like the final member of their entourage. But my attempt was foiled at the last moment by a *yoga* student, and I was ordered off the stage. (Years later, I met Swami Satcidananda in Hong Kong and he showed a movie of his exploits in the West. The screen filled with a shot of the Swami and his students walking onto the Woodstock stage. Behind his head, I could clearly see my bundle of peacock feathers waving up and down. “Hey, that’s me,” I proudly told him.)

We were determined to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa onstage. On the final evening, we were given permission to chant side-stage at the “Hog Farm.” The weather was threatening and it was already dark. The rock group’s electric guitars were screaming out heavy metallic blues notes. The lead singer called to the crowds, “You are moving the sun! You are moving the moon!” The blues notes bent, the bass guitars thumped, the drums thundered, and the crowds went wild in the dimly lit, cloud-covered night. Dancing with abandon, eyes turned upward, with and without partners, the audience clapped and approved of the band’s reminder that mankind is God.

We were the next act. In that moment of demonic insanity, we walked onstage with *karatālas* and our simple message. “Chant Hare Kṛṣṇa and be happy. We’d like you all to chant with us.” Our chant began and a few from the audience joined in.

Suddenly, we heard loud, antagonistic cries, “Shut up! Stop chanting!” The fever of the protests increased but we kept chanting. “Shut up! Stop that chanting! Rock is our soul!” We heard and recognized the black leather jackets of the “Mother-F̮̮̮̮̮̮,” a New York City motorcycle gang, as the leaders of the dissent. We kept chanting and they stepped up their protest. We chanted faster. We saw gang members standing up and moving toward the stage. “How can we stop chanting,” I wondered, “but how can we continue?”

*“Hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa, kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare,*” we chanted into the microphone. Our eyes scanned the dark toward the crowd; our ears weighed the sound of responding chanters against the calls of angry protesters. Suddenly, just as the tension began to peak, the electrical generator exploded and the power was cut off. Darkness fell like a stage curtain. Our act was over.

That night a heavy rain fell, halving the Woodstock fever. The crowds were drenched and the festival began to wind down. We three tired, aspiring servants of Prabhupāda, happy, but sick from the damp, began our return journey to New York City late the following morning.

Part of our gain from our weekend’s preaching was a trunk full of small coins. When we arrived at our 61 Second Avenue temple in New York, we discovered that we had collected almost $600! ISKCON was poor in those days and we were astounded at the huge size of our three-day collection. We decided to send part of the collection to Prabhupāda.

Our letter offering Prabhupāda $200 was sincere, but not in touch with the actual worth of $200. It began with flowery pomp: “My dear Srila Prabhupada, Please accept our humble obeisances and this donation of $200 for your book fund. Please use this money to publish *The Nectar of Devotion* and thus save all the university students throughout the land.”

Prabhupāda, as his reply showed, accepted the spirit of our donation and overlooked its amount:

My Dear Bhurijana, Tosana Krishna and Vrindavana Candra,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you for your sending me the check for $200 for my book fund, and I think of it as a big contribution of $2,000,000. I have come to your country to carry out the wishes of my spiritual master, and you are kindly cooperating with me. Certainly Krishna will be pleased to bless you with all spiritual benediction.  
I shall be glad to hear from you about your other progress in the matter of Sankirtana Party and selling BTGs. That is our main strength for preaching work. Thank you very much.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

If we had thought more carefully, we would have distributed *Back to Godhead* magazines at Woodstock rather than only incense and peacock feathers. *Back to Godhead* distribution was becoming the backbone of Prabhupāda’s preaching formula.

## New Vrindaban, 1969

New Vrindaban, 1969

## Hearing the Tape

Prabhupāda visited New Vrindaban during the summer of 1969. Toṣaṇa Kṛṣṇa and I took turns hitchhiking from Chapel Hill to see him.

When I was dropped off at Route 250, I saw the “ISKCON New Vrindaban” sign pointing down the twisting, uneven, dirt road and I began the hike. A small mountain stream cut across, following the road through a shallow valley to the right. Prabhupāda himself, at 73, had just weeks before walked the entire two miles to the farmhouse temple.

As soon as I arrived, I was invited by Prabhupāda’s secretary into Prabhupāda’s room. I climbed the steep stairs and offered my obeisances. Prabhupāda sat smiling and effulgent behind a metal trunk-desk in an almost bare, second-floor room.

Prabhupāda’s first words were, “Have you heard the tape?”

“No,” I said, and he turned on a tape recorder. *Taṁburā* vibrations and *mṛdaṅga* rhythms filled the room. Viṣṇujana was singing, his voice deep and resonant. He offered respects to *guru,* Gaurāṅga, and the eternal associates of Kṛṣṇa in Goloka: *“vande ‘ham śrī-guroḥ śrī-yuta-pada-kamalaṁ śrī-gurūn vaiṣṇavāṁś ca.*”

Other devotees climbed the stairs and came in to listen. The atmosphere was thick and sweet and I stood up and danced to the taped *kīrtana.*

\* \* \*

Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa reside in Prabhupāda’s pure heart. Everywhere Prabhupāda went was transformed into a *dhāma,* a holy place of pilgrimage.

## Kali-yuga and Ghee

In 1969, New Vrindaban was impoverished. When it came time for my second initiation, the temple could only afford two pounds of butter to heat into ghee for the fire sacrifice. Half of that small amount spilled as the devotee carrying the ghee pot slipped on his way to the sacrificial arena.

Prabhupāda simply shook his head when he was handed the pot with its tiny amount of ghee. “In Vedic times,” he said, “tons of ghee were spared for a single *yajña.* But now, just see!”

\* \* \*

I remember wondering if the small amount of ghee was an inauspicious omen for my initiation into brahminical life, but Prabhupāda performed the *yajña* himself, despite the inconvenient and inappropriate amount of ghee, and that was all-auspicious.

## Lessons on Practicality

The day prior to the ceremony, I surrendered to helping the devotees rip down a house in Wheeling to get lumber for construction at New Vrindaban. I then spent the morning before the initiation searching for my beads. By the time the ceremony was about to begin, I was tired and I dozed while Prabhupāda lectured. I was sitting directly in front of him and he spoke sharply to me, “You are inattentive!”

\* \* \*

Surrender must be tempered with practicality and I should have rested sufficiently before the initiation ceremony.

Dispassionate and intelligent searching for missing items is practical, but frantic and passionate searching only creates anxiety. I vowed from that day onward not to waste time looking over and over again in the same place for missing beads (or anything else). Experience has shown that if lost items are to reappear, they do so on their own, as my beads did, when Kṛṣṇa desires. If He is not disposed, frantic searching does not help.

## My Brahminical Initiation Test

Amid the *kīrtana* and readings, Prabhupāda called me to his side and softly intoned the *gāyatrī mantra* into my ear. Then he demonstrated how to count ten *mantras* on my right hand.”Now you do it.” Unsure, tired, inattentive, and frightened, I started counting from one to ten on the creases and tips of my right hand, praying that Kṛṣṇa would guide me on the correct path from one to ten. Only by His mercy did I succeed.

## Time, Place, and Circumstance

Prabhupāda was walking alone one morning on a dirt road. He suddenly turned and, carrying a *loṭa,* headed out to the fields. I had my camera with me and enthusiastically followed Prabhupāda. I called out from behind,”Prabhupāda, they asked me to take pictures for BTG.”

He turned, paused for a moment and said, “Yes, but not now.”

\* \* \*

Few devotees at this time had been to India and none had any experience of Indian village life. No one, therefore, knew the meaning of a man with a *loṭa* walking out alone into the fields.

How many details about living a simple yet ideal human life Prabhupāda desired to teach us! In order to do so, Prabhupāda wanted to establish a Kṛṣṇa-centered Vedic village at New Vrindaban. Prabhupāda tolerated our ignorance just as an affectionate father tolerates the immature behavior of his children.

## The Power of Kṛṣṇa—the Power of the Scientists
