Each afternoon, Prabhupāda sat on a hill under a persimmon tree. Resting his hands on a makeshift wooden table, he spoke casually to whoever gathered to hear him. One afternoon he waved his hand dramatically across the lush, green valley and spoke about the scientists’ limitations.

“The scientists can drop a bomb. Then all the leaves from these trees will be finished.” He paused as the devotees watched his hand move again to span the valley, “But they cannot again bring the leaves back.”

“Kṛṣṇa is so wonderful,” he continued, “with so much more power than any scientist. He can make all the trees leafless in winter and again bring back all the leaves in summer.”

\* \* \*

We were satisfied just sitting with Prabhupāda and hearing him speak simple *kṛṣṇa-kathā.*

## Speaking from Below, Being Heard from Above

I gave a morning *Bhāgavatam* class from the First Canto. I sat on the simple pillow *āsana* and glorified Śukadeva Gosvāmī, declaring that Śukadeva’s giving up Brahman realization for Bhagavān realization clearly proves the superiority of Bhagavān realization. As I lectured, Prabhupāda listened from his room above.

When I finished speaking, Devānanda challenged, “Why does Śukadeva’s acceptance of Bhagavān necessarily mean Bhagavān realization is superior? Perhaps it is simply his preference.”

I responded dutifully: “Śukadeva was not alone in his rejection: the four Kumāras also rejected Brahman for Bhagavān.”

Devānanda wasn’t satisfied. Other devotees joined in and argued; some of them even became angry: “How can someone in impersonal Brahman have a personal preference?” “The scriptures say that we can’t stay in Brahman, but that we must fall from that position.” “We accept what Prabhupāda says and Prabhupāda says Bhagavān is best!”

After pounding Devānanda into submission, we all filed up the winding stairs to Prabhupāda’s room. I reached the top first. Prabhupāda looked at me and smiled, “So they have put the question to you.” I nodded my head, realizing that Prabhupāda had been listening to my lecture.

“Prabhupāda,” one devotee ventured, “is it that impersonalists *must* fall down, or is it that only *most* of them fall down?”

Prabhupāda replied, “All of them fall down.”

“Why?”

Prabhupāda was silent for several seconds. His silence accentuated the dim light that mixed with the dawn sun and illuminated his golden face.

“Because—” he seemed to be thinking deeply how to explain this esoteric point to his children, “they get lonely.”

\* \* \*

An expert teacher knows his material so thoroughly that he can explain the most complicated subject simply and clearly.

## Faith in Śāstra

One afternoon while Prabhupāda sat under the persimmon tree, Trivikrama approached him. Trivikrama asked Prabhupāda about the contradiction between the shortened Kali-yuga life span predicted in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and modern science’s claim that life spans are increasing.

Prabhupāda denied the scientists’ claim. When Trivikrama responded that the scientific records prove it, Prabhupāda’s answer was simple and to the point. “We accept the authority of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.*”

\* \* \*

Logic and direct experience, although useful in proving śāstric conclusions, are in and of themselves imperfect methods of uncovering absolute conclusions and cannot lead us to the Absolute Truth. When logic and experience conflicted with *śāstra,* Prabhupāda always accepted the śāstric conclusion.

## The Best Choice Was Also Digestible

That same afternoon, Prabhupāda’s servant brought him a tray of freshly cut fruit—mangoes (which were Prabhupāda’s favorite), oranges, apples, and grapes. Prabhupāda ate a few pieces of the fruit and his servant carried the tray away. As the servant walked back to the farmhouse, he stopped and offered me some of Prabhupāda’s remnants. Prabhupāda watched.

I looked at the golden mango slices, the sweet grapes, the succulent orange sections, and chose the orange peel that had touched Prabhupāda’s mouth. Prabhupāda saw me reach onto the plate and take the orange peel. He broke into a huge smile. Laughing as I placed it in my mouth, he said, “It is digestible.”

\* \* \*

Dust from a pure devotee’s feet, water that has washed his feet, and remnants of his food are the most holy items. Prabhupāda appreciated that I chose the remnants that had touched his lips over the apparently more delectable items on the tray.”It is digestible.” No adverse effects accrue from proper choices in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I later found out, while living in Hong Kong, that orange peels are not only digestible, but medicinal.

## A Choice: Practicality or Spiritual Sentiment

Brahmānanda sat with Prabhupāda under the persimmon tree and discussed Prabhupāda’s old apartment at 26 Second Avenue. The devotees had already relocated from the original 26 Second Avenue storefront to 61 Second Avenue. Brahmānanda asked Prabhupāda whether we should keep the old apartment because the rent was $150 per month and the New York temple was poor.

Prabhupāda considered the question only from the practical viewpoint. The temple was poor and therefore it was impractical to keep the apartment. He told Brahmānanda to drop the lease.

## Argument as Offense

Rāyarāma came from New York to discuss *Back to Godhead* magazine with Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda had complained that Kṛṣṇa did not look beautiful on the two-tone brown cover of the last issue. He also objected to the advertisements Rāyarāma carried in the magazine.

Rāyarāma sat with Prabhupāda in his upstairs room. Prabhupāda reprimanded him. Amazingly, Rāyarāma argued back. At the bottom of the steep, spiraling stairs stood Gargamuni, listening both attentively and with apparent relish. I walked over to the stairs and also began to listen.

Prabhupāda said, “If you wish to argue with your spiritual master, then what can I do to help you?”

Gargamuni turned to me and gestured that I should leave. “This is only for big devotees.”

\* \* \*

*Ādau guru-pādāśrayaḥ,* as Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī states in his *Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu,* is the beginning of spiritual life: “One must first accept shelter at the lotus feet of a spiritual master.” We are certainly allowed to present our viewpoint before our *guru*, but we must ultimately surrender to his desire. The *guru’s* desire is Kṛṣṇa’s desire and a disciple must always defer to his spiritual master with respect and faith.

Rāyarāma’s inappropriate attitude was actually a symptom of the lust and doubts which were wreaking havoc in his spiritual life. Soon after this conversation with Prabhupāda, Rāyarāma left the association of devotees and returned to his previous, sinful lifestyle.

Gargamuni’s comment which forbade a beginner like me from witnessing a senior disciple arguing with Prabhupāda was more or less justified, yet the instruction gathered from the episode is certainly instructive for both beginners and “big devotees.”

Of course, the “big devotee” is the one fully surrendered to his *guru,* beyond attraction to sense gratification, and well on his way to realizing his eternal relationship with Kṛṣṇa.

## Not for BTG

Prabhupāda sat outside the temple-farmhouse on a mat in the hot sun. Devānanda massaged his soft body, which now glistened with mustard oil. Prabhupāda was jolly as he spoke to the devotees present.

I listened for a while and then got my camera. I focused and snapped. Prabhupāda turned to me and smiled, “This picture is not for BTG.”

\* \* \*

Official or publicly published pictures of exalted personalities who propagate the serious mission of reclaiming souls from pain and death should not be casual or intimately personal. Rather, they should reflect the seriousness of the mission. I understood this same point when I heard from Gurudāsa that Prabhupāda had objected to the photo of himself published on the back of the first edition of *Kṛṣṇa* book. He said it was too informal. The BBT used a more formal photo for the second edition.

## Moments with Prabhupāda in Darśana:

The basic principle of sādhana.

Brahmānanda, Hayagrīva, Kīrtanānanda, and others sat upstairs with Prabhupāda as Prabhupāda gave darśana. He told us, “The secret of Kṛṣṇa consciousness is to arrange your day so you cannot forget Kṛṣṇa for a moment.” Prabhupāda turned to Brahmānanda, “You have been with us for some time now. It is not difficult, is it? You are able to arrange like this?”

Brahmānanda smiled and attested to the simplicity of the principle and the bliss that results from successfully executing it.

\* \* \*

The *Viṣṇu Purāṇa* states:

smartavyaḥ satataṁ viṣṇur  
vismartavyo na jātucit  
sarve vidhi-niṣedhāḥ syur  
etayor eva kiṅkarāḥ

“The essence of all the rules and regulations is to always remember Kṛṣṇa and never forget Him. All other rules and regulations are meant to assist this primary one.”

Expert and more expert

Prabhupāda then turned the conversation to Hayagrīva. Hayagrīva was editing Prabhupāda’s *Bhagavad-gītā* and had come with technical editing questions. With his professorial voice, Hayagrīva explained how he removed the italics from Sanskrit words that were now common in the English language, such as *“brāhmaṇa*” and *“guru.*” He asked Prabhupāda’s approval for this.

Hayagrīva continued questioning and Prabhupāda laughed, seeming to toy with Hayagrīva and his technical questions of style and usage. Hayagrīva didn’t seem to notice. Ultimately, Prabhupāda allowed Hayagrīva to proceed as he desired. After all, Hayagrīva was expert in editing and the English language.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda did not interfere with the work of his “experts.” Rather, he acknowledged their skills and encouraged them to engage their talents fully in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

The proper ear to hear proper chanting

As devotees sat and spoke with Prabhupāda, others would listen at the bottom of the stairs or come up and join in the discussion. Arundhatī dāsī sat at the bottom of the stairs, chanting *japa.* Prabhupāda noticed her chanting and asked who it was that was chanting. A devotee replied “Arundhatī,” and Prabhupāda asked her to come up.

She climbed the stairs and offered her obeisances. Prabhupāda said, “Very nice chanting!”

\* \* \*

Every Western devotee walking through Vṛndāvana’s Loi Bazaar immediately hears the motives behind the shopkeepers’ chanting of “Hare Kṛṣṇa! Haribol, Prabhu! Come!” It is the quality of the chanting that is important. We can attract Kṛṣṇa by chanting His names if we chant with quality. Both Kṛṣṇa and Kṛṣṇa’s devotee can hear the difference between chanting that is motivated or inattentive and chanting that is pure. Prabhupāda explains the importance of the quality with which one chants Kṛṣṇa’s names in a purport of one of the prayers Kuntīdevī offers Lord Kṛṣṇa:

janmaiśvarya-śruta-śrībhir  
edhamāna-madaḥ pumān  
naivārhaty abhidhātuṁ vai  
tvām akiñcana-gocaram

My Lord, Your Lordship can easily be approached, but only by those who are materially exhausted. One who is on the path of \[material\] progress, trying to improve himself with respectable parentage, great opulence, high education and bodily beauty, cannot approach You with sincere feeling.

**PURPORT:** Being materially advanced means taking birth in an aristocratic family and possessing great wealth, an education and attractive personal beauty. All materialistic men are mad after possessing all these material opulences, and this is known as the advancement of material civilization. But the result is that by possessing all these material assets one becomes artificially puffed up, intoxicated by such temporary possessions. Consequently, such materially puffed up persons are incapable of uttering the holy name of the Lord by addressing Him feelingly, “O Govinda, O Kṛṣṇa.” It is said in the *śāstras* that by once uttering the holy name of the Lord, the sinner gets rid of a quantity of sins that he is unable to commit. Such is the power of uttering the holy name of the Lord. There is not the least exaggeration in this statement. Actually the Lord’s holy name has such powerful potency. But there is a quality to such utterances also. It depends on the quality of feeling. A helpless man can feelingly utter the holy name of the Lord, whereas a man who utters the same holy name in great material satisfaction cannot be so sincere. A materially puffed up person may utter the holy name of the Lord occasionally, but he is incapable of uttering the name in quality.

—Bhāg. 1.8.26

The greatest scholar

Prabhupāda then glorified Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī. “Jīva Gosvāmī was a boy when his father died. He wanted to go to his uncles, Rūpa and Sanātana in Vṛndāvana, but he thought, 'What service can I do? I’ll just be a burden.’ He then went to Benares to study and became the greatest scholar the world has ever seen. It has been said that there has never been a scholar as great as Jīva Gosvāmī, nor in the future will there be a scholar as great as he.”

\* \* \*

We had heard of Jīva Gosvāmī even in those days, but we only knew of him as one of the six Gosvāmīs of Vṛndāvana and the nephew of Rūpa and Sanātana Gosvāmīs. I immediately became attracted to Śrī Jīva Gosvāmī because of Prabhupāda’s glorification. Years later, while living in Vṛndāvana, I was fortunate enough to glimpse from a great distance the piercing sharpness of Śrī Jīva’s śāstric and logical exposition of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava philosophy. Jīva Gosvāmī’s knowledge of the *Bhāgavatam* is so complete that it has been compared to a man holding a walnut in the fist of his hand.

Two incidents of answering from hearing and reading

Prabhupāda asked a simple question: “What is the name of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s eight written stanzas?” The room was quiet. I called out *“Śikṣāṣṭaka,*” pronouncing it *“she-kas-tak*.” I was imitating Prabhupāda’s Bengali pronunciation, which I had heard on a tape that had been distributed to the temples. Pradyumna, who also sat nearby, then answered, *“Śikṣāṣṭakam*,” pronouncing it, *“shik-sha-sta-kam*,” as if transliterated from Sanskrit.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda understood my pronunciation and accepted my answer, but the other devotees couldn’t understand what I said because they had only seen the word, not heard it pronounced by Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda then mentioned how Brahmā had first heard *gāyatrī* directly from Kṛṣṇa. He again questioned the devotees: “Which *gāyatrī-mantra* did Kṛṣṇa bestow on Brahmā?” No one answered. I called out, *“Kāma-gāyatrī.*” Prabhupāda nodded in approval and replied, “You may utter it.” Then I became confused and recited the *brahma-gāyatrī* by mistake. Prabhupāda shook his head.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda had described Lord Brahmā’s initiation by Kṛṣṇa into *kāma-gāyatrī* in *The Teachings of Lord Caitanya* and had even mentioned the *mantra.* That book was published in 1969 and I had already read it. I was thrilled to be able to answer Prabhupāda’s question, but I lacked the surety to reply with the proper *mantra.* I remember at this time feeling inspired to deeply hear, study, learn, and remember the scriptures in preparation for Prabhupāda’s future examinations.

Just like Narendra Mullik

A branch of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement began spontaneously in Atlantic City, New Jersey, in 1968. One youth had met the devotees in New York City, chanted with them, gone home to Atlantic City, and started all his friends chanting. As a result of his spontaneous attraction to Kṛṣṇa’s name, many devotees joined ISKCON, but he never joined.

Prabhupāda asked Brahmānanda about that boy, with whom Brahmānanda had been in contact from 26 Second Avenue. When Brahmānanda explained that the boy could not adopt a Kṛṣṇa conscious lifestyle, Prabhupāda said it reminded him of his own past.

Prabhupāda relaxed, leaned back, and gracefully moved his right arm over the top of his head until his hand touched his left ear. He explained that because he had seen so many cheating *sādhus* as a youth, he had no interest in *sādhus* as a young man, but his friend, Narendra Mullik, literally forced him to meet Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Gosvāmī Mahārāja.

Prabhupāda concluded, “Although I did not take initiation from my Guru Mahārāja for six years after this first meeting, I always thought of him from that first meeting onwards.” Then he added, “Just like Narendra Mullik brought me to my Guru Mahārāja but he himself could not take his shelter, this boy has brought so many others, but he himself could not join.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s mood as he drew the parallel between Narendra Mullik and the Atlantic City boy was attractive, sweet, and mysterious. Hearing him speak made me think about my own case: “Why me and not others?” Many of my friends had begun chanting but then quickly dropped Kṛṣṇa consciousness for other pursuits. Why? Many were more intelligent and seemed more sincere in their quest for the “truth” than I. Why does one soul stay and another leave?

*The Nectar of Devotion* describes that devotional service is cultivated through many lives. Each soul has his own unique Kṛṣṇa conscious history. By the mercy of the Vaiṣṇavas, a soul makes his Kṛṣṇa conscious start by hearing the holy name, seeing a holy Vaiṣṇava book, taking *prasādam,* or performing service, even unknowingly, to a Vaiṣṇava. Thus a Vaiṣṇava connects a soul with the Lord’s internal energy.

From that point a soul can only move forward. His Kṛṣṇa conscious credit is eternal and will increase. One soul may be hearing about Kṛṣṇa for the first time whereas someone else, apparently coming to Kṛṣṇa consciousness at the same time, may actually be further along the path.

Compassionate devotees should therefore loudly chant Kṛṣṇa’s holy names and distribute *prasādam* and books as widely as possible, without being attached to the results. Kṛṣṇa conscious piety will be bestowed on many, many hearts, and when a soul’s time comes, his fortune will manifest by attraction to serve Śrī Śrī Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa and Their devotees.

## A Kiss on a Lotus

One morning after the *japa* period had ended and the *kīrtana* had begun, Prabhupāda came down the stairs from his second floor room to give the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class. As Prabhupāda entered the temple room and walked toward his *vyāsāsana,* all the devotees bowed down. I offered my obeisances and then quickly sat up. Prabhupāda paused just before Kīrtanānanda Swami’s bowed head. Kīrtanānanda Swami raised his head and kissed Prabhupāda’s foot.

\* \* \*

I was stunned. “Am I supposed to do that?” I wondered, noting that Prabhupāda had accepted Kīrtanānanda’s gesture of reverence and affection.

## The Roar of Thunder

In the class that morning, Prabhupāda spoke of the hand gaining strength by its supplying food through the mouth to the stomach. If the hand tries to eat independently, it will weaken and perish. Prabhupāda used this simple analogy to show that the soul must serve Kṛṣṇa to be happy. Then Prabhupāda called for questions.

New Vrindaban was famous for sheltering crazy fellows who had often taken too many drugs before joining the movement. One of them, Maṅgala, raised his hand.

“Yes?” Prabhupāda acknowledged him.

Maṅgala explained that using the hand to feed the stomach is not the only way to be nourished. “In fact,” he said, “when people are sick, they can be fed through the anus.”

All the devotees turned and stared at him. What a peculiar question. Actually, it sounded more like a challenge. Prabhupāda thundered his reply: “What you have said, that is for diseased persons! We are speaking of how a normal person eats and gets nourished. That way is the hand feeding the stomach! We should accept the standard system!”

\* \* \*

In a Vyāsa-pūjā lecture, Prabhupāda once spoke of the great austerities Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura had performed to initiate his preaching. Prabhupāda’s own austerities were uniquely formidable. Perhaps one of his greatest austerities was dealing with the drug-crazed youths of the ‘60s.

## A Foundation for the Future

I worked as a bricklayer’s assistant to support the Chapel Hill temple. I made money at it, but I also learned how to lay bricks. I thought it would be something I could teach others. “With an army of bricklayers,” I thought,”we could develop New Vrindaban.” I informed Prabhupāda of my idea when I saw him at New Vrindaban.

He approved. Then he described the Indian system of making bricks. “First, the clay is shaped into a brick. When they have many bricks, they place them on dried wood and light a fire under them. By the time the fire has died, the bricks are baked strong.”

I was inspired by Prabhupāda’s directions. I walked into a field, found some local clay, and formed a small brick. I then threw it in the cooking fire. When I returned a few hours later, the clay had hardened.

When I showed it to Prabhupāda, he took the brick in his hand and scrutinized it. I couldn’t penetrate his mood. “Should I continue learning to lay bricks?” I asked.

“Yes,” Prabhupāda said. “This is our philosophy: learn something and teach others.”

\* \* \*

I was an ISKCON devotee, the president of a new temple, and a philosophy graduate from the University of Buffalo. Not only was I laying bricks, but I was thinking of it as a possible Kṛṣṇa conscious career. Prabhupāda accepted my enthusiasm, and although he didn’t directly comment on my choice of vocation, he left me with a sparkling jewel: “This is our philosophy: learn something and teach others.”

“Learn something and teach others.” Simple, yet packed with the sentiment of Vaiṣṇava*-sevā* and the power of preaching. “Learn something and teach others”: the key to practical study. “Learn something and teach others”: the mood of a devotee as teacher. “Learn something and teach others”: became grafted to my consciousness by Prabhupāda as a fundamental principle of my Kṛṣṇa conscious life.

## The Pains of Unsimple Living

In the name of efficiency, the New Vrindaban devotees bought a second-hand wringer washing machine. I was one of the devotees delegated to transport it from the main road to the farm house, a two-mile hike.

The machine was old-fashioned, built to last, and heavy. A few of us hoisted it on our shoulders. We would carry it a few hundred feet before its weight forced us to put it down. We then hung it between thick wooden poles and, with more devotees helping, again heaved it onto our shoulders. We strained, walked a few hundred feet, and again had to put it down. We spent hours hoisting, thrusting, pulling, and pushing. Finally, we got the washing machine to the well directly in front of the farmhouse. We were all exhausted.

We ran an extension cord from the farmhouse to the washing machine, but where would the water come from to fill the tub? We realized that we were going to have to move the washing machine down the valley to where a small stream tumbled from a waterfall into a pond. But what about electricity?

As the devotees tossed around possible solutions, someone told Prabhupāda about it. His reply? “Just see.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda envisioned “plain living and high thinking” as the working principle for New Vrindaban. He knew all our work-saving plans would only result in more work. He saw all our muscle straining and ensuing problems as opposing the principle of New Vrindaban. “Just see.”

## A Garland of Affection

My two-week visit to New Vrindaban passed quickly in Prabhupāda’s association. When it was time to return to Chapel Hill, a few devotees and I who were headed back to the New York temple climbed the stairs to Prabhupāda’s room to offer him our respects. We then picked up our bags and started walking down the farm road to the main road.

Suddenly we heard a voice from behind us, “Uddhava, come up!” We turned. Prabhupāda was calling from the upstairs window. Uddhava immediately ran back up the stairs. A few minutes later, Uddhava returned, smiling and garlanded with Prabhupāda’s garland.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda offered Uddhava his garland as an affectionate parting gesture. I remember the twinge of envy that pierced my heart. When I reflect on this incident along with its uncomfortable feeling of envy, I preach to myself: A spiritual master deals with each disciple personally and individually, and one should beware of envying his dealings with other disciples. Do not imagine that his dealings are based on superficialities—likes or dislikes. Dealings between *guru* and disciple are deep and are meant for the disciple’s welfare. In addition, it is not possible for a disciple to understand the depth of consciousness that motivates his spiritual master’s actions.

Uddhava left the association of devotees three years after his visit to Prabhupāda in New Vrindaban.

## Send Photos

I received the following letter soon after I returned to Chapel Hill:

Dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I remember when you were in New Vrindaban with me, you took some photographs with your camera. Please send these pictures to Brahmananda in New York for our Back to Godhead. In future issues we will be featuring many such photographs of our activities. I hope this will meet you in good health.

\* \* \*

I had already sent BTG the pictures I took at New Vrindaban. \[plates 7-10\] How amazing it was that Prabhupāda remembered my taking pictures. Prabhupāda’s remembering us fixed our minds on him.

## Japan, 1970

Japan, 1970

## A Surprise Order to Go East

Despite our moments of enthusiasm and our occasional successes, the Chapel Hill center did not do well and we closed it toward the end of 1969. We then moved to New York to help with the preaching there. One day I received an aerogram from Prabhupāda. His letter was short: he requested that I join Bali Mardan and Sudāmā in Tokyo to help them pioneer the preaching in Japan. My head spun. Beyond a visit to Mexico as a college student, I had never ventured outside North America. I had visions of crowded muddy streets, rickshaws, and bright neon lights. Tokyo? “I don’t even have any money.”

Still, Prabhupāda’s order filled my mind. After I recovered from the initial shock, it occurred to me where to find the money. I left the temple and took the subway to my parents’ home in Rockaway.

I begged my parents for the few thousand dollars in savings bonds they had accumulated for me. They stared at me in disbelief. They had been saving the bonds to give me at my wedding, but what could they do when confronted by an independent, shaven-headed member of the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement who wanted his own money to go off on a mission to Japan? They gave me the money.

I can only imagine what my parents thought after I left. I was intent on saving Japan on Prabhupāda’s order, so I didn’t stay to discuss it with them. I caught the AA train back to our Second Avenue temple.

The temple president was overjoyed to see the savings bonds, especially because there would be money left over after I bought my ticket. He told me that the extra money would be sent to Prabhupāda’s book fund. Then he accompanied me to the bank, not allowing me to turn either left or right, fearing I would “bloop” once the money was in my hands.

\* \* \*

I’m glad that the extra money was sent to the book fund, but I would have preferred giving the donation voluntarily rather than having it offered on my behalf through intimidation.

During my stay in New York, a giant peace march protesting U.S. involvement in the Vietnam War was held in Washington D.C. Devotees from ISKCON temples throughout the East Coast gathered, and after the huge *kīrtanās* we held in Washington, we all again gathered to chant Kṛṣṇa’s names up and down Fifth Avenue in New York City. I snapped several photos of the *“mahā-harināma*” and sent them to BTG. One even found its way onto the cover.

## Preaching in Japan

In the last week of December 1969, I flew into Tokyo’s Haneda Airport, cleared Immigration, and hailed a taxi, handing the driver my copy of the temple address in Meguru-ku. The address proved to be obscure and it took hours to locate. Finally, at 9 pm., I saw a shaven-headed, dark-skinned Westerner taking out the garbage.”Haribol!” I yelled out. It was Sudāmā. After paying the driver, we carried my luggage up the three flights of stairs to a tiny flat.

For six months we rotated our preaching between Meguru-ku, Shinju-ku, the Ginza, Shibuya, and other sophisticated suburbs. On Prabhupāda’s recommendation, we dressed in fine silk robes and wore flower garlands. Sometimes we would travel by subway to the Ginza where we would chant and distribute literature. At other times, Sudāmā’s wife, Cintāmaṇi dāsī, and I would chant alone or distribute pamphlets, Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Temple record albums, and Japanese *Back to Godhead* magazines.\[plates 11-13\] If no devotees were available to chant as we distributed, we played taped *kīrtanas* on our big Aiwa reel-to-reel tape recorder.

Our Sunday feasts were sparsely attended, but they were always joyful—Sudāmā was an excellent cook. He had spent time as Prabhupāda’s personal servant, so he had learned to cook nicely. He taught me how to make *halavā* (saturated in ghee and sugar), *samosās* (with cauliflower and peas), and *gulābjāmuns* (with powdered milk and no flour) exactly as Prabhupāda had taught him. I learned to enjoy cooking, and would cook often. On Ekādaśīs, I would fast all day but still cook for the devotees.

At one point, Sudāmā and Cintāmaṇi left Japan and traveled to Korea to renew their visas. Alone, I would distribute magazines each day at the Ginza. One day as I distributed, I heard a loud “Haribol!” Surprised, I looked up and saw huge Brahmānanda rushing toward me in blue baggy pants, his arms outstretched to envelop me in his embrace. His mission in Tokyo was to produce *Kṛṣṇa* book with the Dai Nippon printing company. He stayed for more than a month, and when Sudāmā returned, we would spend hours sitting and discussing Prabhupāda in our lonely Tokyo temple.

Bali Mardan had left Tokyo to open ISKCON’s first temple in Sydney, Australia, soon after my arrival. A few months later, just after Brahmānanda’s visit, reinforcements—Amogha and Satyavrata—were sent from Los Angeles. We visited Kyoto, the Zen holy city, for a guest appearance on a television show. We also held a Ratha-yātrā in a Meguru-ku park, chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa as we carried Jagannātha, Baladeva, and Subhadrā on a palanquin. We held our “big” *harināma* every Friday evening in Shinju-ku, the most upbeat suburb in Tokyo.

One day, as we chanted in front of a Shinju-ku bank that was closed for the weekend, a huge protest march filed past us on the street. Thousands of protesters, most wearing red or white bandanas and carrying huge banners, shouted slogans as they marched past. Suddenly, the Japanese police arrived with their gas masks and shields, and began to beat the protesters with clubs. We kept chanting as a dangerous riot erupted like a movie scene right before our eyes. In our innocence, we simply kept chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and Kṛṣṇa protected us.

Kṛṣṇa sent quite a few Japanese boys and girls to visit our temple. It was austere preaching to them because they mostly came to practice English, not to study Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We continued, regardless, on Prabhupāda’s order.

## Prabhupāda’s Unexpected Visit to Japan

Prabhupāda arrived unexpectedly in Tokyo in mid-August, 1970, accompanied by a party of six of his most senior disciples. Four of them were sannyāsis. Sudāmā, Cintāmaṇi, Satyavrata, Amogha, and I greeted him at the airport. Prabhupāda looked different. “Was Prabhupāda sick?” I wondered. Prabhupāda looked much older than when I had last seen him at New Vrindaban.

\* \* \*

Except for Sudāmā, no one knew anything about the difficulties Prabhupāda had been undergoing and which had prompted his sudden visit. Later we heard that Prabhupāda had left America due to a “conspiracy” among his disciples. Prabhupāda’s mail was being filtered in Los Angeles and his movements restricted. ISKCON’s letterhead no longer listed him as the Founder-*ācārya* of ISKCON, but simply as the founder. Prabhupāda’s worry and loss of sleep showed in his face.

These problems appeared as a defeat to Prabhupāda’s preaching, but Prabhupāda used the “defeat” to initiate his preaching in India, the biggest preaching field in the world. Thus Prabhupāda’s transcendental intelligence turned bad to good, unfavorable to favorable, and defeat to victory.

Yet ISKCON’s difficulties did affect Prabhupāda. After arriving in Tokyo, he wrote to Haṁsadūta:

Your first letter was duly received, but during the last few months I was very much mentally depressed. I do not wish to discuss those points, but simply request all the members of the Governing Body Commission to organize this institution very uprightly.

Then in the same letter, Prabhupāda discussed his plans for preaching in India:

Now I am staying in our Japan center looking after the publication of books, dealing with Dai Nippon Printing Co., and I have already placed order with them for different books and magazines to the extent of $52,000. Most of these books will be carried to India for making propaganda there on or before the World Sankirtana Party reaches there.

## The Conspiracy Thickens

We were shocked by a telephone call from New Vrindaban a few days later. The callers asked us to inform Prabhupāda that the “conspirators,” whom Prabhupāda had just awarded *sannyāsa,* attended a festival at New Vrindaban and had preached that Prabhupāda was actually Kṛṣṇa! A few of the devotees had become confused, but some senior devotees had defeated the *sannyāsīs.*

\* \* \*

Before leaving America, Prabhupāda had given the “conspirators” *sannyāsa* and ordered them to travel and preach. In that way, he said, they would become purified and free of their offenses. Instead, they caused more trouble and crossed Prabhupāda’s line of tolerance. Prabhupāda was not going to allow his fledgling society to become threatened or contaminated by their philosophical deviation. When he heard this news, he cut them off from ISKCON.

Prabhupāda’s tolerance toward his young disciples was unlimited, but he limited it to protect his society. ISKCON’s mission was too important. He was still concerned, however, for the spiritual lives of the “conspirators.” He made it clear that they would be again welcome in ISKCON when they rectified their mistake. The *sannyāsīs* quickly returned to ISKCON and to Prabhupāda’s service.

## Vyāsa-pūjā Day, 1970

Prabhupāda stayed in his Tokyo apartment on Janmāṣṭamī of 1970. He spent the day with some disciples reading *Kṛṣṇa* book, starting at the beginning and attempting to complete the first volume by midnight.

The following day was Prabhupāda’s Vyāsa-pūjā, and he agreed to celebrate it at our small suburban temple in Shibuya-ku. We spent the morning cleaning, cooking, and decorating. Prabhupāda had not yet visited the temple and I had not seen him since his arrival.

Toward noon, Sudāmā drove Prabhupāda to the temple in our white, rented Toyota van. Prabhupāda entered the wood-paneled compound, left his shoes on the Japanese-style porch, and stepped up to the hallway. He passed through the narrow passage and stared for a moment at his own picture, upside down, which had been removed from the *vyāsāsana* to make room for him to sit. He entered the temple room and observed its old wood walls and *tatami* mat floors. He saw our decorations of yellow, pink, and white carnations on the altar.

Prabhupāda was grave. He took his seat on the *vyāsāsana.* I began to offer *ārati* to Prabhupāda as Kīrtanānanda Swami, Madhudviṣa Swami, Devānanda Swami, Tamal Krishna, and the local Tokyo devotees performed *kīrtana*.

Prabhupāda stopped the *ārati.* “Where is the *puṣpa*?” he demanded. No one understood that Prabhupāda was calling for flowers. Devānanda Swami ran to the kitchen to prepare *“puṣpa* rice,” thinking that was what Prabhupāda meant.

Anxiety filled the room. Prabhupāda wasn’t satisfied.

Prabhupāda looked gravely at the devotees and called for Tamal Krishna. He slowly stepped up to the *vyāsāsana.* Prabhupāda spoke to him in a hushed, yet heavy voice. We could barely hear his words.

“Why you don’t know how to perform the Vyāsa-pūjā? Were you not there in Los Angeles when I performed it for my Guru Mahārāja?”

“Yes, Prabhupāda,” replied Tamal Krishna.

“Then where are the flowers? Where is the offering of *prasāda*? So many *sannyāsīs* are here. Why a junior man is performing the *ārati*?”

Prabhupāda continued, “And why my picture is outside in the hall? It has been turned over as well. The poison has also spread to here!”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was angry. He expected intelligence—we should have learned how to worship the spiritual master by his own example—and we failed to meet his expectations.

Prabhupāda expressed his disappointment to Tamal Krishna. He viewed our Vyāsa-pūjā attempt, and the upside-down picture in the hallway, as an another example that he was being minimized by his disciples. “The poison has also spread here.”

Tamal Krishna felt terrible and apologized to Prabhupāda. He begged Prabhupāda for a second chance and Prabhupāda allowed us to try again the next day. Prabhupāda then instructed Tamal Krishna on how to properly celebrate Vyāsa-pūjā.

Years later, I heard that Prabhupāda told a devotee who had offered him an ill-fitting *kūrta* and then adjusted it and re-offered it: “To rectify your mistakes as soon as possible is a sign of intelligence.

## Vyāsa-pūjā Day, 1970: Take Two

The following day, we observed Vyāsa-pūjā according to Prabhupāda’s instructions. Kīrtanānanda Swami, the most senior *sannyāsī,* offered the *ārati.* We each offered flowers at Prabhupāda’s feet as he sat on the *vyāsāsana* and then read him our written homages. Finally, Prabhupāda was offered a feast.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda told us that each devotee should offer him flowers three times. *Kīrtana* was going on, and after each offering of flowers, the devotee paid his *daṇḍavats.* After I offered flowers three times, he took a yellow flower and placed it in my outstretched palms.

Prabhupāda even specified the order in which the devotees should approach him to make their flower offerings: first *sannyāsīs,* then *gṛhastha* men *with* their wives, then *brahmacārīs.*

## Waste Not, Want Not

Prabhupāda had brought his Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities with him to Japan and had set them up in a closet in his apartment. We used to buy carnations for Them every day and Kārttikeya, Prabhupāda’s servant, would decorate Them. One day Prabhupāda told Kārttikeya to string the discarded carnation stems, with the long stems hanging down, as a decoration across the top of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa’s altar.

\* \* \*

The stem decoration was neither standard nor gorgeous, yet Prabhupāda insisted that Kārttikeya use them. Prabhupāda did not waste Kṛṣṇa’s energy. He insisted that everything be used creatively in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

## A Thank You

When I entered Prabhupāda’s apartment to deliver carnations on the day following the successful Vyāsa-pūjā, Prabhupāda beamed at me, “Thank you very much.”

\* \* \*

I had happily danced during the *kīrtana* on our first attempt to celebrate Prabhupāda’s Vyāsa-pūjā. “After all,” I thought, “Prabhupāda is here with us.” My affection, even in the midst of our disaster, caught Prabhupāda’s attention and, it seemed to me, he was now thanking me. How refined was Prabhupāda’s teaching expertise! He used one incident to teach many devotees according to their individual requirements. Thank *you,* Prabhupāda.

## Tolerance

Brown ants had mobilized a column into our temple room, across the floor, up Prabhupāda’s *vyāsāsana,* directly across Prabhupāda’s face on his picture, and out the far door. Prabhupāda came in the temple room and motioned that he didn’t want to sit on the *vyāsāsana.* Fearfully, a devotee shooed the ants from Prabhupāda’s picture, but Prabhupāda stopped him, “Don’t bother. They are not disturbing.”

## Prabhupāda Leaves for Calcutta

Prabhupāda stayed in Tokyo for more than two weeks before he left for Calcutta on August 29, 1970. During his stay, he took his daily morning walk in the well-kept Tokyo parks. Usually he walked only with Sudāmā or with one or two *sannyāsīs.* On one of those walks, he commented that because the Shinto temples had no deities, they were dead. He also said the fruitless trees were impious.

During this visit Prabhupāda negotiated to print some of his books, including a Hindi *Back to Godhead* magazine, with the Dai Nippon Printing Company. He was preparing for his Indian preaching tour and he wrote letters to his disciples in America who were dealing with the “conspiracy” crisis. Mostly Prabhupāda was waiting for confirmation from Acyutānanda and Jaya Patāka in Calcutta that preparations were complete to receive him and his party upon his arrival in India.

Actually, Prabhupāda’s party increased from six to seven during his Tokyo stay. He personally convinced a Canadian *bhakta* of Japanese descent to accompany him to India. Bruce Enomoto was chanting sixteen rounds a day, studying Japanese, and living outside the temple. Bruce traveled to India with Prabhupāda and later became Prabhupāda’s initiated disciple, Bhānu dāsa.

We regular members of the small Shibuya-ku temple saw Prabhupāda at the temple only a few times other than the two celebrations of Vyāsa-pūjā. We had little idea of his problems, struggles, or plans. Each day, the *sannyāsīs* who were not personally attending to Prabhupāda and the four local devotees would go on *harināma* and magazine distribution at the Ginza in downtown Tokyo. I never thought to ask Prabhupāda if I could go to India with him. He left for Calcutta on schedule and we simply went on with our duties.

## Hong Kong, 1972

Hong Kong, 1972

## In Prabhupāda’s Service; in Kṛṣṇa’s Hands

Preaching on Prabhupāda’s order in Tokyo offered me a surprise opportunity. Bali Mardan, who had just returned to Tokyo from Sydney, asked me in early October 1970, to start a new center in Hong Kong. I agreed and began collecting the fare. The temple president took my first week’s collections, but I had enough by the second week to sail from Tokyo to Okinawa to Taiwan and then on to Hong Kong.

The sea voyage was a pleasant change from routine temple life. I spent my time reading, chanting, and relaxing as the small cargo boat dipped and turned in the South China Sea. I arrived in the middle of the night and felt quite at home: Hong Kong was lit as brightly as New York City.

The next morning I took a taxi to the local Gurudvara Sikh Temple and met up with Bali Mardan, who had already been there for a week. We lived at the Sikh temple for two weeks, sleeping on the rope beds and accepting their *capātīs* and hot *sabjī* once a day. During the day, I collected donations from the local Indian community and informed them of ISKCON’s plan to open a Hare Kṛṣṇa temple. Two weeks later, Bali Mardan was ready to sail to Malaysia to open a new center. The day before he left, Jagattāriṇī dāsī (at that time, Janne), arrived from Los Angeles.

During my stay in Tokyo, Sudāmā had one day asked me if I wanted to get married. I nonchalantly agreed. He opened correspondence with the Los Angeles temple president and under Prabhupāda’s direct arrangement a match was made for me. She was not yet initiated and her name was Janne. We began exchanging letters. Somehow I never really thought that the marriage would take place, so I was surprised when she in fact arrived in Hong Kong.

Janne had been an Australian actress who had casually chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa at the first Pott’s Point temple in Sydney. On her way to London for the premier of *Ned Kelly,* the film in which she co-starred with Mick Jagger, she stopped for a short “pilgrimage” in Hollywood. There Kṛṣṇa arranged a stark view of material reality and she fearfully took shelter of the temple on Watseka Avenue. Prabhupāda was there, Viṣṇujana Swami was leading the devotees out onto Hollywood Boulevard for *harināma* every day, and Gargamuni was managing the temple. The atmosphere crackled with excitement. She moved into the temple that day, telephoning Michael Love of the Beach Boys to drop her luggage off at 3764 Watseka Ave.

Six months later, Janne’s American tourist visa ran out. The temple authorities arranged for her to travel to Hong Kong, marry me, and assist in the Hong Kong preaching. One morning we received a phone call from the Hong Kong airport. She had arrived. Bali Mardan picked her up, leaving me to cross to the Kowloon side of Hong Kong Harbor and rent a guest room in Chung King Mansions. Bali Mardan performed our marriage ceremony in the Kowloon Hindu temple that afternoon and left for Malaysia the following morning.

Later, Janne’s mother sent her a Melbourne newspaper clipping. The headline read: “Weird Cult Forces Famous Actress to Marry Japanese Monk.”

Hong Kong ISKCON was Janne and Bhūrijana, actress and “Japanese monk” from Brooklyn, New York. We were young, idealistic, inexperienced, and thrust together by Fate to preach in a capital city of Kali’s kingdom. We both wanted to please Prabhupāda, so we remained in Hong Kong, but times were difficult.

\* \* \*

And what was Prabhupāda thinking as we struggled in his service in Hong Kong? Subhag Mahārāja later related to me the following incident:

Just after joining ISKCON, Subhag Mahārāja was in England and was sitting with Prabhupāda while Prabhupāda was deep in thought. Suddenly a tear glided down Prabhupāda’s cheek. After several minutes, Subhag asked him what he was thinking that caused him to cry. Prabhupāda said he was thinking of his two disciples in Hong Kong who had sacrificed everything and who were suffering so much just to please him.

## Hell with Electricity

Chung King Mansions, more a tenement than a mansion, was hell, but at least it had electricity. (As a devotee escorted Prabhupāda through a New York subway station in 1966, the devotee suggested that the subway was like hell. Prabhupāda’s reply:”No, in hell there is no electricity.”) It was damp, hot, inhabited by prostitutes, smugglers, peddlers, and a variety of low-class people of every nationality. We tried to move from our guest-house room by renting a flat with the meager collections I had gathered. It didn’t go through and our deposit, which was basically every cent we had, slipped from my hands. For the two weeks until the deposit was returned, we lived on four Hong Kong dollars, about sixty U.S. cents.

During those two weeks, all my anxiety about money stopped. How could I be anxious about money when I had none? Every day during those two weeks, someone from somewhere would come with vegetables, rice, and wheat flour. Or someone would bring cooked chickpea *prasādam* from a temple or invite us to take *prasādam* at their home. All our necessities were supplied. Even our rent was paid by an Indian shop owner who had seen us chanting in a nearby park. We didn’t ask him to pay the rent, but he asked us to allow him to do this service. Slowly the fact dawned on me: Kṛṣṇa was actually protecting us, His insignificant, naive, immature servants who were trying to please Prabhupāda.

On the day the deposit was returned, Kṛṣṇa cut off the supply and, from our returned $150 U.S. dollars, we had to again pay our way.

## Our Hong Kong Visa Connection

Six months after our arrival in Hong Kong, Prabhupāda initiated Janne as Jagattāriṇī dāsī, servant of “one who is capable of saving the entire universe.” Our preaching was beginning to increase. We preached wherever we found an opportunity: chanting in Chinese sections of the city, visiting Hong Kong University and Hong Kong’s International School, and holding Sunday school classes for the children of the Hong Kong Indian community.\[plates 14-15\] But our time in Hong Kong was running out. I had come on a three-month tourist visa and had already extended it by six months. We applied for a missionary visa but received no reply.

Prabhupāda wrote us and described his experience with the American immigration officials. He told us that governments don’t like to change the purpose of a visa while the person is still living in their country.

Prabhupāda recommended we approach influential men, especially Westerners, and ask them for letters of recommendation. We didn’t know many people in Hong Kong and could not think of even one influential person we could ask. Suddenly we remembered Mr. Gindraux, the managing director of TWA Airlines in Hong Kong. Mr. Gindraux had spent time in India and had visited and taken a *prasādam* feast with us at Chung King Mansions. We had tried to help his teenage son with his drug problem.”Yes,” we decided, “we should see Mr. Gindraux and get a letter from him as Prabhupāda suggested.”

We memorized Prabhupāda’s letter as we traveled to the Hong Kong side of the harbor and TWA’s office complex. Mr. Gindraux immediately called us into his office and we repeated Prabhupāda’s words to him, emphasizing Prabhupāda’s suggestion and our need. We then asked him for a letter.

Mr. Gindraux leaned back, first smiled and then laughed. “I can even do better than that,” he said. “I’m going to a party tonight. I will be seeing my friend, Mr. Watson, the head of the immigration department. I’ll talk to him and try to get your work done.”

\* \* \*

We were granted a permanent Hong Kong visa within a few weeks.

## A Surprise Telephone Message

By 1972, we had rented a small, second floor flat on Cameron Road. Jagattāriṇī decorated the walls with pictures of Kṛṣṇa surrounded by cloth, beads and peacock feathers. One day, quite unexpectedly, Prabhupāda’s secretary Śyāmasundara telephoned from New Zealand. Prabhupāda would soon be arriving in Hong Kong on a one-day stopover. We all, myself included, would then travel together to Tokyo. “Prabhupāda is insisting,” Śyāmasundara said, “that he stay in the Park Hotel.”

“Why?” I asked.

Śyāmasundara explained that Prabhupāda’s one day in Hong Kong was considered a stopover, and the airline was providing the hotel room at no charge.

\* \* \*

This was Prabhupāda’s first visit to Hong Kong. To be frugal with Kṛṣṇa’s money, Prabhupāda accepted the airline’s arrangement. Śyāmasundara also explained that Prabhupāda and his entourage would accept the airline-provided transportation from the airport to the Park Hotel.

## A Swan’s Entrance into the Land of Dragons

A large gathering of the interested, the curious, and the devoted mingled in the Park Hotel’s penthouse function room. The room was decorated with flowers and the hotel couch was covered with a cloth. The air was filled with expectancy. About thirty Indian children and one hundred Indian adults waited along with our few Chinese followers.

A *kīrtana* began. It was nearing the time when Prabhupāda would emerge from the hotel’s top floor, walk up the one flight to the meeting room, and greet his Hong Kong Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees.

Suddenly Prabhupāda appeared, effulgent in saffron dress and flower garlands and smiling sweetly. The *kīrtana* increased in intensity, *“Jaya* Prabhupāda! *Jaya* Prabhupāda!”

I fell to the ground in obeisances and quickly jumped up to lead Prabhupāda to his sitting place. I had begged some gladiolas, and was spreading them at Prabhupāda’s feet as he walked. By the time Prabhupāda reached the couch, the *kīrtana* was in full swing. The children chanted and the *mṛdaṅga* and *karatāla* players jumped up and down. Even the staid Indian businessmen were swept into Prabhupāda’s enthusiastic welcome.

One by one, admirers of Prabhupāda’s worldwide preaching came forward to offer garlands of Hong Kong’s indigenous flowers laced with gold and silver tinsel. They touched his feet as everyone chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa.

## An Inappropriate Offering and a Test

I offered the remaining gladiolas near Prabhupāda’s feet. Prabhupāda frowned as he whispered softly to me, “They are spoiled.” He then quickly smiled and added, “Oh, you have so many students!”

“No, Prabhupāda,” I smiled back, *“you* have so many students.”

\* \* \*

*Patraṁ puṣpaṁ phalaṁ toyaṁ:* Kṛṣṇa accepts even a leaf, a fruit, a flower, or some water from a devotee if they are offered with devotion. Implicit in that phrase, “with devotion,” is that the offering must be clean, carefully prepared, and suitable for the person to whom the offering is made. Common sense is helpful. Since this incident, I always examine the quality of my flowers before offering them, especially to Prabhupāda. Old, wilted, “spoiled” flowers are not acceptable.

Prabhupāda did not allow me to become discouraged by his comment about the “spoiled” flowers. He quickly followed it with, “Oh, you have so many students!”That statement did not enter my heart as a simple comment. It felt like a test. I momentarily paused, then responded with heartfelt confidence. These were his students, not mine. I was simply acting as his servant. Any other reply, I felt, would have been improper and also inaccurate.

## Prabhupāda on Gandhi

The *kīrtana* ended and Prabhupāda intoned the *prema-dhvani* prayers. The children and the few Chinese devotees present bowed down and replied, *“Jaya*!” to Prabhupāda’s glorifications. All eyes then focused on Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda thanked those present for offering such a warm welcome, and after a few minutes of explaining the importance of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, he began criticizing Mahatma Gandhi.

“Of course Gandhi is given so much respect for bringing India out of British control to independence. But it was Subash Candra Bose who actually forced the British to grant India independence. Gandhi held up independence for twenty years with his idea of nonviolence. Subash Candra Bose’s Indian National Army was actually the thing that made the British leave India.”

Prabhupāda spoke about Gandhi for some time and then called for questions. The Hong Kong Indians stared at Prabhupāda in shocked silence.

\* \* \*

I remember thinking, “Why is Prabhupāda talking about Gandhi? What does Gandhi have to do with Kṛṣṇa consciousness? And why is he speaking of Gandhi during this first visit?”

Later, while we were alone, Prabhupāda explained to me the details of how Subash Candra Bose’s Indian National Army (INA) had succeeded while Gandhi’s attempt had failed. Prabhupāda prefaced his explanation, “I will explain in case they ask you.”

## Indian National Army

Subash Candra Bose had a pact with Germany, who agreed that all Indian soldiers captured fighting for the British would be given to Bose’s army. This army, stationed in Burma, would first train and then, it was planned, attack the British in India. It was fear of this Indian National Army, not Gandhi’s pacifism, that forced the British out of India.

Hearing Prabhupāda’s explanation of bygone, seemingly inconsequential history was as sweet as nectar, and I didn’t ask him why he had spoken about it to the Hong Kong Indians. I speculated that Prabhupāda wanted to attack their cherished conceptions of material reality in the hopes that they would respond and he would be able to preach to them. Or, perhaps Prabhupāda was showing that he was not coming to Hong Kong like most *sādhus,* to flatter and collect funds.

I never asked Prabhupāda directly though, and it wasn’t until years later, when I looked back at the transcripts of the talk, that his reasoning became clear. As Subash Candra Bose had collected an overseas army of Indians to liberate India, Prabhupāda similarly wanted to collect such an overseas army of Indian Vaiṣṇavas to one day truly liberate their native country.

## A Lone Question

Prabhupāda’s call for questions received no response, so I stood up and addressed the audience, “Does anyone have any questions for Prabhupāda?”

Silence. Suddenly a young boy said, “Who started the forest fire?” It was Lalu, a ten-year-old boy from one of our children’s classes. “Prabhupāda, who started the forest fire?” he repeated.

Prabhupāda accepted the question as serious and began preaching, “No one started the forest fire. *Saṁsāra-dāvānala-līḍha-loka-trāṇāya kāruṇya-ghanāghanatvam.* This material existence is a blazing forest fire of suffering—birth and death. We try so hard to be happy here, but we cannot. No one starts the forest fire of material suffering. No one wants this fire to burn, but it must burn us in every case.”

\* \* \*

Months before Prabhupāda’s visit, I had read the story of Kṛṣṇa swallowing the forest fire to our Sunday school class. When the class ended, Lalu wanted to know who started the fire. I remember smiling and saying, “Prabhupāda is coming in a few months. Why not ask him?” By the time Prabhupāda came, I had forgotten the incident, but Lalu had saved his question.

## A Gift of Eternal Benefit

Larry Mak was a Chinese businessman working with our Spiritual Sky incense factory in America. I introduced him to Prabhupāda:

**Bhūrijana:** This gentleman takes care of the punk. He ships the punk to Los Angeles. He has a present for you.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh.  
**Bhūrijana:** This is Larry Mak.

Larry Mak opened a jewelry box and pulled out a gold bracelet. I clasped it around Prabhupāda’s right wrist.

**Prabhupāda:** What is that? Oh, this is for *sannyāsī* or for ladies?  
**Bhūrijana:** It is for the health.

\* \* \*

Larry Mak wanted to offer Prabhupāda a gift. I suggested a gold bracelet because I had heard that gold was good for the health. Larry Mak bought an ornately carved, 18-karat gold ladies’ bracelet. Prabhupāda accepted Larry Mak’s gift, but he also commented on the inappropriateness of a *sannyāsī* actually wearing such an ornament. Prabhupāda again questioned me about the bracelet when we flew together to Japan. When I told him that it had cost $250, he appreciated Larry Mak’s sentiment.

I heard that the bracelet was eventually melted down and used to make Prabhupāda a gold tooth, to Larry Mak’s eternal benefit.

## Food for Thought

Just before Prabhupāda stood up to leave the flower-and-cloth-covered couch, he asked me a question. Referring to the many young Indian boys and girls present he asked, “Who are the leaders? Call them.” I searched the children’s innocent faces and pointed, “Lalu, Suresh, Raju, come up.” The three boys looked at each other and stood up. Prabhupāda gave them each a garland from around his neck. As they were about to sit down he instructed them to become serious in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. “Every day, you should eat one flower from your garland.”

Prabhupāda then left the reception room and went to his room. My heart swelled as the children began spontaneously singing, *“saṁsāra-dāvānala-līḍha-loka* … “

\* \* \*

Eat a flower a day! Prabhupāda’s request was unique and merciful. If the children followed it, it would help them remember the most fortunate event of their lives, but none of them did. When I learned of their failure, I was so disappointed that I began to lose hope in the future of their Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The last remnant of hope finally evaporated during Prabhupāda’s next visit in 1974. Lalu again attended Prabhupāda’s lecture, this time at the Hong Kong Hilton. He even danced during the *kīrtana.* As he danced, a coin, ten Hong Kong cents, fell from his pocket. I tapped him on the shoulder and suggested he offer it at Prabhupāda’s feet. Lalu nervously picked it up, refused, and put the coin back in his pocket.

## In a Park Hotel Room

After the meeting, we gathered in Prabhupāda’s room. I asked Prabhupāda if he would allow me to get him some of the all-purpose Chinese medicine, ginseng, which was legendary for its invigorating qualities. Prabhupāda looked at me and smiled.”I don’t want to take any medicine. I have already told my disciples that if I am dying, don’t call a doctor and don’t take me to the hospital. Just place some *tulasī* leaves on my head and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa.”

\* \* \*

Hearing Prabhupāda speak of dying in 1972 was unnerving. We never thought of Prabhupāda leaving this world. But his instructions on how he wished to leave revealed his determined faith in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

## A Fall for the Better

Suddenly we heard a knock on Prabhupāda’s door. It was Gulab, one of our Indian congregational members. His eyes searched the hotel room, and when they lighted on Prabhupāda’s effulgent form, he dove at Prabhupāda’s feet. He then asked Prabhupāda if he could take *sannyāsa.* Prabhupāda looked skeptical, “How can you take *sannyāsa*?”

\* \* \*

Gulab was a worker in one of the many Sindhi cloth shops in Hong Kong. He chanted a few rounds every day and understood no philosophy. He spent his life toiling and suffering to support his wife and children, who still lived in Bombay. Prabhupāda immediately saw Gulab’s lack of qualification for *sannyāsa* and understood that he was trying to avoid family responsibilities.

Yet seeing Prabhupāda and diving at his lotus feet cannot go in vain. Although Gulab was not ready at that time to attain Prabhupāda’s shelter, later, by Prabhupāda’s mercy, he achieved the mercy of Prabhupāda’s disciple Tamal Krishna Goswami Mahārāja. He now is an initiated devotee, Gauravāṇī dāsa.

## Karma in Every Culture

Early the next morning, Prabhupāda went for a walk in Kowloon Park. The smell of stinking garbage greeted him as he stepped from the hotel and made his way through Tsim Tsa Tsui’s asphalt, concrete, and closed shops. The park was a green and luxurious haven from the stark Hong Kong pavement, and it was already filled with Hong Kong citizens doing their eerie ballet of rhythmic, slow-motion Tai Chi exercises.

Prabhupāda held his head high as he circled the flower beds and fountains. “Did you see her picking through the garbage as we walked here?” asked Prabhupāda. “So much prosperity and someone is forced to pick through the garbage for food.”

As we walked, I tried to explain China’s aversion to religion. When the British were attempting to subjugate China, they shipped crates of Bibles alongside crates of opium. Because of this, I explained, the Chinese government was now anti-religion. Prabhupāda’s reaction was strong, “They do not know anything about religion.” He said that the Chinese in Hong Kong were only interested in making money, “push-button money.”

After fifteen minutes in the park, Prabhupāda headed down Cameron Road toward our small temple. The lift carried him up one flight, through the obnoxious fumes of the nonvegetarian restaurant next door, and into our temple room. Incense blocked out the horrendous neighborhood smells. A tape of Prabhupāda singing *“'rādhā-kṛṣṇa’ bol bol bolo re sobāi*” played softly.

\* \* \*

The Chinese, or anyone’s, aversion to religion shows a lack of understanding and common sense. Prabhupāda also defined the capitalists’ desire to do no work other than organizing others to make money for them as “push-button money.”

When he later traveled to India, Prabhupāda told how he saw “one lady in Hong Kong picking through the garbage” for food. Wherever he traveled, Prabhupāda spotted examples proving that the only shelter from material suffering was Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

## Prabhupāda Visits our Humble Temple

Prabhupāda examined our temple, noticing the elaborate and multi-colored bead decorations my wife had created, the orange-yellow linoleum floor, the brightly lit rooms, the incense, and the atmosphere created by our simple worship.

I asked Prabhupāda to sit in a carved wooden chair we had constructed for his *vyāsāsana.* He sat, his feet on the ground, and I offered him *ārati. “Ei śikhā diyā sab nadīyā phirche nece’ gaura-nitāi,*” the tape continued as I offered water in a conch shell followed by the other *ārati* articles.

The music soon ended, and Prabhupāda’s taped explanation of the song began. He motioned that I should end the *ārati,* even before the final items were offered. Prabhupāda then spoke. A reporter from *Asia* magazine sat with us on the floor, waiting for his interview.

\* \* \*

We were preaching in Hong Kong only for Prabhupāda’s pleasure. I was satisfied to finally be able to offer Prabhupāda *ārati* in our little center. It was the perfection of two years of austere Hong Kong preaching.

## Prabhupāda Speaks

Prabhupāda:

'rādhā-kṛṣṇa’ bol bol bolo re sobāi  
(ei) śikhā diyā, sab nadīyā  
phirche nece’ gaura-nitāi

So '*rādhā-kṛṣṇa’ bol bol bolo re sobāi, ei śikhā dīyā:* This is the teaching of Lord Caitanya. Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura, the composer of this song, says that “you get out of all kinds of dangers if you simply take to chanting this Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra.*” *'Rādhā-kṛṣṇa’ bolo, saṅge calo:* “Please cooperate with Me,” Lord Caitanya says, “and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa.” *Ei-mātra bhikhā:* “I am asking only this. I am begging.”  
So our mission is also like that. We are asking people without any argument or political purpose. Simply we are asking that you chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. That’s all. Wherever we are opening our branches, our only business is requesting people to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa or Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa. We don’t want anything else. We simply wish to spread the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra* because people are being washed away by illusion. They are being washed away and always in trouble. *Khāccho hābuḍubu, bhāi. Jīv kṛṣṇa-dās, e biśwās, korle to’ ār duḥkho nāi:* “But if you try to understand that your position is eternal servitude to Kṛṣṇa, then your all troubles are over.”  
So you are all here. I think you should open a very nice center for preaching this gospel. And we have no discrimination. We accept anyone because we do not see the outward body. Just as a gentleman is not interested with the outward dress, he is interested with the person he talks to. Similarly, we are interested to see every person as spirit soul. *Paṇḍitāḥ sama-darśinaḥ. Paṇḍita* means learned. *Sama-darśinaḥ.*

vidyā-vinaya-sampanne  
brāhmaṇe gavi hastini  
śuni caiva śva-pāke ca  
paṇḍitāḥ sama-darśinaḥ

“One who is actually learned, he sees everyone equally, either he is a very learned scholar or he is a dog or a *caṇḍāla* or an elephant.” So how the learned scholar and dog can be seen on the equal level? Not that the dog and the learned scholar are equal, but seeing them equally means to see the spirit soul within the body. That is true vision. Outwardly, by the body, one is a learned scholar and another one is a dog. That is outwardly. But inwardly, everyone is spirit soul, Brahman. That is called *brahma-darśana.* So if people are taught this philosophy, then all these distinctions and creations of different kinds of problems—the Chinese or Communists, the Americans or Capitalists, and so many differences we have created—will end. The problem now is fight coming from competition. There is enmity, enviousness. All due to ignorance. Or, in one word, it is due to lack of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Therefore we have taken this path for social improvement in human society. Let us cooperate.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was speaking to our congregation—Chinese and Indians—who were, to varying degrees, interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He knew that, in order to cooperate and spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness, they would have to remove the ignorance of bodily identification through Kṛṣṇa conscious realization.

## Prabhupāda’s Encouragement

**Prabhupāda:** So this is nice place. You have decorated it nicely. Everything is nice so far the little place is concerned. How many rooms you have got?  
**Bhūrijana:** This is it.  
**Prabhupāda:** Only one room.  
**Bhūrijana:** And this little room in the back.  
**Prabhupāda:** That’s very natural. It is costly here.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda noticed our simple living facility, which we had decorated to the best of our ability, and he encouraged us.

## Education: the Key to the Future

**Prabhupāda:** But these children and their parents are also very much interested. So I think you should have a nice institution here to train the children in this country. And after their education, they may take to any line of livelihood. That doesn’t matter. But the foundation … Just like in our Vedic system, first of all *brahmacārī.* Even Kṛṣṇa, God Himself, although He knows everything, but just to teach us, He also became a *brahmacārī* in the *gurukula.* Lord Rāmacandra, He also accepted a *guru,* Vasiṣṭha. That is our Vedic system. Anybody may be anything, but the process is *tad-vijñānārtham sa gurum evābhigacchet.* In order to learn the value of life, spiritual value of life, one must go to a *guru.*

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda saw the seed of Kṛṣṇa consciousness in the children of Hong Kong and thus provided a vision that would allow that seed to grow and blossom.

## A Universal Principle

**Prabhupāda:** Similarly, everyone has to hear either from Kṛṣṇa or from His bona fide representative. That is *śrotriyam.* The result will be that after becoming a student of such bona fide *guru,* one will be firmly fixed up in God consciousness. That is the result, firmly fixed up. Now, these boys and girls from foreign countries, three or four years ago they did not know Kṛṣṇa. But now they are so perfectly devotee of Kṛṣṇa, and nobody can deviate them.

\* \* \*

A warning to all of us: hearing attentively and submissively from a bona fide spiritual master firmly fixes a devotee in spiritual life. Acting against this principle is dangerous.

## The Secret of Success

**Prabhupāda:** If somebody says that “You give up this Kṛṣṇa consciousness and take something else.” Now here is Śyāmasundara. His father is a very, very rich man. He is always canvassing Śyāmasundara,”You are my only son. You come, do business. You take millions of dollars, whatever you like.” But he is not going. There are many like that. They cannot give up Kṛṣṇa consciousness. And you know our Jagattāriṇī, wife of Bhūrijana. She was a theatrical girl and earning millions of dollars. But she has given up everything. She is a nice girl, educated and qualified. But she is satisfied.

I asked her to go and marry Bhūrijana. She never saw him and did not know what kind of husband she would be accepting. But simply on my word, she came from Los Angeles and got married.

The only consideration is how to please Kṛṣṇa and His representative. *Guru-kṛṣṇa-prasāde pāya bhakti-latā-bīja. Bhakti* is just like a creeper, and the seed of that creeper can be obtained by the mercy of *guru* and Kṛṣṇa. That is Vedic injunction.

yasya deve parā bhaktir  
yathā deve tathā gurau  
tasyaite kathitā hy arthāḥ  
prakāśante mahātmanaḥ

The Vedic knowledge becomes revealed to a person who has unflinching faith in Kṛṣṇa and *guru.* This is the system.

\* \* \*

Several years ago in Los Angeles, a devotee asked me how long I had been married. When I said twenty-one years, his jaw dropped. “To the same person?” It is perhaps amazing that my wife and I have been married for so long because as in any marriage, we have weathered rough periods. When young devotees ask for a success formula for a Kṛṣṇa conscious marriage, I usually offer them three principles: view your commitment to the marriage as direct service to your spiritual master, marry someone whose Kṛṣṇa consciousness you respect, and commit yourself to raising your children in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I also warn them that illicit sex corrodes the Kṛṣṇa conscious foundation of the husband-wife relationship. These principles, along with “no television,” Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, and more than a pinch of good luck, have helped me maintain my marriage in Prabhupāda’s service.

Prabhupāda’s original point was that children need to hear from a *guru,* but then he expanded this point to make it relevant to the entire audience: everyone needs a *guru.* In Prabhupāda’s preaching, he would often speak about elements of self-sacrifice in his disciples, as he did with Śyāmasundara and Jagattāriṇī. His disciples were living examples of the philosophy.

## An Interview with Prabhupāda

**Bhūrijana:** A reporter is here to interview you, Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** All right.  
**Reporter:** The Hare Kṛṣṇa movement started in the United States. Why did it start in the United States rather than in India?  
**Prabhupāda:** Because the United States, they are our best customer. A businessman goes to a place … Just like you have come here. Why you have come here? Wherever there is best possibility of doing your business, there you must go. I went to United States because I know these people are not poverty-stricken.

Our Indian people have been trained to think that they are poverty-stricken. Actually, they are not poverty-stricken. The leaders have educated them: “You are all poverty-stricken.” This is India’s position. I knew that the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement would not be successful in India. The government will not help. The public is educated in a different way. They are after technology.

And “familiarity breeds contempt.” They say, “What is this Hare Kṛṣṇa movement? We have known it since a long time. What effect it will have?” Many Indian students in foreign countries, they say,”Swamijī, how will this Hare Kṛṣṇa movement benefit us? We want technology.”

So that is the mentality of the Indians at the present moment. They have lost their own culture and therefore they are beggars. So I thought it wise to go to a country where there is no poverty.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda quickly caught the reporter as having personally come to Hong Kong for the same reason that Prabhupāda traveled West. The reporter, whose name was Dom Moraes, was a published author of Indian, but not Hindu, origin. He had immigrated to Hong Kong from Bombay and had been living in Hong Kong for years. He was not a pious man. I was surprised at how candidly Prabhupāda answered his questions.

## The Cause of Satisfaction

**Prabhupāda:** When one has satisfied his senses, he is no longer interested in sense gratification. Perhaps you know C.R. Das from Calcutta. Fifty years ago, C.R. Das was earning fifty thousand rupees per month, but he was not satisfied.  
One day, he and his wife were sitting together. His wife questioned, “Why do you look so morose? Everything is at your command. You have education, popularity. Still, you are unhappy. Why?”  
Just then, by chance, C.R. Das saw one mendicant passing by on the street as a *sādhu,* a beggar. So he said, “I want to become like him.”  
Many other instances are there in history. Like Bharata Mahārāja. He was young man, twenty-four years old, and emperor of the whole planet. He had a young wife; he was a king, and he had everything. But he left everything.  
Lord Buddha was a young prince and he was surrounded by dancing girls. But as soon as he went outside, he saw an old man. “What is this?” “This is old man. Everyone has to become old like this.” So he became immediately converted to a saintly person. “I must go and meditate and realize myself. Why shall I become old?”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda preached to the reporter rather than answering him with carefully worded statements.

## Preaching on the Guru’s Order

**Reporter:** How many disciples do you now have in the U. S.?  
**Prabhupāda:** Although one cannot expect many disciples, still, there are two thousand. Not many come because I insist on many restrictions. And because people in general have forgotten Kṛṣṇa, forgotten God, Kṛṣṇa consciousness is difficult to understand. I am trying to remind them and bring them to Kṛṣṇa consciousness.  
It is a very difficult job. I have to shed my blood three tons before I make one person convinced in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. That is my experience. Especially Europeans and Americans. They do not accept anything blindly. Questions, questions, questions. They are inquisitive, and they want to know, so I give them answers. Especially in Europe and America, when I make this condition that you cannot have illicit sex, you cannot have intoxication, you cannot eat meat, you cannot gamble … This is their daily habit. This is their life.  
Even a British lord, Lord Zetland, refused these principles, “Oh, we cannot give up these things.” So I strictly control my students, but still they are coming. So it is very difficult job, still, there are many thousands. And they are so sincere. If I ask them, like I have asked this boy \[Sudāmā Vipra Swami\], to go to Red China, they go even at the risk of their life.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda revealed the price a preacher must pay to convert a materialist into a devotee: “It is a very difficult job. I have to shed my blood three tons before I make one person convinced in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. That is my experience.”

*“Koṭiṣv api mahā-mune.*” A devotee is a rare soul in degraded Kali-yuga. Yet Prabhupāda was so powerful that he inspired thousands to give up material life, worship Kṛṣṇa, follow Kṛṣṇa’s instructions in the *Bhagavad-gītā,* and selflessly preach—even at the risk of their lives—the message of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Thus, by Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s order and by Prabhupāda’s mercy, souls trapped in *māyā’s* kingdom are becoming dear to Kṛṣṇa:

ya idaṁ paramaṁ guhyaṁ  
mad-bhakteṣv abhidhāsyati  
bhaktiṁ mayi parāṁ kṛtvā  
mām evaiṣyaty asaṁśayaḥ

na ca tasmān manuṣyeṣu  
kaścin me priya-kṛttamaḥ  
bhavitā na ca me tasmād  
anyaḥ priyataro bhuvi

For one who explains this supreme secret to the devotees, pure devotional service is guaranteed, and at the end he will come back to Me. There is no servant in this world more dear to Me than he, nor will there ever be one more dear.

—Bg. 18.68–9

How dear preacher-devotees are to Kṛṣṇa! How dear to Kṛṣṇa must be Śrīla Prabhupāda!

## Preaching to Indians with Westerners

**Prabhupāda:** It is the duty of the Indians to spread this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, but I am training these foreigners. They are doing this duty. But it is the duty of the Indians. It is their culture. But in India, a young Indian man is satisfied if he gets a nice wife and a little bit of money. Then he says,”My life is successful.”  
Therefore I went away. I previously approached many gentlemen. “Please, you have got four sons. Give me one son. I shall make him a real *brāhmaṇa.*” But there was no result because they do not know the value of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.  
Now with Western disciples, Kṛṣṇa has given me a chance to preach in India. A very good chance. Now they are appreciating. When I go to India they become surprised, “Swamijī, how you have done this thing?”

## Success Without Pride

**Reporter:** Your home base is in Los Angeles.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, my headquarters is in Los Angeles. Now we have made very big headquarters in Bombay, Juhu. Twenty thousand square yards. We are constructing a very nice temple there. And similarly, we have got another headquarters at Lord Caitanya’s birthsite, Navadvīpa. There also we are constructing a very big temple.  
**Reporter:** What about England? London? How is your movement doing?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, in London we have a very nice temple near the British Museum, 7 Bury Place. And all Europeans, they come to see our temple from Germany, from France. Because we have been advertised in cooperation with the Beatles. The Beatles, George Harrison. You know his name? He is a very famous man. Yes. So we have produced some records in cooperation with George Harrison’s organization. Because the records are produced through George Harrison, we have got a very, very big sale. And that has advertised Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Temple, London. So people come from all parts of the world to see what is our Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa temple.

## Why Criticize Others?

**Reporter:** Do you have any connection with the Maharishi?  
**Prabhupāda:** No.  
**Reporter:** What do you think of the Maharishi?  
**Prabhupāda:** Why shall I speak of others? Then people will be angry. At least, my position in foreign countries is far better than any Swami’s up to date. Vivekananda went to America about eighty years ago, 1893. So Ramakrishna Mission has got about four or five centers in America.
