But I have got centers in America and Europe, about forty centers, and so many students. In the Ramakrishna Mission you won’t find so many American boys as followers. But we have got thousands of followers. And they are all young men and girls. So that is my hope, that even if I die—I am seventy-six now—these boys will conduct this movement. I am sure.

\* \* \*

Practical advice for preachers speaking to reporters: reporters become thrilled—they have their story—but the public tends to become angry when religionists criticize others, especially other religionists.

In addition, Prabhupāda expresses his conviction that his disciples and their subsequent followers would sincerely and intelligently continue to maintain and expand ISKCON.

## All Roads Lead to Vṛndāvana—and Book Distribution

**Reporter:** Where were you born?  
**Prabhupāda:** I was born in Calcutta, 1896.  
**Reporter:** And what did you do?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. I was ordinary man. That’s all.  
**Reporter:** Did you work?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. I worked as a manager of a chemical concern in my family life. I have got my family also, my wife and children, my grandchildren, in Calcutta. But I have nothing to do with them.  
**Reporter:** And when did you start on …  
**Prabhupāda:** I left home in 1960, practically.  
**Reporter:** You left Calcutta?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Say, for last twenty-two years.  
**Reporter:** And where did you go when you left Calcutta?  
**Prabhupāda:** I retired to Vṛndāvana. So there I began to write books. When three books were finished I started for America. There also I wrote dozens of books. You have seen our books? Our *Kṛṣṇa* book is selling like anything in Europe and America. We are practically maintaining ourselves by selling books.  
We sell our books all over the world, about 20–25,000 rupees daily. We spend seven to eight lakhs of rupees monthly. In Los Angeles alone we spend $20,000 per month. In New York we spend $10,000 per month.

\* \* \*

We know of Prabhupāda’s pleasure when his books, filled with transcendental knowledge, are sold, but here Prabhupāda mentions another point about book distribution: “We are practically maintaining ourselves by selling books.” What a transcendental way to maintain ourselves: selling books by speaking about Kṛṣṇa all day.

## Crooked Questions, Straight Answers

**Reporter:** Doing what? How do you spend it?  
**Prabhupāda:** We have to maintain our establishment, the temple, the Deity, so many devotees. In each center we have got at least twenty-five devotees. At the most two hundred devotees. So their living costs, everything. But some way or other, Kṛṣṇa is giving us. But we have no fixed income, neither we have any bank balance.

\* \* \*

Common materialists can never understand how a pure devotee is satisfied by working to please Kṛṣṇa. When viewing Prabhupāda and his movement, such a reporter can’t help but apply his own materialistic and selfish tendencies and assume that ISKCON is a business with a boss on top who is “enjoying” the fruits extracted from the hard work of those whose sentiments he is exploiting.

## The Root of a Problem

**Reporter:** In the minds of some people, the sudden attraction of Western youth to Eastern religions …  
**Prabhupāda:** No. It is not Eastern. That is a wrong conception. God is for everybody. When I speak of Kṛṣṇa consciousness to Eastern people, they say, “What is this Kṛṣṇa? We know Kṛṣṇa. What we have to learn from Swamijī?” Again, “Familiarity breeds contempt.”  
But in the Western countries when we speak of Kṛṣṇa, they see the philosophy. They see the science and become attracted. Kṛṣṇa is for everyone. Kṛṣṇa is neither Western nor Eastern. But Easterners, especially Indians, they have learned to reject. That is their education—immediately reject Kṛṣṇa.  
This is their new culture—to reject everything. At least Jawaharlal Nehru began like that, “Anything Indian is bad. Everything London-made is good.” That was his philosophy. And if one European would go to see him, immediately admission. And if an Indian goes to see him, three days he has to wait. So Jawaharlal made this impression, “Everything Indian is bad, and anything made in London …” Because he was made in London. He was educated in London.  
In my household life, I did some business and I had to see Jawaharlal Nehru when he was a common man. I went to his house. I saw it is completely Europeanized, although he is in *khādī.* His father hated Indian medicine. Motilal Nehru. I was selling medicine, so I introduced him to one preparation and Motilal Nehru says, “Doctor, in case of medicine, please do not prescribe Indian.”  
You see? So this is our mentality. We have got all foreign mentality, but we are claiming that we have become independent. But we are not independent. We have been culturally conquered by the materialistic advancement of foreign countries. We have lost our own culture. This is our position.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda directly described the root cause of contemporary India’s atheistic, anti-traditional leanings—the national leadership of India, beginning with Nehru, abhorred Indian culture and trained the people accordingly.

## No Compromises from Prabhupāda

**Reporter:** Thank you so much. I’m sure …  
**Prabhupāda:** So many Indian swamis, they requested me to dress myself with coat and pants. I never agreed. See all my pictures? Why shall I take to coat, pant? What is use? Now my students, they are giving up coat, pant. And girls, they are taking to *sārīs.* There is now even a good demand for *sārīs* in Europe and America.  
**Bhūrijana:** They were even telling me here that I should wear diplomat’s clothes instead of *dhotī.* They were ashamed.  
**Prabhupāda:** Hare Kṛṣṇa. So we shall go now?

\* \* \*

Although his example of “coat, pant” is simple, Prabhupāda’s message of no compromise is essential for those who preach.

Dom Moraes went on to write his article for the weekly insert of the *Asia News,* which was sent out in newspapers throughout Asia. The article came out a month later, with pictures of Prabhupāda’s reception at the Park Hotel and excerpts from the interview. The article turned out neither sweet nor complimentary, and it received many angry letters of complaint from readers throughout Asia. I also visited the *Asia News* editor and complained. He agreed, saying, “Dom Moraes is too cynical. We should have sent someone else.” But it was too late; the article had already been published. If at all possible, devotees lining up newspaper and magazine coverage should demand the right to approve articles prior to publication.

## God’s Doll House

Prabhupāda’s visit to our temple ended with the reporter’s interview. On his way out, Prabhupāda saw a handmade doll’s house in the corner of the temple room. Jagattāriṇī had custom-designed and personally pieced together the cross-sectioned plastic doll’s house. It had different rooms, and she had outfitted it with small furniture, velvet carpets, mirrors, and toys. Prabhupāda walked over and looked inside. In one small corner of the plushly decorated doll’s house was an altar in a small temple room. On a throne sat Jagattāriṇī’s Laḍḍu Gopāla Deity. When Prabhupāda saw the Deity, he seemed surprised, but then he joined his hands together before his heart in respect.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda did not seem to notice any peculiarity in a Laḍḍu Gopāla Deity being in a doll’s house. He saw that a Deity of Kṛṣṇa was being cared for, and he simply offered his respects.

## A Distasteful Blunder

As we walked down the stairs to the street, heading back to the Park Hotel to prepare for the flight to Tokyo, Prabhupāda instructed me to prepare *kicchari* for his breakfast.

“First boil three cups of water, then add one cup rice and one cup mung *dāl.* Add one teaspoon of salt, some turmeric, and let it cook for fifteen minutes.”

I offered my obeisances and raced back to the temple. I went over the proportions and ingredients again and again in my mind. I put the water on to boil. Then it occurred to me that the other devotees would also need to eat, so I tripled the ingredients.

After ten minutes, I noticed that the *kicchari* wasn’t cooking. “Three times the ingredients,” I lamented, “but the same flame!”

The *kicchari* was only half-cooked—still hard. I added more water and tried cooking the *kicchari* longer.

Prabhupāda’s departure time was fast approaching. His taxi was due to leave in fifteen minutes. Anxiously, I lifted the pot and raced to the Park Hotel. I burst into Prabhupāda’s room and served Prabhupāda the steaming hot *kicchari.*

Prabhupāda, peaceful as always, ate one spoonful, then another, and pushed the rest aside.

\* \* \*

I remember an old BTG article on the *Rāmāyaṇa.* Hanumān first located Sītā-devī, his assigned duty, and only after succeeding in that did he go further and burn Laṅkā for Rāma. If only I had learned this lesson from the example of Śrī Hanumānjī.

## Sent by Kṛṣṇa’s Mercy

There was little time to muse over my mistake. I helped Prabhupāda catch his taxi and then quickly went back to the temple to pack and ready myself to accompany Prabhupāda’s party to Tokyo.

My *dhotīs* and *kurtās* found their way into my travel bag. Some money and some books followed. But where was my passport?

I stared at the clock. Overturning papers and clothes, I searched and re-searched everywhere. The recommended check-in time had already ended. Quickly passing through anxiety, frustration, anger, desperation, and hopelessness, I finally lost all anxiety and began to pray, “Kṛṣṇa, I can’t find my passport and there’s nothing I can do by myself. I surrender. If you want me to accompany Prabhupāda to Japan, You’ll have to place my passport in my hand. I’ve looked and looked and I still can’t find it.”

I then looked again free from anxiety and in a dependent mood. Within seconds, I found my passport, said a quick goodbye to my wife, and was on my way to the airport. Once aboard, I passed Prabhupāda’s seat on the way to mine. He looked at me with concern at my lateness. I smiled and said, “You must have some service for me in Tokyo.”

\* \* \*

I’ve mentally noted the following lessons from this incident. Hectic times are intense. Plan and prepare everything well in advance. Then, as the event looms closer, go over all important plans and preparations again and again. Count on arrivals and departures to be the most frantic. Above all, depend on Kṛṣṇa. Be surrendered as His servant and know that He will ultimately protect you according to His desire. Plan, plan, plan ahead; but depend on Kṛṣṇa.

## Asking Questions

Prabhupāda was accompanied by Nanda Kumāra, (his cook), Śyāmasundara (his secretary), and Pradyumna (his Sanskrit editor). At first I sat next to Śyāmasundara, but Prabhupāda adjusted our seating arrangements because he wanted Śyāmasundara to take dictation of his letters. I later sat next to Prabhupāda.

I didn’t have many questions to ask Prabhupāda as I sat beside him, but I did have one: Caitanya Mahāprabhu could speak *kṛṣṇa-kathā* with Rāmānanda Rāya, but you are surrounded by young disciples. Do you have anyone with whom you can speak *kṛṣṇa-kathā*?

I turned this question over and over in my mind, but never asked it. I just wasn’t bold enough.

\* \* \*

Remembering this incident, I find myself suggesting to the disciples of Godbrothers, like an uncle, that they should take advantage of opportunities to inquire from their spiritual master. If they have questions, but fear them improper, they should nevertheless ask and trust that their spiritual master, who is their well-wishing friend, will inform them of any impropriety. Hesitating to ask, I fear, may one day leave them with the realization that the opportunity to inquire, which Kṛṣṇa had provided, has passed.

## Chant! Chant! Chant!

“Keep your consciousness pure when you are in Prabhupāda’s presence by always chanting and always praying for his mercy.” This good advice came to me just before I went with Prabhupāda to Tokyo. I tried my best to follow it.

While sitting next to Prabhupāda I chanted and chanted and chanted. I chanted the *mahā-mantra.* I chanted every *śloka* and song I could remember. When I ran out of *ślokas* and songs, I returned to continuously chanting the *mahā-mantra.*

At one point, the passenger behind me called over the seat, “Stop that chanting!” Prabhupāda smiled. I lowered the volume, but continued chanting. I cut and served some fruit for Prabhupāda, but continued chanting. Prabhupāda dozed after that, but I chanted on.

Finally, just as the plane was preparing to land, and after Prabhupāda had taken out his mirror and applied fresh *tilaka,* he turned to me and said, “This is a nice habit you have, this always chanting. Nanda Kumāra, see how Bhūrijana is always chanting. You should also get this habit!”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s instructive encouragement to always chant Kṛṣṇa’s name is the essence of the path of *sādhana-bhakti.*

smartavyaḥ satataṁ viṣṇur  
vismartavyo na jātucit  
sarve vidhi-niṣedhāḥ syur  
etayor eva kiṅkarāḥ

“One should always remember Kṛṣṇa and never forget Him. All the rules and regulations should be understood as assistants to always remembering Kṛṣṇa and never forgetting Him.”

—Padma Purāṇa

In his *Upadeśāmṛta,* Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī refers to constant chanting as the essence of all advice. I therefore embrace Prabhupāda’s encouraging words as a most dear instruction, and I pray to him for the ability to always chant Hare Kṛṣṇa.

## Tokyo, 1972

Tokyo, 1972

## Samosās from Kārttikeya and Sudāmā

Samosās from Kārttikeya and Sudāmā

The Tokyo temple was situated in a remote, rural district called Asakawa-cho. Next door to the temple was a restaurant that specialized in cooking meat in an open barbecue pit, and special buses would cart Tokyo residents two hours to reach it. Business was booming.

Prabhupāda heard about the restaurant during the long drive from the airport, and both positive and negative lessons about the “restaurant near our Tokyo temple” later appeared in Prabhupāda’s worldwide preaching. On the positive side, he explained that people will travel from anywhere to our Kṛṣṇa conscious restaurants if the *prasādam* is of high quality. On the negative side, he commented on modern civilization’s misuse of time wherein a man will waste two hours simply to get flesh to eat. “I have seen in Tokyo,” he would add.

Immediately upon his arrival, Prabhupāda was escorted up the winding metal stairs to the temple room. The *kīrtana* ended as Prabhupāda sat down on the simple *vyāsāsana* before the assembled devotees and small Deities of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.

**Prabhupāda:** So what *prasāda* you have prepared?  
**Cintāmaṇi:** Um, Kārttikeya Prabhu?  
**Prabhupāda:** Kārttikeya Prabhu? Where is Kārttikeya? \[*Kārttikeya comes from the kitchen. The devotees laugh*.\]  
**Devotee:** There’s some *rasgullās* and some fruit. We have some fruit and vegetables.  
**Prabhupāda:** I wanted some *samosā.*  
**Devotees:** Ah.  
**Prabhupāda:** Sudāmā Prabhu, can you prepare it?  
**Sudāmā:** Yes, Śrīla Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** So do it.

\* \* \*

Kārttikeya had served as Prabhupāda’s servant. Later he became a *sannyāsī,* then left the *sannyāsa* order. Prabhupāda knew that Kārttikeya was in Japan assisting Sudāmā and, out of affection, he had called for him. Sudāmā had also been Prabhupāda’s servant and was an expert cook. When Sudāmā was serving Prabhupāda, Prabhupāda taught him to make *samosās* using peas and cauliflower. Prabhupāda was hungry. Other than a little fruit on the plane, all he had eaten that morning was a few spoonfuls of half-cooked *kicchari.* But he showed no signs of agitation or impatience. Rather, his mood, both when asking for Kārttikeya and requesting Sudāmā to make *samosās,* was personal and affectionate, befitting exchanges between a spiritual master and his disciples.

## Next Business

Prabhupāda then spoke to Karandhara, the head of the Los Angeles temple and a BBT trustee. Karandhara was one of Prabhupāda’s main managers. He had arrived the previous evening.

**Prabhupāda:** Karandhara Prabhu, how do you like this place?  
**Karandhara:** It’s nice, Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, for spiritual advancement it is a nice place.

\* \* \*

Could Prabhupāda have been hinting that the temple, hidden in the remote Japanese countryside, hours from Tokyo, was peaceful and conducive for one’s own Kṛṣṇa conscious advancement, but not practical for Japan’s only preaching base?

## Exchanges with Karandhara

**Prabhupāda:** You have reached here last night?  
**Karandhara:** Yes. Here are some gifts from the devotees in Los Angeles.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, what is this? Something sweet?  
**Karandhara:** Yes, Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** You open it. Open it. What is this?  
**Karandhara:** That is sandalwood pulp.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, so many letters.  
**Śyāmasundara:** Checks. These are checks, Śrīla Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** That’s nice. When you bring checks, I am very glad. \[*Chuckles.*\] So how much you have deposited?  
**Karandhara:** In which account?  
**Prabhupāda:** My account. So in Los Angeles is everything all right?  
**Karandhara:** Yes, Prabhupāda. All the different envelopes are from the different departments—Spiritual Sky, Mail Order, ISKCON Media.  
**Prabhupāda:** So you keep it in this envelope. Then I shall see.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda appreciated the affectionate gifts of his Los Angeles disciples—sandalwood paste, *sandeśa,* and letters, but Prabhupāda appreciated most the offerings of money with which he could spread the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

A critic may think that Prabhupāda’s motive was to take money from his disciples, but Prabhupāda was interested only in spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness around the world. He needed money. Prabhupāda never hoarded money nor used it for his own benefit. He was eager to recover money from *māyā* and use it in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

## Prasādam Distribution

**Prabhupāda:** You distribute that *prasādam.* Give everyone.  
**Devotee:** Is this a *laḍḍu*?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. I will also take little. Now distribute. \[*Pause.*\] Oh, you take more. Why little, little? Give more. Give me also little more.  
**Trivikrama:** *Jaya*!  
**Prabhupāda:** Mmm. It is very nice.  
**Devotee:** Prabhupāda distributes mercy to the world.  
**Prabhupāda:** Who has made it?  
**Karandhara:** Dhruvānanda.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh. He is good cook?  
**Karandhara:** Yes.  
**Prabhupāda:** Mmm. He has made nice.  
**Sudāmā:** Shall I save one for later for you?  
**Prabhupāda:** No, no saving. \[*Laughter.*\] Give me immediately. \[*Laughter.*\] Give them.  
**Romaharṣaṇa:** Prabhupāda? Your Divine Grace? I have a question. I was always under the impression or understanding from your teachings that one never eats in front of the Deities. Can you please explain that?  
**Śyāmasundara:** He says that he always understood that one should not eat in front of the Deity. What is the difference?  
**Prabhupāda:** Difference is generally we should not eat before the Deity. But there is another injunction: *prasādaḥ prāptir mātreṇa bhoktavyaḥ.* As soon as *prasādam* comes, you should immediately eat it. All the devotees laughed as Prabhupāda ate the remaining part of his *laḍḍu.*

\* \* \*

Caitanya Mahāprabhu and His associates were fond of taking *prasādam;* they tasted the sweetness of Kṛṣṇa consciousness by eating Kṛṣṇa’s food remnants. Prabhupāda also relished *kṛṣṇa-prasādam,* and he immediately honored and appreciated the fine taste of the sweets Karandhara brought from Los Angeles.

Romaharṣaṇa was an eccentric devotee living in Tokyo. The devotees were shocked by his question that seemed to indicate that perhaps Prabhupāda was acting inappropriately or even offensively by eating in front of the Deities. But Prabhupāda simply laughed and counter-quoted a verse from the *Padma Purāṇa.* When Prabhupāda popped the *laḍḍu* into his mouth after quoting the verse, the disturbance the devotees felt by the question vanished and they felt at once both satisfied and joyful.

## To Go or Not to Go?

A few days later, Prabhupāda sat on the *vyāsāsana* and lectured. At the end of the lecture, he remarked that devotees should want to leave the material world and go back to Kṛṣṇaloka. When I heard this, I remembered Sri Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s prayer, *janmani janmanīśvare bhavatād bhaktir ahaitukī tvayi.* Lord Caitanya did not want liberation. He only wanted to perform devotional service birth after birth. Thinking these exalted thoughts, I said, “Prabhupāda, we don’t want to go back to Godhead. We only want to serve you.”

Prabhupāda looked up, “Don’t make me stay here to accept your service.”

“You’re already there, Prabhupāda,” I whispered to him.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda pierced my imitative allegiance to eternal service. He knew me. He knew that I had not understood the depth of love for Kṛṣṇa that a devotee who has imbibed the eternal serving spirit experiences. Only based on that love does the experience of decrying liberation have transcendental meaning.

## A Service for Bhūrijana

Prabhupāda walked each morning from his small, rented, Japanese cottage to the nearby temple, his cane indenting the rocky soil. When he entered the temple room, he leaned his cane against the wall and walked toward his *vyāsāsana.*

I followed directly behind Prabhupāda and made sure that his cane didn’t fall over. If it began to fall, I would smile, catch it, and stand it upright again.

\* \* \*

I derived great happiness from this simple service to Prabhupāda’s cane. I admit at times praying for it to fall just so I could catch it. I continued serving Prabhupāda’s cane throughout his visit, even when we later visited Kobe. There, Prabhupāda would walk up the stairs to his apartment and drop his cane as he grabbed hold of the stair railing. I would catch the cane and carry it up the stairs.

How satisfying it is to serve with affection.

## “Let Us Enjoy”

One day we sat with Prabhupāda while Śyāmasundara read him his mail. The first letter was from Devānanda, a devotee who had joined ISKCON after Prabhupāda’s visit to the Morningstar Ranch in 1967. Devānanda later took *sannyāsa* and traveled with Prabhupāda in 1970 from the West Coast of America through Japan and to India. By the time Prabhupāda received this letter from him, he was confused and had again started taking intoxication.

Śyāmasundara tried to explain the gist of Devānanda’s letter.

**Śyāmasundara:** He’s saying that he had an argument with the devotee sitting behind him and he explains what went on. But not much of it seems to make sense. Should I go on?  
**Prabhupāda:** \[*Smiling.*\] Yes, let us enjoy.

Śyāmasundara then read the letter, which rambled on as Devānanda described the details of a trivial and inconclusive argument he had with another devotee. With Prabhupāda’s permission, we laughed at Devānanda’s letter, and Prabhupāda laughed along with us.

\* \* \*

The old saying, “You’ve got to laugh to keep from crying,” was appropriate here. Devānanda was not the first of Prabhupāda’s leading disciples to fall victim to *māyā*, and Prabhupāda had on occasion shed tears at his disciples’ leaving Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Joking about Devānanda’s difficulties was a unique mood, and Prabhupāda allowed us to see how *māyā* had made a fool of Devānanda.

Although Prabhupāda had many times attempted to help Devānanda, now, thousands of miles away, Devānanda was again taking intoxication. Prabhupāda could do little but humorously place Devānanda’s example before his other disciples as a lesson.

## Who Should Surrender?

Prabhupāda left the temple room after class and made his way back to his cottage. A few disciples trailed behind. A cold rain fell as we walked in single file close to the temple building, watching water drizzle off its eaves.

I was walking directly behind Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda, still walking, said to me, “Trivikrama can go to the Philippines. What do you think?”

I thought fast. Prabhupāda was responding to my request that we find someone to accompany Sudāmā Vipra Swami to open a temple in Manila. Knowing the nature of the Philippines, I didn’t think it wise that a *sannyāsī* go alone, and since Prabhupāda didn’t think much of my suggestion that Nanda Kumāra go, he made his own recommendation. But he asked my opinion.

“No, Prabhupāda, I don’t think he’s the right person.”

“Why?”

“With two swamis, I think they would have trouble deciding who would surrender to whom.”

Prabhupāda thought for a moment. “It doesn’t make a difference who surrenders to whom.”

\* \* \*

I was surprised at my own boldness in responding to Prabhupāda’s suggestion, but since he had asked, I had to answer according to my realization. Because both *sannyāsīs* had strong personalities and neither could be intimidated, there would be problems. Certainly Prabhupāda could have reprimanded my boldness or simply overruled my objection, but he took my doubt seriously and later suggested a different devotee to help establish ISKCON in Manila.

Prabhupāda’s final comment, however, was the actual philosophical conclusion: “It doesn’t matter who surrenders to whom,” as long as the service goes on and both *guru* and Kṛṣṇa are satisfied.

## Shivering in the Cold

In Asakawa-cho, far from the city of Tokyo, the temple windows were paper and the matted *tatami* floor was cold. As we chanted before Prabhupāda during *maṅgala-ārati,* our breath turned to vapor.

Prabhupāda sat on the red velvet *vyāsāsana.* He looked at me, wrapped in a blanket, dancing and shivering. His soft eyes met mine and he hunched his shoulders in sympathy, “It is so cold, isn’t it?”

\* \* \*

I could understand that as I was inconvenienced by the cold, he was too. Of course, I cannot say that Prabhupāda’s experience was the same as mine; he was an exalted devotee and I was “my body,” but Prabhupāda didn’t like cold weather either.

Prabhupāda’s dislike of the cold expanded my understanding of the mood a great devotee maintains while serving: he will tolerate cold or any other unpleasant circumstances in the service of *guru* and Kṛṣṇa. This sincere and dutiful serving mood is neither mechanical nor automatic. It is a product of devotion and it gradually blossoms into love.

## Stealing a Heart

During Prabhupāda’s transit through Hong Kong, I had heard that he was dissatisfied with ISKCON leadership and had therefore temporarily disbanded the GBC. With boyish naivete and the desire to encourage him, I bought him a 24-karat gold ring and engraved it with the words, *“Jaya* ISKCON.” I presented this ring to Prabhupāda in Hong Kong before we traveled together to Tokyo. Now as I sat with Prabhupāda in Tokyo during his massage, Sudāmā carefully rubbed mustard oil onto Prabhupāda’s chest, back, legs, and hands. Sudāmā then noticed Prabhupāda’s golden *“Jaya* ISKCON” ring.

“Prabhupāda,” he asked, “you have a new ring.”

“Yes.”

“Who gave it to you?” Sudāmā asked, as he massaged the back of Prabhupāda’s neck.

Prabhupāda slowly lifted his head and stared at me. Only the three of us were in the room, and I felt softened by Prabhupāda’s glance.

“Who gave you the ring, Prabhupāda?” Sudāmā repeated.

Prabhupāda kept looking at me and said softly, “Oh, someone.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda could have told Sudāmā that I had given him the ring. Instead he replied, “Oh, someone.” When he said those two words, my heart melted and flowed to his lotus feet in the intimacy of our simple and loving secret.

## A Gift of a Name

While massaging Prabhupāda, Sudāmā mentioned that Prabhupāda had named the Japan *yātrā* “New Gāyā,” after the town in Bihar, India, where Buddha gained enlightenment. Hearing this I joked, “Prabhupāda, if you don’t give our Hong Kong *yātrā* a name, I will get envious.” Prabhupāda looked at me and laughed. “You can call it New Ki Ki Tah. That is a province of Gāyā.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda reciprocated and granted my request. I think I was less interested in a name for the Hong Kong *yātrā* than the gesture of asking Prabhupāda for its name. The name New Ki Ki Tah somehow remained overlooked for the Hong Kong *yātrā.*

## Moments Lost and Gained—a Double Loss

Prabhupāda initiated a Japanese boy and gave him the name Tukārāma. Tukārāma was weak and he immediately wanted to leave the temple and go home. Thinking that a Japanese devotee was rare, I decided to go with him. We traveled to Tokyo and distributed books together. We then shared noodles on the *tatami* mat in his small room in Shinju-ku. I wanted to help him, so I spent the night there and then returned to the temple by midday. Prabhupāda noticed my absence during the morning *Bhāgavatam* class. “Where is Bhūrijana?”

“Preaching,” was the message I had requested Sudāmā to pass on to Prabhupāda.

\* \* \*

Tukārāma never returned. I felt like the Hindu who ate a sparse meal in a Muslim’s home—his belly was still empty and his caste was lost. I neither succeeded in helping Tukārāma nor associated with Prabhupāda. Both the young devotee and the holy moments with Prabhupāda escaped me.

## A Gift of a Moment

Romaharṣaṇa lamented his lack of personal association with Prabhupāda. We went to Prabhupāda’s cottage together and sat silently praying under his closed window. Suddenly, the curtains parted and Prabhupāda faced us through the glass window. “Who is that?” he called.

“Your dogs barking for mercy,” I replied.

The cottage doors slid open and Prabhupāda invited us in. He immediately gave me an assignment to locate his secretary.

\* \* \*

Romaharṣaṇa’s lamentation proved miraculously fruitful. Prabhupāda noticed our prayers, called us in, and then sent me away, leaving a tearful disciple alone with his spiritual master.

## A Lost Moment

Nanda Kumāra was massaging Prabhupāda in the morning sun. I walked by, watching from a distance, and offered my obeisances. Then I noticed Nanda Kumāra waving to me. I stared, interpreted his signal, and walked away.

Later Nanda Kumāra questioned, “I was calling you. Why didn’t you come?”

“Oh no,” I lamented. “I was coming, but I thought you were telling me to go.”

\* \* \*

Another eternal moment stolen by ignorance.

## The Nicest God

Trivikrama Swami was showing me a letter from his younger brother, a Christian, when we heard the buzzer. “Prabhupāda’s calling us,” he said. We both rushed for Prabhupāda’s room.

Prabhupāda sat behind his low desk, smiling and peaceful. Trivikrama Swami took out his letter and showed Prabhupāda the tract his brother had enclosed called “A Hindu turns to Jesus.”

Trivikrama read: “While many Westerners are turning to Hare Kṛṣṇa, here’s a Hindu with a Ph.D. who’s been saved by Jesus.” The tract then quoted the ex-Hindu’s criticism of Indian religion. “They believe in the caste system and that an eternal soul changes from body to body. This is against Christianity and the words of the Holy Bible. Hare Kṛṣṇas, like most Hindus, also worship stone idols of Kṛṣṇa, giving the stone the same respect as God.”

Prabhupāda listened silently. Trivikrama finished and asked Prabhupāda what he thought. Prabhupāda replied, “He is a big Ph.D. and still has not learned anything.” He then reached for a large manila envelope and slipped out an eight-by-ten inch photo. Keeping its back to us, Prabhupāda looked at the picture with devotion. Then he turned the picture over to show us exquisitely adorned Rukmiṇī-Dvārakādīśa. He said proudly, “Anyway, our God is the nicest God!”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda buzzed for us simply to share a gift he had just received from Los Angeles. How could he take the tract’s criticisms seriously when he was taking *darśana* of the “nicest God”—Rukmiṇī-Dvārakādīśa of Los Angeles?

## The Intoxication of Māyā

When I was in Prabhupāda’s association, at times, I felt practically liberated. At other times, however, I felt miserable and in *māyā.* I asked Prabhupāda why this happened. He said, “The intoxication of *māyā* is very strong.”

\* \* \*

An exalted devotee is powerful. His association may award us, despite our disqualification, with a semblance of transcendence. But until our attachment to Krsna is mature, *māyā* is strong enough to pull us down. *Māyā* cannot touch us, however, if we somehow remain always in Krsna’s shelter.

kṛṣṇa——sūrya-sama; māyā haya andhakāra  
yāhāṅ kṛṣṇa, tāhāṅ nāhi māyāra adhikāra

“Kṛṣṇa is compared to sunshine, and *māyā* is compared to darkness. Wherever there is sunshine, there cannot be darkness. As soon as one takes to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, the darkness of illusion (the influence of the external energy) will immediately vanish.  
PURPORT: When a person is Kṛṣṇa conscious, he continuously hears about Kṛṣṇa, thinks about Him, worships Him and serves Him as a devotee. If he remains in Kṛṣṇa consciousness in this way, the darkness of māyā certainly will not be able to touch him.

—*Cc., Madhya* 22-31

On the other hand, those who do not practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness fall victim to *māyā* and suffer regardless of the fortune of their circumstances. Prabhupāda explains in his purport to *Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya* 10.65: “*Māyā* is so strong that Kala Kṛṣṇadāsa left Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s company to join gypsy women. Even though a person may associate with Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, he can be allured by *māyā* and leave the Lord’s company due to his slight independence. … One need only misuse his little independence. Once fallen and separated from the Supreme Personality of Godhead’s association, one becomes a candidate for suffering in the material world.”

## Adding to the Bhāgavatam Class

Prabhupāda asked for a *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* volume from which to give his morning class. I ran to the temple library and from the few books present, chose the single Second Canto chapter, “Answers Citing the Lord’s Version.” Knowing that this chapter contained the four seed verses of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* I thought to please Prabhupāda by my choice of chapters. I also considered that hearing Prabhupāda explain these essential verses would be wonderful. I ran back to Prabhupāda and handed the thin volume to him. Prabhupāda accepted the book from my hand and smiled.

\* \* \*

I was happy to do this service for Prabhupāda and I carefully (and quickly) considered the available books before choosing. At the time, I thought that Prabhupāda was pleased by my choice. In retrospect, however, I think that if Prabhupāda was pleased with anything, it was not my choice of *Bhāgavatams,* but my attempt to make a conscious choice and my quick return.

## Introducing the Daily Bhāgavatam Śloka

Prabhupāda first introduced the system of devotees chanting the *Bhāgavatam ślokas* before class in Japan in 1972. The system he introduced is basically the same we still practice in ISKCON today, but with two differences.

Prabhupāda started on the first day with the first verse of the chapter. He read and taught us the *śloka,* which was the subject of his morning’s class. On the second day, he chanted the first verse line by line and we responded in unison. Then he taught us the second verse. In addition, all devotees were expected to chant the *ślokas* individually as well as in unison.

On the third day, Prabhupāda chanted the first two *ślokas* line by line and we responded in unison. He then taught us the third *śloka.* Day after day we chanted all the previous verses line by line before learning the day’s verse.

\* \* \*

By chanting each Sanskrit *śloka* every day we naturally memorized the *ślokas.* To this day, I more or less remember the opening *ślokas* of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* Canto Two, Chapter Nine, and the sound of all the *ślokas* that we covered in the chapter remains familiar. Śatadhanya Prabhu, who was also present, offers the following remembrances:

One day, Prabhupāda was sitting on the *vyāsasana.* He became very upset, and speaking loudly, described how he had gone through all the trouble to include the Sanskrit *ślokas* and transliterations in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* but the devotees were neither showing any interest nor learning the correct Sanskrit pronunciation. In short, they were ignoring the Sanskrit he had placed within his books. He became adamant and said that from now on everyone in ISKCON must learn to pronounce correctly and understand the Sanskrit *ślokas.* For the next three weeks, every morning in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class, he called upon each devotee, requesting us to chant the *ślokas* aloud. The thing that made it unique was that Prabhupāda would take ten or fifteen minutes with one person and personally teach him how to pronounce every single Sanskrit syllable in the *śloka.* In that way Prabhupada went around the temple room and personally taught the seven or eight people every day. We were studying the Second Canto, “Answers Citing the Lord’s Version,” and I remember, even to this day, the *ślokas* which Prabhupada drilled into us intensely day after day. In fact, we would all get tutored by Pradyumna during the day so that the next morning we would not be embarrassed. We were finally able to correctly chant the *ślokas* in that chapter.  
Before Prabhupada left, he announced that there would be a final exam in the temple room. All of us came at class time and Prabhupāda called on each devotee to recite the entire chapter in Sanskrit. Then Prabhupada went through all the trouble to make corrections and explain how we did. This went on for hours, and he told each of us whether our recitation was good or more work had to be done. Somehow I was last because I was sitting on the side opposite the *vyāsasana,* so when everyone had spoken, I took my turn. I recited the whole chapter. Then Prabhupada looked at me and said, “First prize.”

## Knees Down!

One morning as he spoke, Prabhupāda saw Śyāmasundara sitting with his knees up. Prabhupāda spoke sharply, “Sit properly!”

\* \* \*

Sometimes I explain this incident to devotees sitting improperly during the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class. Inside I smile, remembering Prabhupāda’s “Sit properly!” and feel I am acting on his behalf.

## Morning Walks

Prabhupāda took his morning walks down a reddish dirt road, past his cottage, past the temple, and onward through a slightly uphill, wooded area. It was a forty-minute route.

Some morning walks were active, with Prabhupāda instigating debate and questions. Other walks were more meditative with only two or three devotees walking in silence, relishing the sound of Prabhupāda's cane digging into the earth as he walked.

## Uncovering a Flaw

On one morning walk, Prabhupāda discussed an ISKCON controversy with Karandhara. Kīrtandnanda Swami had written Prabhupāda suggesting that devotees only wear devotional clothing when distributing books. He spoke of his experiences that devotees wearing nondevotee clothing tended to fall into nondevotee behavior. Karandhara and others had taken the stand that Kirtanānanda’s suggestion, if implemented, would significantly decrease the number of books distributed. Prabhupāda was impressed by the figures put forward by Karandhara showing the expected rate of decrease in book distribution. As Prabhupāda and Karandhara discussed the points back and forth, Sudāmā, Trivikrama Swami, and I gathered closer to listen.

At one point in the discussion, Karandhara offered an alternative by which books could be distributed, but the amount of money collected would diminish. I interjected, “We’re not interested in money.” Prabhupāda immediately reprimanded me. “So we are not interested in money? Without money how can Kṛṣṇa consciousness spread? This is hippie philosophy. Money is Lakṣmī and is meant for Krsna’s service. This is nonsense.”

\* \* \*

Although I had meant to say that devotees are not interested in money outside of Krsna’s service, I could not hide from Prabhupāda a flaw in my consciousness. I remember one initiation where Prabhupāda named a disciple, “Ramānayana” (one who brings Ramā, the goddess of fortune), and then ordered him to regain Lakṣmī, Sītā-devī, from the bogus spiritualists who, like Rāvaṇa, imitated holy men to steal Sītā-devī from Rāma. Prabhupāda was both fully spiritual and completely practical.

Prabhupāda concluded that devotees could continue wearing nondevotee dress—but the dress of gentlemen, not hippie dress—while preaching and distributing books. Prabhupāda explains his conclusions in the purports to the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*:

In our Krsna consciousness movement, the tactic of dressing oneself like an ordinary *karmī* is necessary because everyone in the demoniac kingdom is against the Vaiṣṇava teachings. Krsna consciousness is not at all to the liking of the demons of the present age. As soon as they see a Vaiṣṇava dressed in saffron garments with beads on his neck and *tilaka* on his forehead, they are immediately irritated. … The greater demons, like Hiraṇyakaśipu, are always prepared to chastise the Vaiṣṇavas, and they try to make arrangements so that Vaiṣṇavas will not come to sell their books and preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Thus what was done by Hiraṇyakaśipu long, long ago is still being done. That is the way of materialistic life. Demons or materialists do not at all like the advancement of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and they try to hinder it in many ways. Yet the preachers of Kṛṣṇa consciousness must go forward—in their Vaiṣṇava dress or any other dress—for the purpose of preaching. Cāṇakya Paṇḍita says that if an honest person deals with a great cheater, it is necessary for him to become a cheater also, not for the purpose of cheating but to make his preaching successful.

—*Bhāg.* 7.5.7

Sometimes the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement sends its representative *sannyāsīs* to foreign countries where the *daṇḍa* and *kamaṇḍalu* are not very much appreciated. We send our preachers in ordinary dress to introduce our books and philosophy. Our only concern is to attract people to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We may do this in the dress of *sannyāsīs* or in the regular dress of gentlemen. Our only concern is to spread interest in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

—*Bhāg.* 7.13.9

And again in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta:*

Sometimes members of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness—especially in the Western countries—find it difficult to approach people to distribute books because people are unfamiliar with the traditional saffron robes of the devotees. The devotees have therefore inquired whether they can wear European and American dress before the general public. From the instructions given to King Pratāparudra by Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya, we can understand that we may change our dress in any way to facilitate our service. When our members change their dress to meet the public or to introduce our books, they are not breaking the devotional principles. The real principle is to spread this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, and if one has to change into regular Western dress for this purpose, there should be no objection.

—Cc., *Madhya* 14.5

## A Morning March with Prabhupāda

On another morning walk, about fifteen devotees waited by the temple to join Prabhupāda. As he walked past, Prabhupāda met the assembly’s greeting of *“Jaya* Prabhupāda!” and obeisances with a silent glance. Suddenly, Sañjaya turned on a portable tape recorder and, like marching music, Yamunā-devi sang, *“govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi.*”

Prabhupāda stopped, turned, and planted his cane firmly on the ground. “There is one Bengali proverb.” He paused as everyone stopped walking. The tape recorder was silenced. “Eat alone, study in twos, and walk in threes.” Prabhupāda turned and continued his walk.

\* \* \*

All the devotees wanted Prabhupāda’s association, yet he obviously wanted a casual and peaceful morning walk. I lagged behind and thought of turning back, but then I realized that I was the only one who wouldn’t be with Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda appreciated our desire to be with him, so he tolerated the entourage tagging along, although he continued the walk in silence. After that day, though, few devotees went on walks with Prabhupāda. No one wanted to inconvenience him.

## A Snake, a Rope, a Scorpion, or a Crab?

One morning as Prabhupāda, Śyāmasundara, and I walked, a small creature crawled across the road. Prabhupāda pointed at the crablike creature with the tip of his cane. “A scorpion. Be careful.”

We continued walking, as Śyāmasundara turned and whispered in my ear, “It’s a land crab.”

\* \* \*

A scorpion or a crab? A snake or a rope? I had never seen a scorpion, but this creature looked quite like a crab. Prabhupāda explains the perfection and knowledge of an exalted devotee, a spiritual master, as being on a completely different level than scorpions, crabs, or spectacles-free eyesight in the following conversation, which took place during the 1975 Gaura-Pūrṇimā festival in Māyāpur.

**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Pure devotee does not aspire anything, simply to be engaged in loving service to the Lord, wherever it may be. It doesn’t matter.  
**Jayādvaita:** They know everything and they are perfect in everything. But sometimes, from our material viewpoint, we see some discrepancies. Just like we think that …  
**Prabhupāda:** Because material viewpoint. The viewpoint is wrong; therefore you find discrepancies.  
**Jayādvaita:** So we should think that we have the defect.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. *Ācārya* is explained, *bhakti-saṁsanaḥ:* “One who’s preaching the cult of devotional service, he’s *ācārya.*” Then why should you find any discrepancy?  
**Jayādvaita:** Because we see … For instance, sometimes the *ācārya* may seem to forget something or not to know something, so from our point of view, if someone has forgotten, that is an imperfection.  
**Prabhupāda:** Then you do not understand. *Ācārya* is not God, omniscient. He is servant of God. His business is to preach *bhakti* cult. That is *ācārya.*  
**Jayādvaita:** And that is the perfection.  
**Prabhupāda:** That is the perfection. Hare Kṛṣṇa.  
**Jayādvaita:** So we have a misunderstanding about what perfection is?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Perfection is here, how he is preaching *bhakti* cult. That’s all.  
**Satsvarūpa:** Prabhupāda, in one purport in the *Bhagavad-gītā,* you write that a disciple of a bona fide spiritual master is supposed to know everything.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, if he follows the spiritual master.  
**Satsvarūpa:** But how could he know? … What does that mean, “everything?”  
**Prabhupāda:** Everything means whatever his *guru* knows, he should know, that much. Not like God, everything. Within his limit, that’s all. If he tries to understand whatever his *guru* has said, that much is “everything.” Otherwise, “everything” does not mean that we know everything, like God, like Kṛṣṇa. That is not possible. If he regularly chants and follows the regulative principles, follows the orders of *guru,* then he knows everything. That’s all. Not very much … knows everything, then what is the use of reading books when he knows everything? You cannot expect anyone to know like Kṛṣṇa, everything.  
**Jayādvaita:** Kṛṣṇa says in the *Bhagavad-gītā* that one who knows Him knows everything.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Because if he knows that Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, then he knows everything. That’s all. Not that he should know as Kṛṣṇa. *Yasmin vijñāte sarvam eva vijñātam* … If he accepts Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, the Absolute Truth, then he knows everything. That is finish.  
**Jayādvaita:** That knowledge itself is complete.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes.  
**Satsvarūpa:** If there’s some material information that such a person doesn’t know, that’s not really knowledge anyway.  
**Prabhupāda:** I did not follow.  
**Satsvarūpa:** If he doesn’t know how many people live in …  
**Jayādvaita:** Just like Gaurakiśora could not write. So it appeared that he did not, there was something that he did not know, although he knew Kṛṣṇa.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. He knows everything. Otherwise how Bhaktisiddhānta accepted him as *guru*? He knows Kṛṣṇa. That’s all.  
**Nalinī-kānta:** What the spiritual master says, that is also perfect?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Because he does not say anything concocted. Whatever he says, he says from *śāstra* and *guru.*

## A Moment Alone

One morning as Prabhupāda, Śyāmasundara, and I were nearing the temple after a morning walk, Śyāmasundara split off to make a telephone call. Prabhupāda and I continued walking toward his cottage. Prabhupāda stopped. We stood together, surveying the rugged Japanese landscape with its scrub, barren, reddish earth, and pine trees on the small hills. The moment was sweet and silent; the two of us were alone and my heart filled with affection for Prabhupāda. “I just want to serve you, Prabhupāda,” I spoke softly.

Without speaking, Prabhupāda’s glance focused straight on me for a few seconds. “Did he hear? Did he understand?” I wondered. Prabhupāda continued his silence and we walked toward his cottage.

\* \* \*

Words spoken in a sweet and intimate moment must bear the test of time.

## Kobe, Japan—1972

Kobe, Japan—1972

## Our Morning Program of Praise

Prabhupāda’s stay in Tokyo was broken by an engagement in Kobe, 270 miles south of Tokyo. In Kobe, Prabhupāda lived in the third-floor apartment of our host, the president of the Kobe Hindu Association. Every morning we held a quiet morning program. Prabhupāda sat on a cloth spread over the upholstered couch and chanted the *Gurv-aṣṭaka* prayers followed by a soft *kīrtana.* Once, he played the *mṛdaṅga* as he chanted the *Gurv-aṣṭaka* to his Guru Mahārāja, his eyes closed, the early morning sun brightening his golden complexion.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was grave during these morning hours. After the *Gurv-aṣṭaka* prayers, we were inspired to chant *“Jaya* Prabhupāda, *Jaya* Prabhupāda” to praise him. Prabhupāda’s response was to also chant *“Jaya* Prabhupāda, *Jaya* Prabhupāda” to glorify his own Guru Mahārāja.

## Walking Past Hypocrisy

The devotees present—Śyāmasundara, Bhānu, Pradyumna, Sudāmā, and I—went on several morning walks through the Kobe suburbs with Prabhupāda. On one morning walk, he saw a sign for the Kobe SPCA and asked us what SPCA stood for. Sudāmā answered, “The Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals.” Prabhupāda retorted, “No, it means the Society for the *Promotion* of Cruelty to Animals!”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda condemned the hypocrisy of a Society whose professed purpose was to eradicate cruelty to animals, but who neither insisted upon a vegetarian diet for its members nor opposed the organized murder of animals in slaughterhouses.

## The Chopping Technique

Some of Prabhupāda’s morning classes were less formal and more like preaching-conversations. The topics varied. One morning he said that many apparently sophisticated women who had big dogs as pets were actually engaging in sex with their dogs. He used this as an example to show the degradation of modern society. He said that syphilis had entered human society through women having sex with animals. Other topics were more standard, with Prabhupāda crushing our reliance on modern material scientific concepts.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was often extremely heavy when describing modern civilization and the so-called advancement of modern technology. Invectives such as “rascal,” “cheater,” and “nonsense” abounded in his appraisal of society’s leaders. He called his preaching style “the chopping technique” and said his Guru Mahārāja had also used it. Once Prabhupāda mentioned that some of his Godbrothers had criticizedhis Guru Mahārāja’s preaching style. “Those who criticized fell down,” he added.

## Crushing Chance

God’s way: chance comes from necessity

During one conversation, Prabhupāda argued with the devotees that chance does not really exist. He explained how a man’s necessity arises, and the needed article comes to a deserving man, if he works. The process that induces the article to come is not chance, but Kṛṣṇa’s supervision as Supersoul. Because the ultimate cause is not directly perceivable by a materialist, they foolishly name chance, not God, as the cause.

**Prabhupāda:** If the chance comes as soon as the necessity is there, then we have to admit immediately God.  
**Śyāmasundara:** Yes.  
**Prabhupāda:** Because in the *Bhagavad-gītā* we hear *mattaḥ smṛtir jñānam apohanaṁ,* that God is in everyone’s heart as Supersoul. Now, I am thinking of getting something. So God knows immediately that “He wants to have this,” so He gives me the necessary thing which appears to me as chance, without knowing God. The things are supplied by God because He is giving me all facilities to enjoy this material world to my heart’s content by supplying all the ingredients. That is the material condition. So these foolish persons are taking it as chance, but it is not chance. God is omnipotent. As soon as He understands that I want this, He gives me some facility so that I get it. So it is not chance. It is by arrangement of superior authority. But because they are atheists, they have no sense of God consciousness, they are taking as chance, that necessity creates that chance; automatically it is coming. Not automatically, but all of a sudden it falls … Just like I am hungry, I want some food. So Kṛṣṇa knows it that you want … Some way or other, the food comes to me. So it is the arrangement of Kṛṣṇa, but I see it is chance: “I was hungry and by chance the food has come.” That is my less intelligence. It is not chance; it is plan. Otherwise you cannot adjust the meaning of chance in that way, that as soon as there is necessity, immediately the opportune chance comes before us.  
**Śyāmasundara:** They say, “Well, it’s my luck,” or, “My bad luck.”  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, they say. So this “luck,” as soon as you say “luck,” there must be somebody who is giving you the luck, good luck or bad luck.  
**Śyāmasundara:** One man may desire something very badly, and his whole life long he will not get it. He will always say, “I am so unlucky.”  
**Prabhupāda:** Because he is not fit to get it, so God does not supply it. So we do not take anything as chance. We take everything as plan. But because God’s omnipotency is so subtle, we can’t see how things happen. Therefore we say,”It is a chance, chance of physical arrangement.” Just like in the airport, as soon as I step on the door it becomes opened. It is not chance. A child will see it is as chance: “Oh, how it is? I wanted to go and the door is already open.” He takes it as chance. That is poor fund of knowledge. There is arrangement, nice arrangement, electrical arrangement. So to a poor fund of knowledge it becomes a chance, and to the mind it is not chance; it is arranged by higher authority.

\* \* \*

Earlier during Prabhupāda’s stay, while he was in his small cottage in Tokyo, Trivikrama Swami entered Prabhupāda’s room when Prabhupāda was alone. Prabhupāda was quietly reading Kṛṣṇa book. “See what I do when I am free?” Prabhupāda remarked. Prabhupāda was a devotee of Kṛṣṇa. He had a great attachment to direct *kṛṣṇa-kathā.* Then why did he spend so much of his time poking holes in contemporary thought?

Faith in *guru* and *śāstra* is the foundation of *kṛṣṇa-bhakti.* Simultaneously maintaining faith in modern, materialistic science and technology and advancing in Kṛṣṇa consciousness is impossible. Prabhupāda had to demolish the forest of our unquestioned beliefs, our cultural conditioning, before the *bhakti* seeds he had planted in our hearts could grow and blossom.

Under Kṛṣṇa’s control

**Prabhupāda:** That plan is Kṛṣṇa’s. That is said in the *Bhagavad-gītā, mayādhyakṣeṇa prakṛtiḥ sūyate sa-carācaram:* “Under My plan, under My superintendence, the nature is working. The changes of the world are going on for that reason.” *Hetunānena kaunteya jagad viparivartate*: “All these changes are taking placeon account of My supervision.” So there is no question of chance. It is all planned by the Supreme. *Daiva netreṇa,* by superior arrangement.  
**Śyāmasundara:** Doesn’t necessity mean plan?  
**Prabhupāda:** Necessity means for a foolish person like me, I want something. That is my necessity and God supplies me. “Man proposes, God disposes.” And that reception, or that achievement, without it being explained by me, I take it as a chance. Because I cannot explain it. Just like the same example: the flower is fructifying. We are saying chance because we do not see how the working is going on.  
**Śyāmasundara:** You defined miracle like that once before.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. So there is nothing like miracle. Everything is done in so subtle way that we cannot understand. We take it as chance. The same example: just like a child steps before the door and it opens. He thinks, “Oh, by chance the door is opened.” But it is not by chance. It is a plan.

\* \* \*

After chopping down our blind faith in chance being the ultimate causal principle, Prabhupāda next established the more reasonable premise that ordered events occur not by chance, but by arrangement.

The architect of fortune

**Prabhupāda:** The proprietor is living there. The servants are living there. The cats and dogs are also living there. The trees and plants are also living there, and insects and microbes and snakes and rats. So many living entities in the same building. Why they are different? What is the answer? They have been given the same chance of living in the same house, born in the same house. As the proprietor’s son is born in the same house, these also, they are also taking birth the same place. Why they are denied the same advantage? And if they are denied, who has denied it? What is the answer to this question? They are all living entities.  
**Bhūrijana:** The difference is that the human living entities have higher intelligence because of their body.  
**Prabhupāda:** That is the question, that “Who has given you high intelligence and not to the rats and cats?”  
**Pradyumna:** You said in one place, “Man is the architect of his own happiness and distress.”  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Yes, that is an axiomatic truth even by the modern man. Yes, that “Man is the architect of his own fortune.” So as soon as there is work to make your fortune, then there must be a person to decide to give you a fortunate position. Just like in an establishment, so many men are working, but there is a president. He is considering the work file, “How this man has worked?” And he is being promoted, his salary is being increased, and somebody is degraded, no promotion, rather, transferred to some other place. The natural conclusion is when there are so many varieties of life in our presence and, although in the same place, they haven’t got the same facility, so there must be somebody who decides on this point. So how you can deny God? Our point is the Supreme Person, the president, who decides on this fact, He is God. What is the opposite answer?  
**Pradyumna:** They would say that you are in your position and they are in their position just by chance, just like …  
**Prabhupāda:** That is nonsense. This is sheer nonsense. There is nothing by chance. What is that chance? By chance one is becoming millionaire, and by chance one is becoming a cockroach. What is that chance? Explain that chance. It is evasive. It is most foolish reply, “chance.” We have got this nice apartment. Is it by chance?  
**Pradyumna:** No.  
**Prabhupāda:** Then?  
**Bhūrijana:** There’s never an example of chance.  
**Prabhupāda:** This is all nonsense. People are befooled by all this philosophy.  
**Bhūrijana:** Albert Einstein said, “I cannot believe that the highest material principle is chance.” He’s a material scientist. He said, “I cannot believe … “  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Actually, if one is learned, scientific, he must admit. He must admit, unless he is a lunatic, rascal. He will say all these nonsense things, “chance.” Why chance? What is taking place within your practical experience by chance? If by prearrangement we did not come here, then who would care for us? Even on the street we could not lie down. Nobody would allow. The police will arrest. “Who are these men?” How do you say chance? Everything is done by prearrangement. Chance is an explanation given by the rascals and fools. They are not sane men. There cannot be anything by chance. We got up on the train, and the train is running, and it is all chance? There is a huge management behind the train. Therefore we are comfortably seated, and we come to the destination right on time. All these are chances?  
**Śyāmasundara:** Their answer is that there was a necessity to go to the city, so we would have tried hundreds of different ways to come here, and by chance, eventually we would have found …  
**Prabhupāda:** No. We have not tried hundreds of others. There was a plan.  
**Sudāmā:** But even where does … In my mind, if I hear the word “chance,” where does chance, how does chance come about, if there is such a thing?  
**Prabhupāda:** No. The rascal says that I am trying in so many ways; one of them succeeds by chance. But I am not working in so many ways. We had a plan: to come here to preach. So according to that plan, we arranged with this man, and it is not chance. It is all prearranged. Where is that “I am trying this way or that way?” We are going to preach. There is a plan. So our men go before my reaching there and they make nice arrangement, nice apartment. Then they receive me. These are not chances. This is all prearranged plan.

\* \* \*

To bring the argument from “plan” to the necessity of a “planner” who enforces that the plan is a logical step. My next comment brought out a doubt. I was a casualty of Kali-yuga’s educational system and I thought that evolution’s basic principle was change, not chance. Things necessarily change and that change is done by chance. I offered this argument to Prabhupāda.

Chance, Change, or Kṛṣṇa?

**Bhūrijana:** But they don’t acknowledge the plan.  
**Prabhupāda:** Why they don’t acknowledge? Everything is being done by plan. The rascal who is speaking like that, he is educated by a plan, by his parents. And therefore he is able now to talk nonsense and get the Nobel Prize. His education was planned.  
**Sudāmā:** Just like his book was planned, so now he is given the Nobel Prize. He won’t say, “By chance I won.”  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. His plan to misguide the people, that is his plan.  
**Bhūrijana:** They say that change is the principle. They say that change is the highest principle and out of so many different changes …  
**Prabhupāda:** No, how the change takes place? You are changing. You are changing from your childhood to boyhood, boyhood to youthhood. So there is a plan. Unless there is plan, why one child does not by chance become immediately old? What the nonsense will reply? Let the rascal reply to this. That here is a chance, that one child immediately becomes old man, by chance. Why there is process? This is plan. So you should have depth of knowledge, otherwise you will be carried away by these rascals. We cannot be carried away by these rascals. We never so easily believed that they are going to the moon planet. You see? We have to scrutinize everything. Yes. That is brahminical qualification. A *brāhmaṇa* will not accept anything simply because it is said by some rascal. A *śūdra* will accept because he has no intelligence. That is the difference between *brāhmaṇa* and *śūdra.* It is not a caste system.  
It is class—*guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ,* division of high qualities and actual activities according to that quality. They misinterpreted. Because by the influence of Kali-yuga everyone is *śūdra,* so he does not know what is the actually brahminical qualification. Therefore there is competition: “Why this man? I am as good as he is, and why he should be called *brāhmaṇa*?Why he should be given greater facility?” So actually it has happened. A so-called *brāhmaṇa,* caste *brāhmaṇa,* he is working his intelligence like *śūdra,* and he is claiming, by birthright, *brāhmaṇa.* There must be protest. This has happened. Otherwise, that division is perfect, *guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ.* Anyone who comes to brahminical quality, he becomes *brāhmaṇa.* That is the injunction of the *śāstra.* Kṛṣṇa says *guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ.* You have no qualification, you do not work according to your quality, and why you are claiming to be *brāhmaṇa*? That is self-evident. *Guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ.* He never said by birth, never said. *Kalau śūdra-sambhavaḥ:* “In this age, Kali-yuga, all *śūdras.*” Therefore they accept everything cheaply and at once, the *śūdras.*

**Śyāmasundara:** Cheaply and at once?  
**Prabhupāda:** At once. Yes. The newspaper said that “Mr. Such-and-Such went to moon planet.” Oh, immediately believe. See? A newspaper, a ten-cent newspaper. And in the *Bhagavad-gītā* Kṛṣṇa says, *yānti deva-vratā devān:* “One can go to the demigods’ planets by worshiping them. You can go, *yānti deva-vratā devān,* as others have gone. Similarly, one can come to Me by worshiping Me.” *Mad-yājino ‘pi yānti mām.* So they never worshiped Candra, and how they can go to Candra’s planet, the moon planet? Then Kṛṣṇa is false. Kṛṣṇa is imperfect. They become perfect. They are defying Kṛṣṇa’s instruction. They have gone to moon planet. Then our whole propaganda, Kṛṣṇa consciousness, becomes bogus. Therefore I always protest.  
**Sudāmā:** They have not gone.  
**Prabhupāda:** They have not gone. We have got our tests. I am speaking from the very beginning, “They have not gone.” And practically you see, even if they have gone, what utility they have made? They are simply planning, again planning. “We shall get petrol from there. We shall have defense from there.” Simply bluffing, simply bluffing. The Americans will go to the moon planet to defend his country from the Russians. Just see. And we have to believe all these nonsense proposals. What defense they will do from there? Is it not the proposal?

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s reply was simple: change is not random, it is also by plan. He thus was training his disciples in independent, clear thinking. He didn’t want us to remain faithful, foolish believers in the tenets of modern science. If we were to be of use to him and make advancement within this life, we would have to see beyond the trickery of modern science and faithfully preach the Vedic viewpoint.

At the time, I couldn’t deeply accept Prabhupāda’s arguments, but later when we returned to our Asakawa-cho temple, Prabhupāda continued dismembering our blind faith in Evolution. One morning, Śyāmasundara and I walked with Prabhupāda. A steep decline dropped left off the dirt road; to the right rose a hill topped with rows of pine.

“Just see,” said Prabhupāda, pointing with his cane. “All trees are facing the sun.” He stopped to observe and allow the point to sink in. “There is nothing that is chance in creation.” Prabhupāda continued, “Evolutionary theory is nonsense. If chance is law, then why at least one tree is not pointing away from the sun?”

Prabhupāda’s simple and clear example destroyed my conditioned faith in the theory of Evolution.

## Kobe Part II: Like a Lion in the Chase

Kobe Part II: Like a Lion in the Chase

## Our Prabhupāda, Our Hero

Our journey to Kobe contained a dramatic pastime in which Prabhupāda was the hero. I’ve written it into a short play. This play is a 95% accurate description of the pastime. I couldn’t, however, find a record of the lecture Prabhupāda gave in Kobe, so I edited one from *Back to Godhead* magazine which was in a similar mood. I changed a few time sequences and added detail and dialogue to make the story read as a play. I also added some words and anecdotes of Prabhupāda’s which occurred elsewhere, such as a description of the faults of Māyāvāda philosophy that Prabhupāda spoke at New Vrindaban in 1969.

## Scene One, 1972: Tokyo Train Station

\[Bhānu and Bhūrijana enter the train from left.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Here’s seats 86–89. They’re ours. When’s Prabhupāda coming? The train’s supposed to leave in ten minutes.  
**Bhānu:** Don’t worry. I asked Prabhupāda to wait with Sudāmā until we found the seats. He should be here any moment.  
**Bhūrijana:** When will we arrive in Kobe?  
**Bhānu:** It’s about 270 miles and it’ll take about three hours. We should be there by about two o’clock this afternoon.  
**Bhūrijana:** From Tokyo! That’s *fast.* \[*Looks around.*\] This train even *looks* like an airplane.  
**Bhānu:** The Japanese call this train “the Bullet,” the Shin-kan-sen. It goes about 100 miles an hour. It won’t even stop before Kobe.  
**Bhūrijana:** Here comes Prabhupāda now. With Sudāmā—and not a moment to spare. *Jaya* Śrīla Prabhupāda!

\[Prabhupāda enters wearing a garland.\]

**Bhānu:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda!  
**Prabhupāda:** Hare Kṛṣṇa. Thank you. Which seats?

\[Bhānu directs Prabhupāda and the other devotees into a compartment in which two sets of seats face each other.\]

**Bhānu:** You please sit here, Prabhupāda. I’ll sit here. Sudāmā over here, Bhūrijana, quickly. The train is due to leave now.

\[Conductor enters.\]

**Conductor:** Tickets, please. Please sit down. Due to the high speeds, no one is allowed to stand for the entire ride.

\[Conductor moves on. The train begins moving.\]

**Bhānu:** Leaving right on time.  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda, the Japanese technology is so advanced that a train like this can reach a speed of about 100 miles an hour.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, they are going fast, but where are they going? That is the question. Just like the story of the poet Kālidāsa. He is sitting on the end of a tree branch sawing. One passerby approaches him to warn him. “You will fall if you keep up your sawing.” “Go away and stop criticizing. Mind your own business.” Kālidāsa keeps sawing and … and what happens?  
**Bhānu:** Of course he falls.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. The rascal falls and runs and says, “How did you know I would fall? Are you an astrologer?” That is the definition of a rascal. He is going so fast, but he does not know where he is going.  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda, you are like the passerby who tried to help.  
**Prabhupāda:** Therefore I say this rascal modern civilization based on cow-killing and technology is doomed. It will fall.  
**Bhānu:** And they won’t listen to you.  
**Prabhupāda:** This world based on technology is ruining everything. Technology and Māyāvāda philosophy. Because of technology, people have given up their simple living style. Everyone is moving to the city for sense gratification. And because of this nonsense the Māyāvādīs have preached, “I am God. You are also God. All is one. It does not matter what I do. All paths are the same.” \[*Pauses.*\] All nonsense. If we have bought a ticket to Kobe, will we arrive in Calcutta? No. Everyone has become foolish by this rubbish Māyāvāda philosophy. It is nonsense and we must preach strongly.  
**Bhūrijana:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** You young boys should also preach strongly. You must learn the truth—*mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke*—and preach strongly, as I am doing all over the world. Preach the truth just as Kṛṣṇa is speaking in *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* and do not compromise Kṛṣṇa’s words.  
**Bhūrijana:** Our mission is so difficult, Prabhupāda. No one wants to listen.  
**Prabhupāda:** But still we have taken so much risk and have left Vṛndāvana at such an advanced age … traveling constantly all over the world … just to save them.

\[Bhānu gets up and stands to the side.\]

**Bhānu:** Prabhupāda, I think it will be more comfortable for you if you put your lotus feet up on the seat. I don’t mind standing.

\[Prabhupāda puts his feet up.\]

**Sudāmā:** Everyone else your age has long ago retired.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, I am an old man, \[*Laughing.*\] and still …  
**Bhūrijana:** Seventy-six already.  
**Prabhupāda:** But I still keep traveling and preaching. I must. To save them and to train you.  
**Sudāmā:** Uh Prabhupāda?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes?  
**Sudāmā:** Before, when you said that Māyāvāda philosophy has ruined the world …  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes?  
**Sudāmā:** Well, Prabhupāda, that reminded me that I forgot to tell you something.  
**Prabhupāda:** You can speak.  
**Sudāmā:** The program for the Hindu community in Kobe, you know the one that you’ll be speaking at tomorrow night.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes?  
**Sudāmā:** \[*Hesitatingly.*\] Well, by mistake, I mean … uh, on the same program … the same night, I foolishly allowed the Hindu community leader, Mr. Mahesh, to schedule a …  
**Prabhupāda:** Speak up.  
**Sudāmā:** A Māyāvādī *sannyāsī.*  
**Prabhupāda:** What?  
**Sudāmā:** Yes, a Māyāvādī *sannyāsī* will be speaking the same night as you.  
**Bhūrijana:** The same night? Sudāmā, you didn’t!  
**Bhānu:** Before the entire Kobe Hindu community! At the same program as Prabhupāda? You didn’t.  
**Sudāmā:** \[*Apologetically.*\] I did. I didn’t mean it, but I couldn’t help it.  
**Bhānu:** Prabhupāda will never tolerate hearing Māyāvādī philosophy. Imagine Prabhupāda sitting there and a Māyāvādī *sannyāsī* preaching, “You are all God.”  
**Sudāmā:** I couldn’t refuse. That Mr. Mahesh said that if all the members of the Hindu community were satisfied with Prabhupāda’s program, he would consider donating to us one of his apartment buildings for a temple.  
**Bhūrijana:** Sudāmā, I think you have made a great mistake.  
**Prabhupāda:** Satisfy the Hindu community? I will satisfy Kṛṣṇa. \[*Laughing.*\] But don’t worry, Sudāmā has tried his best. You are all still young boys and make mistakes, but you have given up everything, so how can I take offense by your little mistakes?  
**Bhūrijana:** But it’s a *Māyāvādī* that’s speaking.  
**Prabhupāda:** We shall see what is Kṛṣṇa’s plan. We shall see.

\[Conductor walks past and sees Bhānu standing up.\]

**Conductor:** You cannot stand. The train is moving too fast. You must sit down immediately. Take your seat.

\[The devotees all nudge Bhūrijana to be the one to request Prabhupāda to move his feet.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Prabhupāda, the conductor is asking Bhānu to sit down. Could you please move your lotus feet?  
**Prabhupāda:** So the lotus-like devotee can sit down … Hare Kṛṣṇa.  
**All:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda!

## Scene Two, Kobe, the Next Morning

\[Prabhupāda is sitting on the couch in an apartment playing mṛdaṅga. Sudāmā and Bhūrijana are sitting on the floor. Prabhupāda is leading the Gurv-aṣṭaka prayers—“yasya prasādād bhagavat-prasādo.” After chanting, Prabhupāda says the prema-dhvani prayers and is handed a volume of the Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.\]

**Prabhupāda:** Continue reading from the *Bhāgavatam*.  
**Bhūrijana:** *Oṁ namo bhagavate vāsudevāya*. Reading from *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, Canto Two, Chapter Nine, verse 35:

yathā mahānti bhūtāni  
bhūteṣūccāvaceṣv anu  
praviṣṭāny apraviṣṭāni  
tathā teṣu na teṣv aham

Lord Kṛṣṇa is speaking: “O Brahmā, I Myself exist within everything created, and at the same time I am outside of everything.” Should I keep reading the purport, Prabhupāda?

**Prabhupāda:** Yes, keep reading. This is very important.  
**Bhūrijana:** Purport by His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda.  
“The impersonalists perceive that the Supreme Brahman is all-pervading, and they conclude there is no possibility of His personal form. But the mystery of understanding transcendental knowledge is love of Godhead, and one who is surcharged with such transcendental love of Godhead can, without difficulty, see the Personality of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa, in every atom and every object. And at the same time he can see Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, in His own abode, in Goloka Vṛndāvana. This vision can be revealed as follows:

premāñjana-cchurita-bhakti-vilocanena  
santaḥ sadaiva hṛdayeṣu vilokayanti

\[Buzz Buzz Buzzzz. Doorbell rings.\]

**Sudāmā:** I’ll get it. \[Offers obeisances and gets up.\]  
**Bhūrijana**:

yaṁ śyāmasundaram acintya-guṇa-svarupāṁ  
govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi

**Sudāmā:** \[*From off stage.*\] Oh, Mr. Mahesh. So nice to see you. And Mr. Santanam. Yes, Mr. Mahesh, your chauffeur was waiting when the train arrived last night. Thank you. Yes, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s here. Come and meet him.

\[Sudāmā enters with two smiling, wealthy-looking Indians wearing suits and ties. They offer praṇāmas.\]

**Sudāmā:** Mr. Mahesh and Mr. Santanam, this is Śrīla Prabhupāda, the Founder-*ācārya* of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness.  
**Mahesh:** So pleased to meet you, Swamijī.  
**Prabhupāda:** Hare Kṛṣṇa. Please sit down. \[*To Bhūrijana.*\] Continue.  
**Bhūrijana**:  
“I worship the original Personality of Godhead, Govinda, whom the pure devotees, their eyes smeared with the ointment of love of Godhead, always observe within their hearts. This Personality of Godhead is known as Śyāmasundara, Govinda, or Kṛṣṇa, and He possesses all-attractive transcendental qualities.”

**Prabhupāda:** One must strictly follow the process of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, then one will get spiritual knowledge and will one day see Kṛṣṇa face to face. At the present, our knowledge is limited to this body. It is material, and material knowledge means ignorance—the cause of fear and anxiety. But this ignorance can be removed by getting the right knowledge of the self by hearing from the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* We can then become free from material attachment and enter into the kingdom of fearlessness.  
This material world means fearfulness. Just like prisoners are always fearful in a prison house. They cannot violate the jail rules and regulations, and if they do violate the rules, this means another term or extension of their prison life. Similarly, we in this material existence are always fearful. Fearful and full of anxiety. Everyone in the material life, in all species and varieties of life, is full of anxieties because they are stealing the property of God and using it as if it were their own. Because we are thief, we are always in anxiety. “When will my stealing be finished? When will I get caught?” Liberation, *mukti,* means getting relief from these constant anxieties.  
This is possible only when we give up our nonsense idea that this world is meant for us to enjoy and we take to the devotional service of Kṛṣṇa and act as His servant. Otherwise, it is not possible. We can take up devotional service in the association of a learned philosopher who is actually self-realized and who knows Kṛṣṇa as He is.  
Hare Kṛṣṇa. Any questions?

\[Pause.\]

**Mahesh:** Swamijī, we have been hearing about you from Sudāmā.  
**Sudāmā:** Mr. Mahesh is the President of the Kobe Hindu Association and he owns many cloth factories. He is even the owner of this apartment building.  
**Mahesh:** I am nothing. Everything is the grace of Lord.  
**Prabhupāda:** So, where does your cloth come from? It is also the grace of the Lord?  
**Mahesh:** Certainly, everything is from Bhagavān.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, you are stealing from Bhagavān? \[*Everyone laughs.*\]  
**Mahesh:** Swamijī, I am not just taking, I am also giving charity.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, you are just little thief. \[*Everyone laughs.*\]  
**Sudāmā:** And Mr. Santanam is the President of the Kobe branch of the Bank of India.  
**Prabhupāda:** *Jaya.*  
**M. Santanam:** Swamijī, I have been wanting to meet you for many years. I am knowing Sudāmā dāsa since he first came to Tokyo.  
**Prabhupāda:** Thank you.  
**M. Santanam:** Swamijī, I am born *brāhmaṇa* and …  
**Prabhupāda:** \[*Cuts him off.*\] No. *Kalau śūdra sambhavaḥ.* Everyone in Kali-yuga is born *śūdra.*  
**M. Santanam:** No, your disciples are better than me, even though I am born *brāhmaṇa.* You can see my thread.  
**Prabhupāda:** No. Everyone in Kali-yuga is born a *śūdra.*  
**M. Santanam:** But I have a sacred thread.
