**Prabhupāda:** One thread costs a few paisa. Thread is not the test of a *brāhmaṇa.* Everyone in Kali-yuga is born a *śūdra,* still anyone can buy thread. But one must become trained by qualification to become a *brāhmaṇa. Bhagavad-gītā* says: *cātur-varṇyaṁ mayā sṛṣṭaṁ, guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ.* Kṛṣṇa says *guṇa-karma,* by qualities, not *janma-karma,* not by birth. *Śamo damas tapaḥ śaucaṁ.* These are the qualities of a *brāhmaṇa.* It is not by birth; it is by qualities.

\[Pause.\]

**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda, Mr. Mahesh is considering donating this building to us for us to use as a temple.  
**Mahesh:** Yes, I am considering, but there are so many factors involved.  
**Prabhupāda:** Your building is very nice. It would make a nice temple. What is the difficulty in giving this building for Kṛṣṇa’s use?  
**Mahesh:** Actually, this building is where my niece and elder brother are staying. All three floors are presently occupied. But, um, we can speak about this matter tomorrow. \[*Pause.*\] About the program tonight. We have rented one big hall and many Kobe families will be there. And one favorite Swamijī is also speaking. Most of our local families can speak English and Hindi, so you can speak as you like.  
**Prabhupāda:** My disciples do not know Hindi, so I will speak in English.

\[Bhūrijana pays obeisances and exits.\]

**Mahesh:** The other Swamijī, Swami Kaivalya does not know English. You have heard of Swami Kaivalya?  
**Prabhupāda:** No, what is his philosophy? He is Vaiṣṇava?  
**Mahesh:** Hmmm, I think he is not Vaiṣṇava. But actually, in our Hindu religion, all paths are same and all is one.  
**Prabhupāda:** All is one? So we can just take your building?  
**Mahesh:** Ummm. Actually, Swamijī, there are many Hindu families here in Kobe. They are all interested in seeing you. All have heard of the famous Hare Kṛṣṇa Swamijī. And everyone will be coming tonight. If the program is successful, they can support one Hindu temple for your devotees to run in Kobe.  
**Prabhupāda:** What is this Hindu? Hindu means hodge-podge. All is one. This is nonsense. Where is the word “Hindu” mentioned in *śāstra*? In *Bhagavad-gītā*? In *Vedas*? Anywhere? Where is this “Hindu” mentioned even one time? Anyplace? One place?  
**Mahesh:** Ummm.  
**Prabhupāda:** \[*To Mr. Santanam.*\]: You are born in a *brāhmaṇa* family. Any one place? No, this Hindu is a name given by Mohammedans. They cannot pronounce Indus River, so they are calling those on the other side Hindus in their own pronunciation. You know this?  
\[Pause.\]  
You don’t know. But you can learn. To learn, one must approach a bona fide spiritual master and hear from him. This is the process. *Tad viddhi praṇipātena paripraśnena sevayā.* We must serve him and inquire from him. Then he will train us to see the truth. By training we can develop.  
**M. Santanam:** Swamijī, who is real *guru*?  
**Prabhupāda:** A *guru* knows Kṛṣṇa. *Premāñjana-cchurita-bhakti-vilocanena.* His eyes are decorated with love of Kṛṣṇa. He can see Kṛṣṇa everywhere. Become trained by such a spiritual master, and then you will understand everything. We are not Hindus, and we are not training these boys to become Hindus. We are training them to love Kṛṣṇa, God.

\[Bhūrijana enters.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Prabhupāda, excuse me. Your breakfast is ready in the next room.  
**Mahesh:** \[*Stands up.*\] We must go now anyway, and we will meet again tonight at the program.  
**M. Santanam:** Yes.

\[Prabhupāda exits.\]

**Mahesh:** Swamijī is very strong.  
**Sudāmā:** He is seeing Kṛṣṇa, he must be strong.  
**Bhūrijana:** Sudāmā, ask about the garland money.  
**Sudāmā:** Mr. Mahesh, we must get Prabhupāda a nice garland for the program tonight. You can please give us some yen to purchase flowers?  
**Mahesh:** Yen for flowers? How much? Here, take one thousand.  
**Sudāmā:** That will get about two roses.  
**Mahesh:** How much do you need?  
**Sudāmā:** At least 10,000 yen.  
**Mahesh:** Ten thousand! So much? All right, here is 10,000 yen. But you must make one garland for Swami Kaivalya as well.  
**Sudāmā:** But that’s not enough yen for two garlands. Flowers are so expensive that it’ll hardly be enough for one.  
**Mahesh:** That’s all I can give you. And make two garlands. Swamijī should be at the hall at 7:00 for the program; you please come at 6:30.

\[Mr. Mahesh and Mr. Santanam leave.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Sudāmā, I’ve got an idea how we can solve the garland problem. But anyway, it looks like the program tonight might bring some problems of its own.

## Scene Three, at the Rented Hall

Scene Three, at the Rented Hall

**Mahesh:** \[*Looks at his watch and says to himself.*\] It is already ten minutes before seven and Swami Prabhupāda and his Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees have not arrived yet. \[*Looks around*.\] So many have come to see the Hare Kṛṣṇa *guru*. \[*Bearded, fifty-year-old Swami Kaivalya, dressed in saffron and wearing tri-punja Māyāvādī tilaka, enters.*\] Ah, Swami Kaivalya, you have just come. So many people are here, isn’t it?  
**Swami Kaivalya:** *Daṇḍavat,* Maheshjī.  
**Mahesh:** Oh, I see the Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees. Please, Swamijī, please take your seat. \[*Mr. Mahesh seats Swami Kaivalya in one of the folding chairs that have been set up in rows.*\] We will be ready very soon. You are ready to lecture?  
**Swami Kaivalya:** *Haṅ jī.*  
**Mahesh:** We should be starting soon.

\[Sudāmā and Bhūrijana enter.\]

**Mahesh:** Ah, welcome, welcome. Swami is coming? I think he will be happy. So many of our Kobe Hindus have come to see him. Already 150 families are here and more are coming every minute. He is very popular. And of course our Swamijī Kaivalya is here too.  
**Bhūrijana:** Oh, Swami Kaivalya is here. Where? I have a garland for him. Hold Prabhupāda’s garland, Sudāmā.

\[Bhūrijana hands Sudāmā a long garland of roses and holds a second garland.\]

**Mahesh:** Both are very nice garlands. You have done well.

\[Bhūrijana and Sudāmā approach Swami Kaivalya. Bhūrijana pays his obeisances and offers the garland he is holding to Swami Kaivalya, who returns praṇāmas.\]

**Bhūrijana:** \[*To Sudāmā.*\] He doesn’t speak any English, does he?  
**Sudāmā:** No, only Hindi. Anyway, how did you manage to get two garlands worth of flowers out of 10,000 yen?  
**Bhūrijana** \[*Whispers.*\] The 10,000 yen all went toward Prabhupāda’s garland. Our Māyāvādī Swamijī is at this very moment getting full benefit from wearing Prabhupāda’s remnants—the garland Prabhupāda wore yesterday.  
**Sudāmā:** Pretty good, Bhūrijana.

\[Mr. Mahesh walks over.\]

**Mahesh:** Now, Hare Kṛṣṇa. When your Swami comes, he can sit there, on the chair just next to Swami Kaivalya. Who is speaking first?

\[Sudāmā looks around.\]

**Sudāmā:** No, no, Mr. Mahesh. Prabhupāda shouldn’t sit in an ordinary chair. You know how important he is. When was the last time you had such a large gathering of your local Indians?  
**Mahesh:** *Accha.*  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda should be sitting on a *vyāsāsana,* an elevated seat. He should sit on that big seat over there.  
**Mahesh:** That cannot be. That seat is for *Guru Grantha.* Our Sindhi tradition is that whoever reads the *Guru Grantha,* he must sit on this seat. Swamijī cannot.  
**Bhūrijana:** Prabhupāda should sit on it.  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda *must* sit on it.  
**Bhūrijana:** And we better get Prabhupāda’s *vyāsāsana* ready fast because Prabhupāda will be here in just a few minutes.

\[Bhūrijana takes Prabhupāda’s garland from Sudāmā and then exits. He returns with vases of flowers and places them on the vyāsāsana and he again exits.\]

**Sudāmā:** Please, Mr. Mahesh. Prabhupāda is such an exalted soul. He’s not an ordinary *sannyāsī.* Please. He should be shown all respect.  
**Mahesh:** All right, all right. The lecturers can all sit on the *āsana.*  
**Sudāmā:** Don’t you think Prabhupāda should speak first?  
**Mahesh:** Why?  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda is senior to Swami Kaivalya by a lot. And not only that, but …  
**Mahesh:** All right, all right.

\[Bhūrijana excitedly enters from the right.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Prabhupāda’s here!

\[Prabhupāda enters wearing the long rose garland.\]

**Sudāmā and Bhūrijana:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda! *Jaya* Prabhupāda!

\[They sing as they lead Prabhupāda from the side of the stage to the vyāsāsana. Prabhupāda walks up the steps and sits on the vyāsāsana.\]

**Prabhupāda:** You can start *kīrtana* now.  
**Sudāmā**:

nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale  
śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāmin iti nāmine

namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe  
nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe

śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu nityānanda  
śrī-advaita gadādhara śrīvāsādi-gaura-bhakta-vṛnda

hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare  
hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare

**Response:** \[*Only by Prabhupāda’s disciples. The Kobe Indians simply stare.*\]

hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare  
hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare

\[Kīrtana continues for 15 minutes with the few ISKCON devotees chanting and dancing enthusiastically before the amazed audience of Kobe Indian families.\]

Sudāmā:

hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare  
hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare

Response:

hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare  
hare rāma hare rāma rāma rāma hare hare

**Prabhupāda:** *Jaya oṁ viṣṇu-pāda paramahaṁsa parivrājakācārya aṣṭottara-śata śrī śrīmad Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Gosvāmī Mahārāja Prabhupāda ki jaya! Śrī śrī rādhā-kṛṣṇa gopa-gopīnatha śyāma-kuṇḍa, rādhā-kuṇḍa, giri-govardhana ki jaya!*  
All glories to the assembled devotees. All glories to the assembled devotees. All glories to Śrī Śrī Guru and Gaurāṅga.  
**Bhūrijana:** *Jaya oṁ viṣṇu-pāda paramahaṁsa parivrājakācārya aṣṭottara-śata śrī śrīmad His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Śrīla Prabhupāda ki jaya!*  
**Prabhupāda:** *Oṁ namo bhagavate vāsudevāya.* \[*Three times. Only Prabhupāda’s disciples respond*.\]  
We are reading from the Bhagavad-gītā, Second Chapter, verse 17.

avināśi tu tad viddhi  
yena sarvam idaṁ tatam  
vināśam avyayasyāsya  
na kaścit kartum arhati

“That which pervades the entire body you should know to be indestructible. No one is able to destroy that imperishable soul.”

So here, Kṛṣṇa clearly says that consciousness, the symptom of the soul, is *avināśi,* imperishable. Previously He said to Arjuna, “You, I, and all these other kings existed in the past, we exist now, and we shall continue to exist in the future.” There is no truth to the rascal Māyāvāda idea that after liberation we all intermingle and become a homogeneous lump. Even after we attain liberation we remain individual particles of consciousness.

This Māyāvāda philosophy is false. We are individuals eternally. Kṛṣṇa will explain that later in the *Bhagavad-gītā: mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke jīva-bhūtaḥ sanātanaḥ.* “The living entities in this material world are My eternal fragmental parts.” This is confirmed in the *Upaniṣads: nityo nityānāṁ cetanaś cetanānām.* “Of all the eternal conscious living beings, there is one who is Supreme.”

Yesterday we consulted the dictionary about the meaning of the word “God,” and one of the definitions was “the Supreme Being.” So we are living beings, but we are not supreme; we are subordinate. God is also a living being, but He is the Supreme Being. That is the difference. It is a very simple thing. You cannot say, “I am the Supreme Being.” Yet the rascals say, “I am God.” How can you be God? Are you Supreme? As soon as we ask this question—”Are you Supreme?”—you must answer, “No.” Then how have you become God?

Just try to understand how foolish they are. Will any sane man say, “I am moving the sun, I am moving the moon, I am moving the seas?” Will any sane man say that? *No.* Will anyone here say that you are moving the sun and the moon? Anyone? Who will say? Nobody will say this. And still these rascals are claiming, “I have become God. We are all God.” This rascal atheistic philosophy has killed the whole world. So many so-called “incarnations,” so many “gods” are preaching false theories only. So many *gurus*—all rascals. Take my word for it. Anyone who is speaking against the principles of the *Bhagavad-gītā* is rascal number one. That’s all. Don’t give him any credit. On every page of the *Bhagavad-gītā* Kṛṣṇa is referred to as Bhagavān, the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Ask anyone if they accept. Ask him to his face: “Do you accept Kṛṣṇa as the Supreme Personality of Godhead?” If he says, “No,” tell him, “Then you are a rascal.” That’s all. There is no exception.

Know that Kṛṣṇa is God, the Supreme. *Īśvaraḥ paramaḥ kṛṣṇaḥ sac-cid-ānanda-vigrahaḥ.* Kṛṣṇa is accepted as God by all the *ācāryas* and by all the Vedic scriptures. And when He was present on earth He proved that He was God. Nobody was equal to Kṛṣṇa when He was personally present.

So let us utilize our minute consciousness to understand the supreme consciousness, God. Although I am conscious, I am not supremely conscious. I am not all-knowing, but God is. So there is always a difference between God and ourselves. We can never be equal to God. That is impossible. We can become His humble servant, but we cannot do so if we think that one day we will become God. Understanding this is real intelligence. Otherwise, it is all rascaldom. Don’t be victims of the rascals. Hare Kṛṣṇa. Thank you very much.

**Sudāmā:** Are there any questions? \[*Long pause.*\]  
**Mahesh:** No questions. Now Swami Kaivalya, you please come.

\[Swami Kaivalya steps to the vyāsāsana with Mr. Mahesh and Mr. Mahesh offers his hand to Prabhupāda to help him down. Mr. Mahesh again offers Prabhupāda his hand and then again, but Prabhupāda remains sitting on the vyāsāsana.\]

**Sudāmā:** I think Prabhupāda isn’t getting down.  
**Swami Kaivalya:** *Ṭhik, ṭhik.*

\[Mr. Mahesh unhappily leads Swami Kaivalya back to his chair. He then carries the microphone from the vyāsāsana and sets it up in front of Swami Kaivalya.\]

**Mahesh:** You can speak from here.

\[Swami Kaivalya sits down and Mr. Mahesh announces.\]

**Mahesh:** Now we request Swami Kaivalya to speak about his philosophy.

\[Swami Kaivalya stands up, offers praṇāmas to the audience, closes his eyes and begins lecturing in Hindi.\]

**Swami Kaivalya:** Oṁ Oṁ Oṁ. Oṁ namo nārāyaṇa …  
**Bhūrijana:** \[After a few minutes, to Sudāmā.\]: Look, I can’t even tell if Prabhupāda’s listening to the Māyāvādī speak. He’s just sitting and chanting with his eyes closed.  
**Sudāmā:** We might as well chant too. We can’t understand a thing.

\[Sudāmā, Bhānu, and Bhūrijana put their hands in their bead bags and softly chant, rocking back and forth as they imitate Prabhupāda’s movements.\]

**Bhūrijana:** \[*After a few minutes.*\] I’m getting bored.  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda doesn’t look bored.  
**Bhūrijana:** At least Swami Kaivalya is wearing Prabhupāda’s garland. I wonder what he’s ranting and raving about?  
**Sudāmā:** I can’t imagine, but look at how intense Prabhupāda seems.  
**Bhūrijana:** \[*After a few minutes.*\] It seems like he’s been going on and on for about an hour already.  
**Bhānu:** No, he’s been speaking thirty minutes. I’ve been timing him.  
**Sudāmā:** I wonder how much longer this is going to last.  
\[Pause.\]  
**Bhūrijana:** Sudāmā, look at Prabhupāda. He seems disturbed by something. He’s looking all around.  
**Sudāmā:** Prabhupāda *is* disturbed, isn’t he?  
**Bhūrijana:** What’s going on?  
**Sudāmā:** What’s the Māyāvādī saying?

\[Prabhupāda looks from one disciple to another.\]

**Prabhupāda:** \[*Suddenly and strongly.*\] Stop him!

\[Bhūrijana and Sudāmā are shocked and don’t quite know what to do. Bhūrijana’s body tenses, and he begins fingering the strings of the karatālas in his hand. Prabhupāda looks at his disciples intensely, and then looks Bhūrijana right in the eye.\]

**Prabhupāda:** Stop him! \[*Two-second pause.*\] Start *kīrtana*! Immediately!

\[The devotees jump up and begin loudly chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. They bang their karatālas and dance.\]

\[Swami Kaivalya looks around and without much ado, makes praṇāmas, says daṇyavat, and sits down. Mr. Mahesh stands up, looks at Swami Kaivalya, looks at the devotees and at Prabhupāda, and walks off.\]

## Scene Four, Prabhupāda’s Room in the Apartment

\[Prabhupāda is sitting on the floor behind a low desk in a dimly lit room. He peacefully sips hot milk. Bhānu is attending him.\]

**Prabhupāda:** Have the others come yet?  
**Bhānu:** No Prabhupāda. They stayed after the *kīrtana* to distribute your books.  
**Prabhupāda:** That is good.  
**Bhānu:** Would you like some more milk?  
**Prabhupāda:** No, this is sufficient. What is that sound? See if Sudāmā and Bhūrijana have come.

\[Bhānu leaves and comes back after a few seconds.\]

**Bhānu:** Yes, they’ve just arrived and they say Mr. Mahesh has come with them.

\[Sudāmā and Bhūrijana enter and offer their obeisances.\]

**Sudāmā and Bhūrijana:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda!  
**Prabhupāda:** So the people appreciated our program? Did they take books?  
**Bhūrijana:** Some bought books, Prabhupāda, but most really couldn’t at all understand what happened.  
**Sudāmā:** And to tell you the truth, Prabhupāda, neither did we. The Māyāvādī spoke in Hindi, so we couldn’t understand anything. You let him speak for thirty minutes and then suddenly you stopped him. What happened?  
**Bhūrijana:** We just wanted to come to see you as fast as we could.  
**Sudāmā:** We ran up the three flights of stairs.

\[Mr. Mahesh enters.\]

**Mahesh:** Swamijī, so many people could not understand why you have done that.  
**Prabhupāda:** \[*Laughs.*\] My Guru Mahārāja used to describe his preaching style as a chopping technique. \[*Prabhupāda “chops” his right hand a few times into the open palm of his left.*\] I have also adopted it. \[*Laughs.*\] These Māyāvādīs think of themselves as Vedāntists. *Dānta* means teeth and *vi* means no. So in front of the Vaiṣṇava *ācāryas,* all their big, big arguments have got no teeth. Just like they say: “We are all God, but have forgotten.” God has forgotten? How is that? How forgetfulness has covered God? That means forgetfulness is greater than God. No sane man will accept this.  
**Sudāmā:** *Jaya* Prabhupāda!  
**Prabhupāda:** So we should not tolerate their toothless arguments. This Māyāvāda philosophy is just atheism. “We are all God.” Nonsense! They are actually saying there is no God!  
**Mahesh:** No, no, no. They are saying that truth is one. There are many paths and one truth.  
**Prabhupāda:** Just atheism. It has spoiled the world. Just see. People have given up worshiping God. They are practically not humans. They are killing cows. They are killing their own children in the womb. And they call this advancement of civilization. They are condemned. They are thinking as soon as the body dies, everything is finished, so let me now enjoy. But this is ignorance. They will not die. *Dehino ‘smin yathā dehe.* They will live. *Na hanyate hanyamāne śarīre.* They will live for so many lifetimes and suffer—birth after birth—if we do not help them. \[*Pause.*\] So you have made yourself a successful businessman. Do you think Māyāvāda philosophy is good business? What do you think?

**Mahesh:** I think everything in our Hindu religion is one of tolerance to other paths. All religions bring us nearer to the God.

\[Prabhupāda gets very serious, the corners of his mouth seem to extend down almost to the edges of his chin.\]

**Prabhupāda:** Hmmm. They are misguiding people away from Kṛṣṇa.  
**Mahesh:** They are trying …  
**Prabhupāda:** … to make everyone forget Kṛṣṇa. “Not to Kṛṣṇa,” they say. “Do not surrender to Kṛṣṇa. Surrender to the unborn, unmanifest within Kṛṣṇa.” They don’t know Kṛṣṇa is Absolute. He is the same in and out. They don’t know and still they speak. That means they are cheaters.  
**Mahesh:** So many opinions about truth are there.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, opinions. But from *śāstra* no one can avoid accepting Kṛṣṇa as the Supreme Personality of Godhead. It is insanity to avoid. All the Vedic literature. *Ṛg Veda* says: *oṁ tad viṣṇoḥ paramaṁ padaṁ. Upaniṣad, Purāṇas, Gītā.* They all say Kṛṣṇa’s form, His activities, everything is eternal.  
**Mahesh:** Why just Kṛṣṇa?  
**Prabhupāda:** Why *not* Kṛṣṇa. The *śāstra* says Kṛṣṇa. *Īśvaraḥ paramaḥ kṛṣṇaḥ sac-cid-ānanda-vigrahaḥ. Śāstra* says, so we are also saying.  
**Mahesh:** But Swamijī, *śāstra* also says God is everything.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, God is everything. If God is everything, why don’t you worship Kṛṣṇa? Simply envy, that is all. Worship everything, but not Kṛṣṇa. Don’t worship Kṛṣṇa’s form. Don’t hear of His activities. No face. No eyes. Practically you want to mutilate Kṛṣṇa. This is Māyāvāda philosophy.  
**Mahesh:** Form is there, but one can go beyond to see.  
**Prabhupāda:** No! There is no beyond Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa says, *Ahaṁ sarvasya prabhavo:* “I am the source of everything.” *Mattaḥ parataraṁ nānyat.* No “beyond Kṛṣṇa.” We accept Kṛṣṇa’s words. We don’t tolerate this Māyāvādī nonsense. We are right. Everyone who disagrees with Kṛṣṇa is wrong.  
**Mahesh:** \[*Gets up and starts walking out.*\] I think my apartment is not suitable for your Hare Kṛṣṇa temple. It has too many stories for walking up. Local people will not come.

\[Mr. Mahesh leaves. Three-second pause.\]

**Sudāmā:** So much for our Kobe temple.  
**Prabhupāda:** \[*Laughing.*\] Never mind, one day he will appreciate how we have chastised him for his rascaldom. \[*Pause.*\] Actually, he is simply attached to his apartment house. He can become a mouse in his next life and live here if he is so attached. \[*Laughs and makes a chopping gesture.*\]

\[Pause.\]

**Bhūrijana:** Prabhupāda, you never told us why you listened to the Māyāvādī for thirty minutes and then stopped him.  
**Prabhupāda:** First, the Māyāvādī’s preaching was all right, but then he began explaining *pañcopāsanā.*  
**Sudāmā:** *Pañcopāsanā*?  
**Prabhupāda:** *Pañcopāsanā* is when they recommend worship of five kinds of demigods. First worship Sūrya, then Gaṇeśa, then Durgā-devī, Śiva, and Viṣṇu.  
**Sudāmā:** So that’s what he was saying.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, so he was explaining this *pañcopāsanā,* their worship system, and then he started to say, “But one must ultimately go beyond this worship and one must give it up—because the Supreme is beyond worship. The Supreme is Brahman, formless.” \[*Pause for two seconds.*\] And I could not tolerate that.  
**Sudāmā:** Whew.  
**Prabhupāda:** How could I tolerate this blasphemy? No, I could not. And neither could my Guru Mahārāja—*“rūpānuga-viruddhāpasiddhānta-dhvānta-hāriṇe.*“ He would not tolerate any statement against the authority of Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī.  
**Bhūrijana:** You are a perfect servant of your Guru Mahārāja, Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda:** These Māyāvādīs, they talk: “All is one,” “The Supreme has no form,” “All is illusion,” but then when they get a little success, they build some hospital. Just see.  
**Bhūrijana:** If everything is illusion, a dream, why cure a disease that a person is only dreaming? Better to wake him up.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, so they build hospitals and show that they have fallen from their own philosophy. They cannot themselves follow this Māyāvāda nonsense. Even the biggest Māyāvādīs fall down like this.  
**Sudāmā:** Or worse.

\[Two-second pause.\]

**Bhānu:** Prabhupāda, must all Māyāvādīs fall down?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes.  
**Bhānu:** Always?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes.  
**Sudāmā:** Why, Prabhupāda?

\[Pause.\]

**Prabhupāda:** Because they get lonely. \[*Pause.*\] The living entity must be active. It is his nature. Even in Brahman he longs for some activity. Always. So in Brahman he cannot stay inactive and he has no qualification to serve Kṛṣṇa in Vaikuṇṭha, so he must fall down. You can understand? This Māyāvāda is the last trap of *māyā.* Forget Kṛṣṇa, don’t serve Kṛṣṇa, I am God, you are God. \[*Laughs.*\] But it is not attractive. And Kṛṣṇa is so attractive. His name, form, pastimes—with His cowherd boyfriends or Mother Yaśodā or the *gopīs.* So attractive. Anyone who comes in contact with Kṛṣṇa—even by hearing about Him—becomes mad after His association. \[*Pause.*\] So when Māyāvādīs hear from Kṛṣṇa’s pure devotees, they also give up their nonsense loneliness and become devotees.

\[Pause.\]

**Sudāmā:** I think it’s late Prabhupāda, and the train leaves early in the morning. We should take your leave and let you take rest.  
**Prabhupāda:** All right. Hare Kṛṣṇa.

\[All the devotees pay their obeisances and leave. Bhūrijana stays back.\]

**Bhūrijana:** You were like a lion at the program tonight.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, when I am out, I am like a lion in the chase, but when I am home with the devotees, I am like a lamb.  
**Bhūrijana:** Jaya Prabhupāda!

\[Pays obeisances and leaves.\]

## Manila, 1972

Manila, 1972

## Preaching in the Philippines

Back in Hong Kong, several months after visiting Prabhupāda in Japan, I received an aerogram from Prabhupāda informing me of his plan to visit to the Philippines. He suggested I join him at our Manila temple. I was thrilled at his invitation and telegraphed Sudāmā Vipra Swami, the devotee who had begun the Filipino preaching. Sudāmā Vipra telegrammed back his surprise. Due to the recently imposed martial law, he hadn’t received word of Prabhupāda’s arrival.

A week before Prabhupāda was due, Jagattāriṇī and I flew to Manila and caught a taxi to our temple on Ortega Street in San Juan, a Manila suburb. We were shocked. The rented temple actually *was* a mansion. And it was bustling with both shaved-up and long-haired Filipino devotees. Our own small Hong Kong center was dead compared to this newly opened temple in Manila. There were so many happy Filipinos busy in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

A devotee construction crew was assembling a new altar and a new *vyāsāsana* for Prabhupāda. The *vyāsāsana* was huge—”so Prabhupāda would feel comfortable enough to speak for hours,” Sudāmā Vipra Swami said. In the evenings, the construction noise stopped and the devotees would settle into a sweet *kīrtana.* What a contrast to the drab Hong Kong Chinese, whose mood was summed up in the Cantonese maxim, *mo chin mo gong* “no money, no talk.”

The day before Prabhupāda’s arrival, Sudāmā Vipra Swami pulled out the screens of the windows to allow air to flow in the humid Manila evenings, “the way Prabhupāda likes it.” The next morning we picked up Prabhupāda and his entourage from the new Manila Airport (the old one had recently and mysteriously burned down after the anti-corruption wing of the police said they would investigate the airport manager’s financial records). We drove Prabhupāda to his suite in the Manila Hotel Intercontinental, the best hotel in the city, because our rented temple in San Juan had no suitable facility. Smiling and always in Vṛndāvana, Prabhupāda gathered us on the floor around him while he sat cross-legged behind a round glass table.

Sudāmā Vipra Swami broached the subject of me moving from Hong Kong to the Philippines to assist him:

**Sudāmā Vipra Swami:** Prabhupāda, what about Bhūrijana staying in the Philippines to help with the preaching?  
**Bhūrijana:** Hong Kong is such a materialistic place. Not many people are coming to our Hong Kong temple and so many are coming in Manila. It seems a better place for me to preach.  
**Prabhupāda:** But Hong Kong is so important city. It is the Chinese gateway. And one-third of all the people of the world live in China.  
**Bhūrijana:** But not many Chinese people are coming. Only Indians, and the Indians in Hong Kong are interested only in making money. They aren’t serious about spiritual life.  
**Prabhupāda:** When a university class is going on, even if only one student is in attendance, it must not be closed.  
**Bhūrijana:** But maintaining the temple is such a strain.

When I explained to Prabhupāda that we were now forced to spend 3,000 Hong Kong dollars (about 600 U.S. dollars) on rent each month Prabhupāda became sympathetic and reluctantly agreed to our closing the Hong Kong temple in favor of us preaching in the Philippines.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda once told me that closing a temple was a disgrace. I held out for years in Hong Kong because of this, but the impenetrable materialism was discouraging, especially when contrasted with the lightness and receptivity in the Philippines. Prabhupāda looked at my case pragmatically, and finally agreed to my request.

## Guru’s Words

Sudāmā Vipra Swami, Siddha-svarūpānanda Goswami, Śrutakīrti, Jagattāriṇī, and I sat around Prabhupāda’s table, watching him as he ate fresh Filipino fruit—mangoes, bananas, and apples—for breakfast. Prabhupāda wanted milk and sent Śrutakīrti out. Śrutakīrti was his new servant, and he was fresh from America. After ten minutes, Śrutakīrti returned pot in hand, complaining that the hotel staff wouldn’t cooperate.

Prabhupāda insisted that he wanted hot milk and Śrutakīrti left again for the hotel kitchen. Ten minutes passed and Śrutakīrti again returned with cold milk. The staff wouldn’t let him into the hotel kitchen.

Prabhupāda looked sharply at Śrutakīrti, “You are not serious!”

Sudāmā Vipra Swami, “Big Dave,” heavy set, with a skull and crossbones tattooed on his arm, picked up the milk pot and walked out. He returned five minutes later with steaming hot milk. “Sometimes you have to yell, Śrīla Prabhupāda,” he said.

Prabhupāda smiled.

\* \* \*

A key to associating with Prabhupāda was to take all he said as weighty. No words or requests, even if spoken in a casual manner, were casual. My experience was that Kṛṣṇa reciprocated when we respected all of Prabhupāda’s words with the utmost deference.

## LSD Philosophy

Also sitting with us was Nilo Santos, a young member of the wealthy Manila aristocracy. Nilo liked to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. Prabhupāda asked, “So what was your philosophy before you chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa?” Nilo smiled and said innocently, “LSD philosophy.” Prabhupāda returned an even bigger smile, “Oh, so many of our students also followed that philosophy. Isn’t that correct, Siddha-svarūpa?”

\* \* \*

The Philippines had been transformed by the movie *The Godfather.* After it hit the Filipino movie theaters, gangs of imitation Mafia groups suddenly appeared, along with organized crime, real guns, and real killing. This fad lasted until the *Woodstock* movie was released in 1970. Then violence was out and drugs, love, and “LSD philosophy” were in. Yet although the mode had changed, countless souls were still being insidiously slaughtered. Prabhupāda once commented that because of Timothy Leary, “so many blossoming young flowers have become spoiled.”

LSD philosophy had spread around the world. Prabhupāda’s mercy had similarly spread to combat and cure it.

## Drinking Hot, Honeyed Milk

Prabhupāda began to drink the hot milk Sudāmā Vipra Swami had brought. He dipped his spoon into a jar of honey and pulled it out half full. Then he dipped the honey-laden spoon into the milk. When he pulled it out, milk floated above the thick layer of honey still on the spoon. Prabhupāda sipped the milk off the top of the honey. He repeated this process several times until the milk had cooled.

\* \* \*

Seeing Prabhupāda enjoy hot milk in this unique fashion brought us great happiness.

## Hong Kong Mangoes

Prabhupāda then ate a long yellow mango. “These are not as good as the mangoes you gave me in Hong Kong,” Prabhupāda said to me as his eyes twinkled. “Those mangoes also came from the Philippines,” I replied.

## A Mountain Surrounded by a Lake

Vijayadvaja, an American devotee serving in the Philippines, cooked for Prabhupāda both morning and noon. Each morning, Vijayadvaja followed Prabhupāda’s recipe for *halavā:* one part semolina, two parts water, one part sugar, and one part ghee. The *halavā* looked like a small mountain floating in ghee. Yet Prabhupāda appreciated Vijayadvaja’s cooking, especially his *halavā,* commenting, “The quality is sufficient.”

Once, however, Vijayadvaja hoped to create a large quantity of remnants by loading up Prabhupāda’s lunch plate with *prasādam.* Prabhupāda’s comment: “When my plate is so full, I lose my appetite.”

\* \* \*

To please the Lord and His devotees is the pleasure of a devotee. Even a grain of selfishness in our attempt spoils the preparation.

## Tolerance, Tolerance, Tolerance

Prabhupāda’s suite in the Hotel Intercontinental was luxurious. A clay pot with a spigot at the bottom held fresh water. Prabhupāda drank from the pot on the first day because it kept the water cool “just like in India.” But when the water was not changed the second day, Prabhupāda would not drink, although he neither complained nor mentioned the oversight.

\* \* \*

In one circumstance Prabhupāda demanded hot milk and in another he tolerated unchanged water. How does a teacher know when to demand and when to tolerate? An expert teacher knows which of his own needs are essential and also knows when a response is required for the advancement of his student.

## A lecture that Filled Me with Fear

I am not a carpenter, but I was asked to construct a bookstand for Prabhupāda. He would use the stand to hold his *Bhagavad-gītā* while he gave the evening lecture. I set to work with borrowed tools and completed it just before Prabhupāda’s class. It had a plywood base which tucked under his *vyāsāsana* cushion. Four wooden posts extended upward to support the angled wood stand on which the book rested.

During his first lecture, Prabhupāda preached strongly. As he spoke, he referred again and again to his *Bhagavad-gītā.* At times he held the stand as he rocked back and forth in the rhythm of his lecture. At other times he pushed forward on the bookstand while making his points.

As soon as Prabhupāda began, I noticed strain on the book stand where the base met the posts. As Prabhupāda rocked back and forth, the posts also began to rock back and forth. I saw a glimmer of silver, the nails above the base. I was afraid. What if it collapses? What if Prabhupāda pushes against the stand and the posts come off? I was sweating. Prayers spontaneously erupted from my heart. I imagined Prabhupāda’s enthusiastic preaching splitting the stand in two. Envisioning the stand’s collapse, I heard Prabhupāda’s voice in my mind demanding, “Who has done this? Who has built this?” I closed my eyes only to open them and focus on the widening gap between the base and the posts. The metal nails glinting in the temple lights seemed to sparkle a promise of my doom.

The lecture was eternal. I heard none of it. Sudāmā Vipra Swami, who was sitting next to me during my ordeal, commented, “Looks like Prabhupāda’s lecture really hit you.”

\* \* \*

Kṛṣṇa pushed me to the limit. Directly after the lecture, I pounded tens of additional nails into the stand and made it strong enough to withstand the United States Marines. What did I learn? Better to be safe than sorry. Get things built, or at least checked, by experts. When planning for an event, always consider what could possibly go wrong and be ready for it. Be especially careful when serving your spiritual master and exalted Vaiṣṇavas. When a mistake is made, correct it as quickly as possible. Success and failure are ultimately in Kṛṣṇa’s hands.

## Bāla Kṛṣṇa, Balarāma, and Gaṇeśa?

Jagattāriṇī had grown attached to her Laḍḍu Gopāla Deity, which we began to call Bāla Kṛṣṇa. He was the Deity to whom Prabhupāda had offered his respects in the doll house in Hong Kong. Since then, Jagattāriṇī had added a slightly larger Laḍḍu Gopāla Deity as “Balarāma,” and a tiny, one-inch Gaṇeśa deity. She had the three deities with her when we saw Prabhupāda in Manila.

When Jagattāriṇī showed Prabhupāda the group of deities, Prabhupāda encouraged her, “These are your deities. They are very beautiful.” He turned to me, “Why Gaṇeśa?” I referred to *The Nectar of Devotion’s* recommendation that devotees worship Gaṇeśa to remove obstacles in their worship of Kṛṣṇa. Prabhupāda looked at me for a moment and said no more.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda knew we needed encouragement and companionship in Hong Kong. He always offered encouragement, even to the point of allowing Jagattāriṇī the companionship of personal deities, despite the informality of her worship and despite the worship of Gaṇeśa and his inclusion with Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma.

## No Second Center

Jagattāriṇī began carrying the deities everywhere. During one of Prabhupāda’s lectures at the temple, Prabhupāda climbed the high *vyāsāsana* steps and began to speak. Jagattāriṇī sat on the floor to the left of the *vyāsāsana* and placed her three deities before her on a velvet cloth. As Prabhupāda spoke, it occurred to her that it may be inappropriate to have the deities on the floor, so she carefully transferred them to the steps of Prabhupāda’s *vyāsāsana.*

When the lecture ended and Prabhupāda was ready to climb down the steps, he saw the deities. Jagattāriṇī had been slow in removing them. He beckoned her forward. “When you are here, you can place the deities on the temple altar. You should not establish another center in the temple.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda waited for the perfect moment to instruct us about the general standard for devotees living in temples. Temples should have one center. Rather than individual devotees conducting independent worship, temple devotees should rally around the temple center, the temple Deities.

## Two Lotuses Near Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma

Jagattāriṇī followed Prabhupāda’s order to keep the deities on the altar, but she still occasionally carried the deities with her in a box. On the final day of Prabhupāda’s visit, we sat around Prabhupāda as he relaxed behind the round, glass hotel table. Jagattāriṇī placed the deities and their cloth on the table as Prabhupāda began his breakfast.

Prabhupāda viewed the deities as he took *prasādam.* When he finished and his plate had been removed, he moved to put his feet up on the table. Seeing the deities, he hesitated, then stretched his legs out on the floor. Later, as he continued speaking, he again went to put his feet on the table, but again saw the deities and stopped. Finally, he placed both feet on the table and continued speaking.

\* \* \*

We were so foolish that we neglected to remove the deities even after Prabhupāda’s two indirect requests. In our childish simplicity, we were satisfied to see Prabhupāda’s lotus feet on the breakfast table near the deities. Jagattāriṇī, of course, quickly removed the deities, appreciating how Prabhupāda was completely relaxed and had acted without adding to her embarrassment.

## Expertise

Sudāmā Vipra Swami arranged a lecture for Prabhupāda in the hotel’s enormous, elegantly decorated banquet hall. Despite the government’s ban on advertising, the news spread and the hall was packed. Five hundred Indians and Filipinos attended. Immediately after his lecture, Prabhupāda called for questions.

An Indian man stood up and said in a loud voice, “Isn’t it true that the all-inclusive conception of the Absolute … ” He rambled on. After about three minutes, the audience began fidgeting.

Prabhupāda leaned toward the microphone and asked, “Are you finished?”

“No,” the man retorted, and he continued for another two minutes. Again Prabhupāda asked, “Are you finished yet?”

“No, let me speak.” His monologue of disjointed, spiritual-sounding phrases continued. After another three minutes, the audience began to wax angry. Prabhupāda again gently spoke into the microphone, “Are you finished yet?”

“Yes, now I am finished.”

Prabhupāda thundered into the microphone, “Then sit down!” The banquet hall filled with laughter and cheering.

\* \* \*

Had Prabhupāda interrupted the man before he was able to “ask” his self-serving “question,” the audience would have favored the man. Prabhupāda waited and allowed the man to parade his foolishness. When the audience had had sufficient time to realize the man’s motives, Prabhupāda, with the full backing of the audience, demanded that the man sit down.

Prabhupāda was expert.

## Practical Bravery

We visited Prabhupāda in his room during the day. In the evening, we would see him during the *kīrtana* and lecture at the temple in San Juan.

On the first day, Prabhupāda traveled from the hotel to San Juan through the late afternoon traffic. The air was filled with smog, there was a traffic jam, and the Filipino drivers drove wildly. On the second day, a lorry almost smashed into Prabhupāda’s car.

Prabhupāda went to the temple that second day, but he said the roads were unsafe and never went again.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda would tolerate unlimited personal inconvenience to help a disciple or to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He was fearless. He had continued preaching in Calcutta even when his life was threatened by Naxalite extremists. Yet ISKCON and its worldwide preaching depended on him. Therefore he chose not to risk the Manila traffic just to give a lecture at the San Juan temple. Prabhupāda was always practical.

## A Hong Kong Surprise

Prabhupāda flew on to India after staying in Manila for less than a week. Even though his stay was short, we were energized by his association. Thinking of my future preaching in Manila, I left for Hong Kong to wind up my affairs. A short tour of Hong Kong’s intense materialism seemed bearable, and I set out to collect money and ship our temples’ possessions to Manila. I also picked up from our Hong Kong publisher a newly printed Chinese edition of *Back to Godhead* magazine. Air-mailing five copies to Prabhupāda in India, I happily returned to Manila—my new preaching field.

But the relief of brighter preaching fields was not meant to be. After only one month of preaching in the Filipino mountain resort city of Bagio, it became clear to me that I belonged in Hong Kong. I returned and just after my first week back, I received a letter from Prabhupāda, written after he received the Chinese BTGs. His reply had been forwarded from the Philippines and it requested that I return to Hong Kong. I immediately telegraphed Prabhupāda my new Hong Kong address. Several days later I received his return telegraph: “May Krishna bless you more and more. Signed. A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami.”

\* \* \*

I read Prabhupāda’s telegram and understood that Prabhupāda was blessing me more and more and more and more for eternity. My understanding of the depth of his eternal blessing is shallow, but that does not limit the depth of his blessing. Nor does it limit the eternal good fortune it brings. My lack of spiritual strength, surrender, and determination only allowed me to remainin Hong Kong for another two years, but Prabhupāda’s “more and more” blessing keeps afloat my ongoing attempts in devotional service.

## Hong Kong, 1974—Part I

Hong Kong, 1974—Part I

I have divided my telling of Prabhupāda’s 1974 Hong Kong visit into two chapters. This chapter describes Prabhupāda’s attempt to rectify and purify my consciousness.

## Struggling and Going Under

We remained preaching in Hong Kong for Prabhupāda’s pleasure. Our opportunities to occasionally see him and the letters we received from him gave us strength to continue. But preaching in Hong Kong was lonely. We had no association with other devotees since almost no one passed through our Far Eastern preaching outpost. Those who did visit were often peculiar or rebels. Our ISKCON authority visited periodically, but his mood was hardly positive. Rather than leading or assisting our struggle to preach, he seemed to only either reprimand us or demand money. Our Indian supporters began relaying to us reports of unfavorable results of heavy-handed book distribution techniques in Australia and other parts of the world. We began to get critical of the devotees, thinking them passionate or fanatical in their attempts to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Jagattāriṇī had not even been initiated before she arrived in Hong Kong. She had flown starry-eyed into an arranged marriage, hoping to save the “unfortunate” Chinese of Hong Kong, but her stars quickly turned to tears. She suddenly had no women-devotee friends and a new husband whose New York “culture” and mannerisms appeared harsh. She had joined the exciting Los Angeles temple fresh from a budding film career, with months of ecstatic *harināma-saṅkīrtana* on Hollywood Boulevard with Viṣṇujana and sixty other devotees. Then suddenly she was alone in Hong Kong. I requested devotees in the U.S. to send a householder couple as reinforcements. One wrote back, “Good devotees do not grow on trees.” We had Prabhupāda’s full support and encouragement, but little else.

We preached to the local Indian community, but discovered that company names like Radha-Krishna Enterprises, Krishna Kripa Exports, and Laksmi-Narayan Traders, did not indicate devotion. We approached wealthy Indian shopkeepers with *Back to Godhead* in hand and were refused its one dollar cost. “But we are doing your work! This is the work of those born in India. We have taken it up. At least read our magazine.” Their cold, blank stares cracked our hearts.

Prabhupāda then recommended we neglect the Indians and preach to the Chinese. Hong Kong was largely populated by Cantonese businessmen who had escaped from Communist China in the late 1940s. Their ideal was business; their tendency, atheism. In addition, they shared no love with the Hong Kong Indians and looked down on Indian culture. Their conception of Indian religion was “Right hand for eating, left hand for toilet.”

Sudāmā Vipra Swami and Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami became our friends. Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa was successfully preaching in New Zealand, and I had met Sudāmā Vipra Swami when Prabhupāda sent him to Hong Kong in an attempt to establish preaching in China before he opened the temple in the Philippines. Both Tuṣṭa Kṛṣṇa and Sudāmā Vipra Swami had been followers of charismatic Siddha-svarūpānanda Goswami, then Sai, a 1960s spiritual leader from Hawaii. After Sai mixed Hare Kṛṣṇa chanting into his brew of natural-living, meditation, and *kuṇḍalinī-yoga,* he surrendered his *āśrama* and his disciples to Prabhupāda. The Kṛṣṇa conscious style of these devotees, compared to the somewhat tense dealings with other ISKCON devotees, was refreshing, relaxed, friendly—and practical. At this time I did not know that tension existed between Siddha-svarūpa and his followers and other disciples of Prabhupāda.

I gradually adapted their ideas as an attempt to make our preaching successful in Hong Kong. With Prabhupāda’s permission, I grew my hair and wore nondevotee clothes. Prabhupāda, however, insisted we wear *tilaka,* “even if applied thin.” We moved our temple room to a smaller room in our flat and converted the temple room into a typical living room with couches and chairs. We began cooking *prasādam* according to Chinese vegetarian tastes and held *kīrtanas* with *mṛdaṅga, karatālas,* and guitar. Gradually, Chinese people began responding to our *Back to Godhead* distribution and weekly *“yoga*” meetings at the Hong Kong City Hall. Guests began attending our Sunday Feast and *kīrtanas.* At times we would gather up a group and ferry from crowded Hong Kong to Dai Long Waan, a secluded nearby island beach, take *prasādam,* and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa as the ocean waves rolled in. Chinese people were becoming devotees.

But along with adapting our preaching techniques, we also slackened our Kṛṣṇa conscious practices—we began neglecting to hold *maṅgala-ārati* and would just awaken and chant our rounds. I became “too busy” to study Prabhupāda’s books. Gradually the weed of material enjoyment began to attach itself around my tender devotional creeper, and going to the beach to chant and wearing nondevotee clothes were beginning to assume other dimensions than a preaching tactic. I imagined that Prabhupāda understood and was offering sanction due to our increased preaching success.

## Real or Imagined Prabhupāda?

One day an aerogram arrived from Prabhupāda’s secretary, Satsvarūpa Mahārāja, informing us that Prabhupāda would visit us in two weeks. His letter closed, “Hoping to see you again in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.” My left eye twitched. Full of anxiety, I began to prepare for Prabhupāda’s second visit to Hong Kong.

On the day of Prabhupāda’s arrival, we arranged for a friend to carry the two luxurious rose garlands we would present to Prabhupāda. An acquaintance agreed to allow us the use of his Rolls Royce to drive Prabhupāda from the airport to the Hong Kong Hilton.

Jagattāriṇī and I traveled to the airport by taxi and waited for the garlands, the Rolls Royce, and Prabhupāda. Watching the black flight-arrival notice board, I saw Prabhupāda’s plane had arrived. Yet neither the garlands nor the Rolls Royce had appeared. I became anxious. “What if Prabhupāda steps out of customs and we don’t have a garland for him? What if there’s no car?”

After ten more minutes, the board announced that Prabhupāda’s flight was clearing Customs. I would be seeing him in only a few minutes. Where were the garlands and the Rolls Royce?

Then I saw passengers from Prabhupāda’s flight walking down the ramp. A crowd gathered to greet the arriving passengers. My anxiety grew. Still no sign of the garlands or the Rolls Royce. “What will Prabhupāda think?”

My wife’s eyes were glued to the gate. My eyes moved rapidly between the incoming passenger gate and the terminal doors. I prayed for a glimpse of rose garlands and a Rolls Royce.

I thought that Prabhupāda would be satisfied by the fine garlands and the luxurious transport. “He’ll see that I’m following him properly,” I thought. “But what will he think if … “

Suddenly I saw a glimpse of saffron behind the Customs barrier. Then Prabhupāda, the *guru* of the entire world, my spiritual master, stepped from the gate. His deep eyes and countenance scanned the crowd. “I’m finished,” I thought. “Prabhupāda’s here and there’s no garland and no car!”

At that moment, Prabhupāda’s eyes met mine. He was grave. As if on cue, my friend with the garlands arrived and the Rolls Royce chauffeur walked up a moment later. “Here are the garlands, Bhūrijana.” “The car is ready by the south door.”

“I’m in *māyā,*” I thought, and I fainted.

\* \* \*

I had been imagining a Prabhupāda that allowed a compromise of purity and Kṛṣṇa conscious practices. When I was suddenly confronted by the real Prabhupāda, the illusory Prabhupāda dissolved like mist after sunrise. Prabhupāda, as he himself said, was “90% liberal,” but that other 10% was strict.

Once Prabhupāda invited a disciple to sit with him as he translated *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* in the early morning hours. The disciple declined saying, “I’m already sleeping only five hours a night.” When Prabhupāda replied, “But I am sleeping less,” the disciple squirmed. “Well, you are a *paramahaṁsa.*” Prabhupāda’s answer was strong. “Why you are not *paramahaṁsa*?”

Yes, Prabhupāda was mellow and his association was sweet, but please do not imagine that the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement spread throughout the world by this alone. Prabhupāda also strongly and continuously pushed his disciples and insisted that they strictly follow the rules and regulations and sacrifice everything to satisfy the desires of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

## The Mercy of a Vaiṣṇava

The black Rolls Royce pulled out from the airport and made its way through heavy traffic from the Kowloon side of the harbor to Hong Kong. My wife and I rode with Prabhupāda in the car’s plush back seat. Prabhupāda held his cane as he sat, garlanded with roses, looking like a royal ambassador from the spiritual world. Satsvarūpa Mahārāja sat in the front seat with the driver.

“So nice to see you, Prabhupāda,” I smiled, trying to look and feel cheerful. Prabhupāda smiled and asked my wife about her parents, whom he had met in Australia.

Turning to me, he asked, “What is your morning program?”

“Well, Prabhupāda, we don’t really have so many guests coming right now, so we usually don’t have much of a morning program.” My wife quickly closed the glass window between the front and back seats, separating Satsvarūpa Mahārāja from our conversation.

Prabhupāda continued, “Guests may or may not be coming, but why don’t you have a morning program? Is your temple in ISKCON? Whose disciple are you?”

“Of course I am your disciple, Prabhupāda.” I was getting nervous.

“Then why you don’t have a morning program?”

Like a symphony opening with the clash of cymbals, Prabhupāda pointed out my deficiencies and misconceptions. My attempts to defend myself only increased Prabhupāda’s attack. By the time the Rolls Royce had passed through the Harbour Tunnel and reached the Hong Kong Hilton, I was exhausted.

\* \* \*

It is both the right and the duty of a spiritual master to criticize his disciples. By that criticism, says Cāṇakya Paṇḍita, good qualities develop. We therefore at times hear that it is glorious to be reprimanded by our spiritual master. It *is* glorious to have him weed the *anarthas* from our hearts, but his reprimand is neither romantic nor pleasurable. It is intensely painful. The person we are working so hard to please is angry—even furious—with us. Nor can we rightfully, in defense, shuck the blame to others for our own faults and inabilities. Only by taking responsibility for our actions can we become rectified. Then only can good qualities surface.

I can think of two analogies to describe how this mercy felt: open heart surgery without anesthesia and Biblical Adam’s attempt to hide from God after he had sinned. I remember feeling I had no shelter and nowhere to hide.

Genuine concern for our spiritual lives caused Prabhupāda to act so strongly because he knew that our present path would in time leave no more or Hari Haribol! We had mixed impurity, the enjoying spirit, into our Kṛṣṇa conscious practices and preaching. We had begun to drift. By asking “Whose disciple are you?” Prabhupāda drew the line: I was his disciple and I had to listen to him.

Despite my own inappropriate behavior, however, I was far from aware of all that was causing Prabhupāda’s anger. For example, I didn’t know that during the previous year Gaurasundara, who had successfully been pioneering the preaching in Hawaii since 1968, and who was also Prabhupāda’s GBC representative for South America, had fallen victim to sex and intoxication. Months before his difficulties, Gaurasundara had begun allowing people to worship him as *guru.* Finally he fell down and sold ISKCON’s Honolulu temple. He then disappeared, keeping much money from the temple’s sale.

Prabhupāda had traveled to Hawaii from Los Angeles on his way to Hong Kong, yet Gaurasundara had remained aloof, avoiding Prabhupāda’s association during his two-week stay. Prabhupāda was furious and spoke strongly, condemning false teachers:

**Prabhupāda:** That is not our life, to become an impostor. It must be real person. By training, *yaḥ śāstra-vidhim siddhi,* by training under the teaching of a bona fide spiritual master, the *śāstra,* and then you’ll automatically, the more you become a sincere, I mean to say, servitor of the spiritual master, the more you become qualified to become a teacher in the future. Otherwise where is your qualification? You may cheat others that you are teacher, but you are not actually teacher. You have no lesson, no education. A cheater is another thing. But practically to become a teacher … Just like in a school, teacher … A teacher is appointed. When the certificate is there that he has passed M.A. examination, and he has undergone the training, B.T., Bachelor training, then he’s appointed: “Yes, you can be a teacher.” And then a bogus man, he can be … He cannot be accepted as teacher. So that is going on. A bogus man, they’re becoming teachers. Therefore the world condition is so degraded. A cheater is taking the place of teacher. Therefore the whole world is degraded.  
**Woman Devotee:** If we have a desire to keep these promises, but we have a hard time to do it …  
**Prabhupāda:** As soon as you fail to keep your promise, then you’re fallen. You have to rectify yourself. Immediately you’re fallen. *Yaḥ śāstra-vidhim utsṛjya vartate kāma-kārataḥ, na sa siddhim.* That means you’ll never get perfection. You’ll fall down. You can cheat others that I am in dress of a devotee, so I am … What is your character? What is your actual value? That has to be judged. That is call bona fide teacher.

In addition, Siddha-svarūpa, who had been referring to Gaurasundara as his *śikṣā-guru,* left ISKCON along with his followers. This had seriously affected or closed temples in Hawaii, the Philippines, New Zealand, and Australia. Strain and conflict between Siddha-svarūpa and other disciples of Prabhupāda had always existed over methods used in book distribution—”mellowness” versus a “somehow or other get it done for Prabhupāda” approach—dress, and the depth to which Siddha-svarūpa was revered by his followers. When differences erupted into hostility, however, Siddha-svarūpa left. Prabhupāda had been confronted by these points for weeks prior to his Hong Kong visit:

**Hanumān:** … and they had some temples in Rio de Janeiro and San Pablo, and they were followers of Gaurasundara and Siddha-svarūpa. And I think they’re still there. And when I was there they were making *kīrtana,* and they were chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. In the middle of their *kīrtana,* they would stop their *kīrtana,* and they would chant *Jaya jaya* Siddha-svarūpa, and *Jaya jaya* Gaurasundara. So I could not do anything. I told them it was all wrong and everything, and uh … But there is many, many innocent people who are following. They go there for the first time and they make them chant *Jaya jaya* Siddha-svarūpa and *Jaya jaya* Gaurasundara, like this. So we were telling, we were talking with …  
**Prabhupāda:** There is no harm in giving *“jaya*” to Vaiṣṇavas provided they follow the Vaiṣṇava principles. Otherwise, to glorify Vaiṣṇava is not bad. (pause) No, no *“jaya*” to Mahāprabhu, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu? They chanted?  
**Satsvarūpa:** Were they chanting, *“Jaya* Lord Caitanya, Nityānanda Prabhu?”  
**Hanumān:** Yes, they were also chanting, *“Bhaja śri-kṛṣṇa caitanya*.” Yes. But the point is that they were chanting the name of Siddha-svarūpa Goswami and Gaurasundara Goswami, uh, Gaurasundara Adhikārī.  
**Prabhupāda:** If one is Vaiṣṇava, then to glorify him is … that’s not …  
**Hanumān:** So that’s all right.  
**Satsvarūpa:** Just as long as that Vaiṣṇava is following the principles.  
**Prabhupāda:** But they should not attempt like that in the presence of their spiritual master. That is not good. When you found this?  
**Satsvarūpa:** When did you find they were doing this?  
**Hanumān:** When I was there. I was in Brazil about three months ago. And the only books they had published is a book written by Siddha-svarūpa Goswami. They had published one book of him, and they were distributing this.  
**Prabhupāda:** What is the name of that book?  
**Hanumān:** It was an introduction to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, question and answer.  
**Prabhupāda:** You have seen that book?  
**Hanumān:** Yes, Prabhupāda. I have given to Karandhara.  
**Prabhupāda:** So is there anything awkward against our devotional life?  
**Karandhara:** I can’t read it. It’s in Portuguese.  
**Prabhupāda:** Oh, it was in Portuguese language.  
**Hanumān:** Gaurasundara has written some books, and so Siddha-svarūpa, answering the question, quotes from the book of Gaurasundara, and he mentions, “My professor, Gaurasundara, taught me,” and he quotes many, many times. Practically more from the book of Gaurasundara than from your books.

And on dress in Hawaii:

**Prabhupāda:** Any questions?  
**Devotee:** Yes, one more.  
**Guest:** Is the exterior clothing important?  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes, important. Just like officially the policeman must dress, but a policeman sometimes in ordinary cloth also, that’s his duty. But that is special case. But external, external dress is also required. In the dress of a police if he is a thief, that is very dangerous. That is very dangerous. Just like this dress of *sannyāsī,* saffron cloth, one will respect that “Here is a *sannyāsī.*” But if he is a thief in a dress of a *sannyāsī,* that is dangerous. That is dangerous.

Siddha-svarūpa and his followers had left ISKCON, but they maintained their relationship with Prabhupāda. I was vaguely aware of some of these facts, but Prabhupāda was fully aware of everything, although he was to mention little of the details of these worldwide occurrences to me during his stay. Yet the brunt of his displeasure landed with full force upon Prabhupāda’s arrival in Hong Kong.

## Preaching and Reading

Prabhupāda and his entourage rode the elevator to the top floor of the hotel. We had rented him the Thai Suite, the most luxurious rooms in the hotel. A group of about twenty young, interested Chinese waited inside.

Prabhupāda took his seat. He was pleased by the group of interested souls and he began *kīrtana.* His lecture criticized “those who preached but who were not reading.” “Without *śravaṇa,* what is the *kīrtana*?” he asked the guests as he looked at me.

\* \* \*

Devotees who are too busy preaching to read Prabhupāda’s books should note this instruction. If we don’t study, we may not be prepared to fight *māyā:*

Men become strong and stout by eating sufficient grains, but the devotee who simply eats ordinary grains but does not taste the transcendental pastimes of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Kṛṣṇa gradually becomes weak and falls down from the transcendental position. However, if one drinks but a drop of the nectar of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, his body and mind begin to bloom, and he begins to laugh, sing and dance.

PURPORT: All the devotees connected with this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement must read all the books that have been translated *(Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam, Bhagavad-gītā* and others); otherwise, after some time, they will simply eat, sleep and fall down from their position. Thus they will miss the opportunity to attain an eternal, blissful life of transcendental pleasure.

—Cc., *Madhya* 25.278

## Leading Others

Prabhupāda continued his forceful mood in front of the guests. “So how do you like our lifestyle?” Prabhupāda asked Jagattāriṇī. She paused, looked at Prabhupāda, and ventured, “I don’t know.” Her tentative answer evoked a heavy reply. “You don’t know! You don’t know! You are leading others and you 'don’t know.’”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s words rattled us to the core. Preachers have to practice what they preach. As Prabhupāda told the teachers in the Dallas *gurukula,* “Example is better than precept.”

## An Offering of a Smiling Face

As Prabhupāda spoke to the guests, he asked me to bring him a *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is.* I couldn’t find one. Instant anxiety. Prabhupāda’s movement is based on his books and none were at the hotel. I felt foolish. “We don’t have any *Bhagavad-gītās* here now, Prabhupāda.” He looked at me sternly. “What *do* you have? Only your face.”

\* \* \*

The Vedic literature orders us to approach the spiritual master with an offering of firewood, and the Deity with rice or a flower, fruit, or water. We should not approach the *guru* empty-handed. Our smiling face proudly paraded before him is not always sufficient to bring him pleasure.

## Leaving Temples Behind

Later, when we were alone, Prabhupāda started again. “Why did you leave your old temple? It was small, but you had decorated it with so much devotion.”

\* \* \*

Our old temple was a small, rented flat in a crowded tourist district, Tsim Tsa Tsui, on the Kowloon side of Hong Kong. The temple walls, along with their cloth decorations, were blackened by the city smog, and the kitchen roof leaked badly. The flat itself was beside a nonvegetarian restaurant, and the fumes nauseated us.

Jagattāriṇī had painstakingly decorated that temple’s walls with pictures of Kṛṣṇa. She artistically surrounded the pictures with peacock feathers and imitation jewels. No guest ever expressed appreciation of her work. Now two years later, Prabhupāda remembered the temple and its decorations. Prabhupāda had traveled the world many times since then and visited big American temples, opulent European temples, and seen the work of many powerful devotees. I was moved that Prabhupāda appreciated and remembered our humble devotional offering.

## The Fall of Unqualified Gurus

Prabhupāda began criticizing his disciple, Gaurasundara, and his anger was as intense as the monsoon rains. “He wants to be *guru,* but he is not qualified. All of them, they want to be guru without qualification and they will fall victim to sex life.” Prabhupāda then turned to me. “Your wife belongs to Kṛṣṇa. You cannot even enjoy one wife. They all belong to Kṛṣṇa.” Prabhupāda continued preaching strongly, “All of them. Even that Swami Satcidananda has fallen down with some Western women.”

\* \* \*

I had met Swami Satcidananda, a popular impersonalist *guru* who had “done well” in the West, at Woodstock and then in Hong Kong. I had noted that his traveling companions were two vampish, dressed-in-black, Western secretaries. Prabhupāda’s example of dealing with members of the opposite sex should be noted. As a *sannyāsī,* he once refused to be alone in a room with his elderly and saintly sister. We should follow Prabhupāda’s example and never challenge the laws of material nature by associating loosely with a member of the opposite sex.

## Fortunate, Unfortunate, and Most Unfortunate

The next morning before leaving for his morning walk, Prabhupāda stood with us at the hotel elevator. He was grave, and as he looked straight toward the closed elevator doors, he spoke. “Those who come to Kṛṣṇa consciousness are fortunate. Those who do not come are unfortunate.” Prabhupāda became even more grave. “But those who come and leave before they are mature … “ He paused and turned to Jagattāriṇī and myself,” … they are *most* unfortunate.”

The elevator arrived. Prabhupāda continued to speak from inside. “It seems that some have come to this movement to take.” I immediately objected, “No, Prabhupāda, I don’t think they have.” Without a moment’s pause Prabhupāda countered, “But we want them to take.”

My head spun. “Yes,” Prabhupāda continued, “We want them to take … our rules and regulations!”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda describes give and take in his purport to Mantra Four of *The Nectar of Instruction.* In this exchange, Prabhupāda expressed his consternation that he was freely giving and yet the exchange was incomplete because some did not fully accept his gift of the practices of *sādhana-bhakti.*

## Envy and Falldown

Later, during his noon massage, Prabhupāda asked me, “Why they don’t associate with the other devotees?”

“I think it is difficult for them because so many devotees are envious that Siddha-svarūpa has so many followers.”

“Who is envious?” Prabhupāda challenged.

“Well,” I stammered, and I mentioned one devotee’s name.

Prabhupāda spoke almost in a whisper. “So he has fallen down. He has written me and told me that he was not able to follow the rules properly, and so he has said that he will leave. But because he feels repentant, he will come back.”

\* \* \*

Too often we eagerly correct others and yet remain blind to our own weaknesses. Prabhupāda taught me that criticizing others, even if the criticisms are justified, does not protect one from his own shortcomings. One is often correct about the faults of others, but wrong about his own.

Thinking, “I am right (and sincere) and the other person is wrong (and insincere)” is dangerous. To protect our spiritual lives from offenses to other devotees, we should not be callous to their good intentions, and we should also beware of making criticisms that are neither spoken with affection nor truly meant to help. In addition, Prabhupāda implied in this exchange that a repentant attitude gives one a second chance.

## Friends in Kṛṣṇa Consciousness

“And you? Man is by nature social. If you do not make friendship with the devotees, you will be forced to associate with someone. That will be forced by material nature. If you do not associate with devotees, you will make friendship with the nondevotees. If you make friendship with the nondevotees,” Prabhupāda paused as he spoke, “you will not be able to follow the rules and regulations.”

\* \* \*

asat-saṅga-tyāga,—ei vaiṣṇava-ācāra  
'strī-saṅgī’—eka asādhu, 'kṛṣṇābhakta’ āra

A Vaiṣṇava should always avoid the association of ordinary people. Common people are very much materially attached, especially to women. Vaiṣṇavas should also avoid the company of those who are not devotees of Lord Kṛṣṇa.

—Cc., *Madhya* 22.87

This verse is Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s definition of the essential behavior of a Vaiṣṇava. As we are social by nature, we must, while avoiding the association of nondevotees, satisfy our need for friendship through partaking in deeply affectionate and friendly association amongst the devotees. Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī instructs us how to do so:

dadāti pratigṛhṇāti  
guhyam ākhyāti pṛcchati  
bhuṇkte bhojayate caiva  
ṣaḍ-vidhaṁ prīti-lakṣaṇam

Offering gifts in charity, accepting charitable gifts, revealing one’s mind in confidence, inquiring confidentially, accepting prasāda and offering prasāda are the six symptoms of love shared by one devotee and another.

PURPORT: In this verse Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī explains how to perform devotional activities in the association of other devotees. There are six kinds of activities: (1) giving charity to the devotees, (2) accepting from the devotees whatever they may offer in return, (3) opening one’s mind to the devotees, (4) inquiring from them about the confidential service of the Lord, (5) honoring prasāda, or spiritual food, given by the devotees, and (6) feeding the devotees with prasāda. … Whenever there is a dealing of *prīti,* or love in intimate dealings, these six activities are executed. In the previous verse, Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī advised that one should renounce worldly association and keep company with the devotees *(saṅga-tyāgāt sato vṛtteḥ*). The International Society for Krishna Consciousness has been established to facilitate these six kinds of loving exchanges between devotees.

—*The Nectar of Instruction*, *Mantra Four*

## Invisible Headaches

“Prabhupāda, some devotees seem very Kṛṣṇa conscious, but they seem, on the other hand, to have displeased you.”

Prabhupāda illustrated the situation with an example: “If the telephone is ringing and a man does not want to answer, he will say,” and Prabhupāda pointed to his head and grimaced, “'Oh, I have a headache. I cannot come.’ Can the actual situation be detected?”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s example showed how internal, unverifiable symptoms of advancement cannot become the criteria for accepting a devotee as advanced, or for accepting a devotee as one’s spiritual master. An advanced devotee’s qualification, however, can be directly observed by his behavior: he is fixed as a pure servant of his Guru Mahārāja and Kṛṣṇa. Requiring internal and esoteric qualifications opens the door for being cheated. Prabhupāda clarifies this in his purport to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 3.33.10:

Sometimes doubts arise in the mind of neophytes about whether or not the spiritual master is liberated, and sometimes neophytes are doubtful about the bodily affairs of the spiritual master. The point of liberation, however, is not to see the bodily symptoms of the spiritual master. One has to see the spiritual symptoms of the spiritual master. *Jīvan-mukta* means that even though one is in the material body (there are still some material necessities, since the body is material), because one is fully situated in the service of the Lord, he should be understood to be liberated.

Liberation entails being situated in one’s own position. That is the definition in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*: *muktir … svarūpeṇa vyavasthitiḥ.* The *svarūpa,* or actual identity of the living entity, is described by Lord Caitanya. *Jīvera 'svarūpa’ haya—kṛṣṇera 'nitya-dāsa*’: the real identity of the living entity is that he is eternally a servitor of the Supreme Lord. If someone is one-hundred-percent engaged in the service of the Lord, he is to be understood as liberated. One must understand whether or not he is liberated by his activities in devotional service, not by other symptoms.

## The Respect of a Vaiṣṇava

When Prabhupāda arrived, we had offered him a 24-karat gold ring with a raised “P” on it, a gold necklace with a “P”, and a gold bracelet engraved with the full word, Prabhupāda. We had also arranged a Rolls Royce to carry him from the airport to his luxurious hotel suite. Prabhupāda appreciated our recognition of his position as the representative of Kṛṣṇa and told Satsvarūpa Mahārāja, “This is a great credit for the one who has made these arrangements.”

\* \* \*

I had asked Sudāmā Vipra Swami how to greet and honor Prabhupāda, and I made opulent arrangements according to his advice. When I heard both Prabhupāda’s appreciation of our offerings and his strong criticism, I said,”Prabhupāda, I know you appreciated the arrangements we made for you. I learned this from Sudāmā Vipra Swami. So he helped me.”

Prabhupāda’s reply was dour. “That is nice. They may have helped you in the past, but they cannot help you anymore.”

“Then how should I treat him and Siddha-svarūpa now?”

“You should treat them with respect,” Prabhupāda replied. “A Vaiṣṇava treats everyone with respect, even an ant!”

\* \* \*

Vaiṣṇava culture is steeped in etiquette. The practice of etiquette is neither superficial nor shallow, but it is meant to evoke the pleasure of Kṛṣṇa and His servants and to avoid offenses. A cultured gentleman would not have needed to ask how to treat someone else. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu taught, “Give all respect to others and require no respect for yourself.”

## Final Instructions: Arrows on Tape

Early on the last morning of Prabhupāda’s stay, Satsvarūpa Mahārāja told me Prabhupāda wanted to see Jagattāriṇī and I. I ran to Prabhupāda’s room and offered my obeisances. My wife chose to remain outside the door and listen from there. Prabhupāda sat on a chair at a thin wooden desk. He was chanting softly to a large, black and white photo of “Gopāla Kṛṣṇa” framed on his desk.

“Play the tape,” he said, and he pointed with his saffron bead bag to the dictaphone on a nearby table.

It was a tape of Prabhupāda’s previous night’s dictation of the Bhaktivedanta purports to *Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya* 19.151–57. I turned the machine on and heard Prabhupāda describe the planting and protection of the *bhakti-latā-bīja.*

brahmāṇḍa bhramite kona bhāgyavān jīva  
guru-kṛṣṇa-prasāde pāya bhakti-latā-bīja

The word *guru-prasāda* indicates that the spiritual master is very merciful in bestowing the boon of devotional service upon the disciple. That is the best possible gift the spiritual master has to offer … Endowed with the mercy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, the spiritual master distributes the mercy to those who are elevated and pious. Thus the spiritual master trains his disciples to render service to the Supreme Personality of Godhead. This is called *guru-kṛpā.* It is *kṛṣṇa-prasāda,* Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, that he sends a bona fide spiritual master to the deserving disciple. By the mercy of Kṛṣṇa, one meets the bona fide spiritual master, and by the mercy of the spiritual master, the disciple is fully trained in the devotional service of the Lord. …

The methods, rules and regulations by which one is perfectly trained in devotional service constitute the *bhakti-latā-bīja,* or the seed of devotional service. This is received from the spiritual master by the grace of Kṛṣṇa. … The *bhakti-latā-bīja* can be received only through the mercy of the spiritual master. Therefore one has to satisfy the spiritual master to get *bhakti-latā-bīja.* The *bhakti-latā-bīja* is the origin of devotional service. Unless one satisfies the spiritual master, he gets the *bīja,* or root cause, of *karma, jñāna* and *yoga* without the benefit of devotional service. However, one who is faithful to his spiritual master gets the *bhakti-latā-bīja.* This *bhakti-latā-bīja* is received when one is initiated by the bona fide spiritual master. After receiving the spiritual master’s mercy, one must repeat his instructions, and this is called hearing and chanting. One who has not properly heard from the spiritual master or who does not follow the regulative principles is not fit for chanting *(kīrtana*). One who has not listened carefully to the instructions of the spiritual master is unfit to chant or preach the cult of devotional service. One has to water the *bhakti-latā-bīja* after receiving instructions from the spiritual master.

One’s devotional attitude increases in the association of a Vaiṣṇava.

tāṅḍera caraṇa sevi bhakta-sane vāsa  
janame janame haya, ei abhilāṣa

By his personal example, Narottama dāsa Ṭhākura stresses that a devotee must always remember to please his predecessor *ācārya.* The Gosvāmīs are represented by one’s spiritual master. One cannot be an *ācārya* (spiritual master) without following strictly in the disciplic succession of the *ācāryas.* One who is actually serious in advancing in devotional service should desire only to satisfy the previous *ācāryas. Ei chaya gosāñi yāra, mui tāra dāsa.* One should always think of oneself as a servant of the servant of the *ācāryas,* and thinking this, one should live in the society of Vaiṣṇavas. However, if one thinks that he has become very mature and can live separate from the association of Vaiṣṇavas and thus gives up all the regulative principles due to offending a Vaiṣṇava, one’s position becomes very dangerous. … Giving up the regulative principles and living according to one’s whims are compared to a mad elephant, which by force uproots the *bhakti-latā* and breaks it to pieces. In this way the *bhakti-latā* shrivels up. Such an offense is especially created when one disobeys the instructions of the spiritual master. This is called *guru-avajñā.* The devotee must therefore be very careful not to commit offenses against the spiritual master. As soon as one is deviated from the spiritual master, the uprooting of the *bhakti-latā* begins, and gradually all the leaves dry up.

While the *bhakti-latā* creeper is growing, the devotee must protect it by fencing it all around. The neophyte devotee must be protected by being surrounded by pure devotees. In this way he will not give the maddened elephant a chance to uproot his *bhakti-latā* creeper. When one associates with nondevotees, the maddened elephant is set loose. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu has said: *asat-saṅga-tyāga*—*ei vaiṣṇava-ācāra.* The first business of a Vaiṣṇava is to give up the company of nondevotees. A so-called mature devotee, however, commits a great offense by giving up the company of pure devotees.

What follows are Prabhupāda’s words directly as they were copied from the original transcript of his dictated tape. Thus some words are different from the edited purport currently published by the BBT.

The living entity is a social animal. If one gives up the society of pure devotees he must associate with the society of *asat-saṅga,* the association of nondevotees. By contact of nondevotees and nondevotional activities the so-called mature devotee becomes a victim of the mad elephant offense. Therefore whatever little growth there was of the devotional creeper is uprooted by such offensive activity. Therefore one must be very careful to defend the creeper with fencing by following the regulative principles and associating with pure devotees.
