Prabhupāda asked the bishop to examine the book, *A Scientific Basis for Krishna Consciousness,* but the bishop didn’t have his glasses. Prabhupāda then lent him his own glasses and his prescription perfectly suited the bishop. Prabhupāda laughed, “Birds of the same feather.”

They discussed the perfect but covered nature of the soul, the importance of hearing *śāstra* to remove the covering, and the possibility of communing directly with God. Prabhupāda clarified the bishop’s fear that accepting the validity of subjective communication with God would increase the number of irreligious acts perpetrated in His name: “God told me to do it.”

**Prabhupāda:** There is individual instruction, but that is subordinate. The general instruction is that one should be fully surrendered to God. That is the general instruction. Now, if one is fully surrendered, in a particular case in particular circumstances, God gives him instructions what to do. … According to circumstances He gives him. But general instruction is there, and that is recorded in scripture. That general instruction must be followed. One cannot say, “God is dictating through me something against the general instruction.” That is not possible. The general instruction must be followed.

As their conversation continued, Prabhupāda requested that the bishop hear a tape of a recent class he had given, and as the bishop and his friend sat in their chairs and listened, Prabhupāda leaned his head to the side and appeared to doze.

The tape finished and the bishop expressed appreciation for Prabhupāda’s message. Wishing Prabhupāda “Godspeed,” the bishop and his friend, with *prasādam* in hand and in a jolly mood, left the cottage.

Later that evening Prabhupāda remarked to his secretary that the Christians should give up their meat-eating and then we could combine and preach with them. But first they must give up their meat-eating.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda seemed to consider the bishop sincere, and therefore expressed hope that a cooperative preaching effort—Kṛṣṇa consciousness and Christianity—might one day occur. Yet I recently heard why Prabhupāda may have played the tape for the bishop instead of speaking directly himself. After the bishop left, Prabhupāda revealed to the devotees that he had smelled alcohol on the bishop’s breath and so didn’t wish to preach to him further.

## Remaining a Fool

Prabhupāda sat at his low desk, satisfied and smiling. He spoke about the humility of advanced devotees. I was in a light, superficial mood. “My only qualification,” I added, “is that I am a fool.”

Prabhupāda looked at me sharply. “Yes, but you should not remain a fool.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s answer cut through the superficiality of my childish comment. Actual devotees are humble, but they are never fools.

## An Invitation

I drove up to Prabhupāda’s beachfront cottage just as he and his entourage were leaving for the morning walk. I quickly parked my car, got out, and offered my obeisances on the pavement. Prabhupāda waved to me, gesturing that I too should join his walk.

\* \* \*

It was exhilarating to receive an invitation directly from Prabhupāda to associate with him.

## Prabhupāda’s Only Criticism of the Devotees

Later, we sat at Prabhupāda’s feet and listened to him preach. Material considerations were insignificant as long as we sat there. We wanted to become like he treated us. He treated us as if we were already sincere and compassionate devotees of Kṛṣṇa. By his association, we wanted to help a thankless world, a world that was caught up in fruitless trivialities and deadly misconceptions. We were neophytes, but Prabhupāda’s association made Kṛṣṇa real to us and devotional service the glory of life. Prabhupāda spoke intimately with us, like comrades in battle.

“These books are for you,” Prabhupāda said as he pointed to several of the books he had written. “Do not make it an export business. They only buy and sell to others. For themselves they have no use.” Prabhupāda imitated a devotee distributing his books, “Please take this book. It is very important.” He then took the customer’s role: “Oh, it is important? What is it about?” Prabhupāda again switched roles. “I don’t know, it is for you!” We all laughed sheepishly. “My only criticism of the devotees is that they think my books are for distribution and not for reading.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda wanted his disciples to become philosophically strong by studying his books. Śāstric study grants us the ability to preach, fixes our intelligence, and purifies our hearts. At this time Prabhupāda also asked the devotees,”Why are we so interested in book distribution? Because it is distribution of knowledge. It is distribution of knowledge.”

## A Farewell Darśana—the Greatest Gift

Prabhupāda was preparing to leave Melbourne. Madhudviṣa Swami, to encourage the *saṅkīrtana* devotees, arranged for Prabhupāda to meet them just prior to his departure. The *saṅkīrtana* devotees entered Prabhupāda’s room and I followed behind.

Prabhupāda was somber. The devotees asked questions and Prabhupāda dutifully answered them as his servant finished packing. Still sitting, Prabhupāda reached over his desktop to the vase and presented each devotee with a yellow daffodil. Burly Ugraśrava, the *saṅkīrtana* leader, then stepped forward with a dark saffron case in his hand. He eyed Prabhupāda’s white case.

Prabhupāda was using that white case to carry his valuables—his travel documents and other papers, money, and keys. When Prabhupāda stayed in hotels, he even carried the case with him on his walks, not trusting to leave it in his room.

Ugraśrava had bought Prabhupāda an expensive new, slightly larger suitcase, which was also suitable for Prabhupāda to carry while traveling. “Prabhupāda,” Ugraśrava said, “we’ve bought you this new, better quality case for your valuables.” Prabhupāda was amused. It was clear that Ugraśrava wanted Prabhupāda’s white case.

“Oh,” Prabhupāda said, his eyes opening wide. “You are giving me?”

Ugraśrava smiled widely. Prabhupāda was accepting his gift. “Yes, Prabhupāda,” he said, as his eyes turned to Prabhupāda’s white suitcase.

“Very good,” said Prabhupāda. He then proceeded to place the white case inside the new, bigger case and zip it closed. Ugraśrava’s mouth fell open.

Prabhupāda’s eyes laughed, but he remained silent. He looked up at Ugraśrava and slowly unzipped the saffron case. He held up the white one, “Did you want this?” Ugraśrava nodded his head sheepishly. After emptying its contents, Prabhupāda presented the white case to Ugraśrava.

\* \* \*

Ugraśrava was an aggressive, “heavy” person who in time left the association of devotees, but because of his affection for Prabhupāda, Ugraśrava, by Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, will not be vanquished.

## The Essence of Spiritual Life

During his visit, Prabhupāda asked me to go to Africa. He later agreed to my offer to preach in Singapore, suggesting I dress in nondevotee clothing, open a bookstore, and keep an apartment. After Prabhupāda left, I went to Singapore. Prabhupāda was pleased and sent me the following letter:

My Dear Spiritual Son Bhurijana dasa:  
Please accept my blessings. I am very glad to receive your letter dated 8/7/74 from Singapore. I am very pleased that you have gone to that place simply on my order, alone, to preach the message of Lord Caitanya. I like this kind of spirit in you very much. You know I went to your country also alone, in old age, because of the order given to me by my spiritual master. So I get great pleasure when I see my own disciples from America going to far off places leaving their rich nation with all facilities and their families, to preach. The secret of success in spiritual life is to satisfy one’s spiritual master. This is the essence. You have captured this. So now go on in this way and do not falter. Follow the regulative principles and devotional practices I have given you. This will keep you fit. Be patient and determined, and ask everyone you meet to please chant Hare Krishna. I shall be very glad if you send me regular reports of your attempt.  
I hope this meets you and also your good wife in best of health.

Your ever well wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

We can sum up the success formula of spiritual life in a single, short sentence: one must satisfy one’s spiritual master—*yasya prasādād bhagavat-prasādo.* The spiritual strength to do this is invoked by following the regulative principles and the standard devotional practices. Then on our *guru’s* order, we can patiently and determinedly preach by asking everyone we meet to please chant *Hare Kṛṣṇa.*

## Melbourne, 1975

Melbourne, 1975

## Ready or Not, Prabhupāda is Coming!

After a few months of lone preaching in Singapore and Malaysia, I returned to Melbourne and took a job to support my family. My wife delivered our daughter on the day I returned to Australia. When I informed Prabhupāda, he wrote: "Now Krishna has blessed you with a daughter. The name Vrindaban Leela you have chosen is very nice." I gradually started a tapestry handbag business, which freed me from the torture of working for others. I was living as an independent householder at this time, and Prabhupāda, accepting that I would not be returning to Hong Kong or Singapore, instructed me through letters how to properly execute the duties of my *āśrama*.

In 1974, ISKCON Melbourne was in a rented house in the down-and-out suburb of St. Kilda. The devotees searched for a new temple during Prabhupāda's 1974 visit, but they could not settle upon one until after Prabhupāda had left. The new property, a historic building near South Melbourne, was at this time one of ISKCON’s best properties outside India. Prabhupāda agreed to come for the official opening in 1975.

The devotees worked frantically day and night to lay the marble floor in the temple room, prepare Prabhupāda's quarters, and paint the building. As Prabhupāda's arrival approached, the budding was nowhere near completion. The devotees asked Prabhupāda to “wait for some time” in Perth, Western Australia, until the building was ready He complied, but when the “some time” proved too long, he decided to fly, ready or not, to Melbourne.

## Foolish Questions, Perfect Answers

Prabhupāda’s flight to Melbourne landed and he was escorted to a press conference in the airport's VIP lounge.\* A hundred jubilant devotees sat on the floor as reporters questioned Prabhupāda, who sat with Madhudviṣa on a raised dais. The questions were the usual:

**Reporter**: What is your name?  
**Prabhupāda**: A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami.  
**Reporter**: What do the “A” and the “C” stand for?  
**Prabhupāda**: If I say, you will not understand.  
**Reporter**: Please, I’d like to know what the “A” and the “C” stand for?  
**Prabhupāda**: You will not understand.  
**Reporter**: What do the “A” and the “C” stand for?

Prabhupāda then said “Abhay Caranaravindam.” He pronounced “Abhay Caranaravindam” so fast that even I could not understand his words.

**Reporter**: I’m sorry, I couldn’t follow that. What did you say?  
**Prabhupāda**: Abhay Caranaravindam. *\[Prabhupāda again quickly pronounced his name.\]*  
**Reporter**: Could you repeat that again?  
**Prabhupāda**: I told you that you would not understand.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was clearly unimpressed by the reporter’s superficial question, and he playfully outmaneuvered him as easily as an adult outmaneuvers a child.

## Sleeping Only Six Hours Each Night?

A second reporter began with a more pointed question.

**Reporter Two**: I’ve heard that your disciples work all day but only get to sleep six hours at night. I wanted to ask you how many hours each night you sleep?  
**Prabhupāda**: I generally go to sleep at ten o’clock at night and wake up at twelve. I then do my translating work.  
**Madhudviṣa**: *\[Interrupting in a protective mood.\]* Prabhupāda doesn’t sleep much at all. He sleeps less, not more than his disciples. He doesn't sleep more than four hours a night.  
**Prabhupāda**: No, not four hours. I go to sleep at ten and awake at twelve. How many hours is that?

\* \* \*

The reporter should have recognized Prabhupāda as an elderly holy man traveling around the world on a God conscious mission. Instead, he was trying to expose that Prabhupāda was a “cult leader” and that he exploited his followers. These unfortunate and befooled followers, he was trying to imply, were working hard to support the luxuriant and easy living of their cult master. Prabhupāda revealed the opposite: although his disciples were working hard, he was not exploiting them for personal pleasure. Both he and his disciples were working hard for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure. Rather than using his disciples to finance his life of ease as the reporter was hoping to prove, he worked even harder than his disciples in the service of his Guru Mahārāja and Kṛṣṇa.

## Support in Kṛṣṇa Consciousness

After the press conference, Prabhupāda went to the new Melbourne temple. When he saw the temple complex, he was impressed. One evening, I accompanied him as he walked from his room to the separate temple building. As we neared the temple room door, he turned to me and said, “You could have done this in Hong Kong.”

I felt the sting of my spiritual master’s disappointment. I fumbled and expressed to him my stock of excuses, “How could I? I was alone. The local Indians are materialistic and the Chinese are not interested and are atheists …”

Prabhupāda looked at me for a moment and then continued to the temple for his lecture.

\* \* \*

Although I am from New York City, I easily rate Hong Kong as the most intensely materialistic city that I have ever seen. As a preaching field, Hong Kong is far from a ripe fruit waiting to be plucked. It’s a tough place to live.

Prabhupāda nevertheless knew that Kṛṣṇa consciousness could spread in Hong Kong. He knew that devotees could be made and a substantial temple could be established. He had hoped I could do this service, but I was unable. He was disappointed. Although I felt unqualified to do the service, he felt I was able.

Quite often it is a teacher’s sustained faith in a student that empowers the student to reach his potential. When I reflect back on these words in particular, I am inspired by Prabhupāda’s faith in me, which was even greater than the faith I had in myself.

## Non-attachment to a Good Ring

Śrutakīrti vigorously massaged sandalwood oil into Prabhupāda’s head. I suddenly noticed that he still wore the gold ring with the engraved “P” on it that I had given him when he visited Hong Kong in 1974.

“You still have the ring,” I said in near surprise. “Yes,” said Prabhupāda, “It is a good ring.”

\* \* \*

That Prabhupāda not only accepted but wore my offering for years thrilled me. I saw him wearing the ring many times. Because the gold was pure and soft and the ring-size expandable, he would at times wear it on one finger and then move it to another.

Years after Prabhupāda’s departure from this world, I learned what finally became of that ring. Prabhupāda's “son” had come to see him in Calcutta and had requested him again and again for money for bangles for his daughter. Prabhupāda finally give him the ring and he left, satisfied. Prabhupāda then turned to his servant and said, “I am not attached.”

## Hearing Accurately

As Śrutakīrti continued massaging Prabhupāda, I handed Prabhupāda a stainless steel case with its top screwed on tightly. Inside was pure sandalwood oil, a gift passed from a devotee through me to Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda lifted the metal case in his soft hands and said, “This case is for kajal.”\*

“No, Prabhupāda,” I replied, “it holds sandalwood oil.” Prabhupāda insisted, “No, this case is for *kajal.”* Thinking that Prabhupāda may not have understood me, I again repeated that the case contained sandalwood oil.

Prabhupāda replied sharply, “There may be sandalwood oil inside, but the case is meant for *kajal.”*

Prabhupāda had understood my words perfectly, but I hadn’t understood him. When I finally understood, I felt embarrassed and foolish.

\*Black collyriurn eye-liner used in India to strenghen and beautify the eyes.

\* \* \*

## Women, Widows, and Success

Śrutakīrti then rubbed mustard oil onto Prabhupāda’s back. I moved to face Prabhupāda’s front and was enjoying the sweet atmosphere that surrounded him. With his eyes closed, Prabhupāda spoke. “My Godbrothers criticize me that I have allowed women to live in our temples. This is not done in India. Only *brahmacārīs* can live. But I have become successful because I have made this adjustment.”

Prabhupāda opened his eyes and continued, “Although they criticize me, their Gaudiya Maths are empty. The only time they have people coming is during Gaura-Pūrṇimā to Māyāpur for *parikrama.* And who is coming for *parikrama?* Widows. Women in white. Because I have made this adjustment I was successful.”

## Adjusting Principles

Śrutakīrti, as he moved to massage Prabhupāda’s side, asked, “Prabhupāda, how can we tell the difference between making an adjustment and changing a principle?”

Prabhupāda’s eyes closed and Śrutakīrti continued to massage. After a few moments, Prabhupāda opened his eyes and said, “That takes a little intelligence.”

\* \* \*

Śrutakīrti’s question was penetrating. Clearly delineating between the adjustable and the non-adjustable, between a detail and a principle, is not easy. It requires intelligence. To foolishly mistake a non-adjustable principle for an adjustable detail will cause havoc in spiritual life, and to accept each detail as if it can never be adjusted is fanaticism. Fanaticism will cause havoc of a different nature.

If we have developed our spiritual intelligence, we can make distinctions between principles and details, but we should confirm our opinions with trusted friends or superiors. If we haven’t developed our spiritual intelligence, we should take guidance from those who have. We should not act independently.

## One Householder’s “Good” Business

I spoke to Prabhupāda about my tapestry handbag business, which was doing well. I was able to send Prabhupāda donations every week. Still, I felt guilty that I was neither serving Prabhupāda in Hong Kong nor living in the temple.

I informed Prabhupāda about the items I was selling, describing them in an almost derogatory tone, but Prabhupāda was enthusiastic and he called the business “good.”

I was surprised and asked, “I’m selling handbags. How is that good?”

Prabhupāda looked at me soberly. “One has to do something.

\* \* \*

A householder living outside the temple must support his family. By Kṛṣṇa’s mercy I both supported my family through a relatively sinless enterprise and was able to send weekly donations to Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda’s appreciation of these small successes led him to term my business “good.”

## Free Books

Wishing to distribute books, but fearing the repercussions of the collecting techniques the devotees used, I asked Prabhupāda whether or not books could be purchased and distributed for free. His reply: “As far as possible.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda did not set up the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust to publish books and distribute them for free. Books were to be sold so the Book Trust could make a profit and publish more books. Prabhupāda generally extolled the glories of selling his books as opposed to distributing them for free. In 1968 he even said that anyone who turns his books into money would go back to Godhead. Collecting money through book distribution is practical. The exchange of energy—money for books—makes people take the books more seriously. Having paid for the book, they will be more likely to read it.

Secondly, devotees require money to maintain the temples and expand the preaching. Money from book distribution can fulfill ISKCON’s needs for money while still allowing the devotee book distributors to speak all day about Kṛṣṇa.

Still, Prabhupāda also allowed us to distribute his books for free. Book distribution wherein a wealthy person buys books and distributes them in charity is successful both in India and Russia. Thousands *of Bhagavad-gītās* have been distributed to wealthy factory owners, who then present them to their workers.

## A “Just See” Story

Stories often floated around about Prabhupāda. I had recently heard a fantastic story from several devotees who obviously accepted it as true. I had my doubts and decided to ask Prabhupāda directly.

“Prabhupāda, some devotees told me a story that I wanted to ask you about. The story was that you were in your room with Viṣṇujana Mahārāja and he asked you about your *rasa*. The way I heard the story was that you started glowing brighter and brighter until Viṣṇujana Mahārāja crawled out of the room. Did that happen?”

Prabhupāda smiled, leaned back, and expanded his smile even further. “Just see,” he said while shaking his head.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s “just see” and his expression told it all. The remainder *of* the sentence—“the nonsense that people are speaking”—was clearly understood.

## The Once-and-for-all Answer

Another time, as Prabhupāda took his massage, I asked him, “What is the position of devotees living outside?” Prabhupāda replied, “I shall answer this question once and for all times in the lecture tonight.”

As usual, Prabhupāda’s class that evening was attended by all the devotees, and as the devotees waited for Prabhupāda to arrive, an air of expectancy electrified the atmosphere. I was especially eager to hear Prabhupāda’s lecture.

Prabhupāda proceeded with his class, and as I listened, I thought on several occasions that Prabhupāda was obliquely answering the question. At the end of the lecture Prabhupāda mentioned, “it is sometimes asked about living outside the temple for a devotee.” He then began his answer. “Just as one does not have to be a member of the stock exchange to do business. He can do business without being a member. But if one is a member of the stock exchange, his doing business becomes easy because all of his associations are to do with business. Similarly, one can be a devotee and live outside the temple, but completing his devotional business is easier if he lives inside.”

\* \* \*

During these years devotees often asked Prabhupāda the “inside/outside” question. ISKCON’s general view was clear and simple: those living in the temple were devotees and those living outside were demons. This did not represent Prabhupāda’s idea, however. In the following excerpt from a morning walk (Hawaii, 1975), Prabhupāda thoroughly explains the inside/outside controversy:

**Siddha-Svarūpa**: It is very difficult to put everyone who is living outside the temple in one category. Some people, they are, many people that I know living outside are following strictly the regulative principles, and they are …  
**Prabhupāda**: Huh?  
**Siddha-Svarūpa**: They are strictly following regulative principles and chanting their rounds and having morning *ārati* in their homes. And evening also, they are chanting. So instead of fighting, I think we should only try to encourage everybody to chant and follow the regulative principles.  
**Prabhupāda**: No, that is … Whether you live in temple or outside temple, the rules and regulation and the process must be followed. Then you are successful. It doesn’t matter that you have to live in the temple. *Gṛhe thāko, vane thāko, sadā ‘hari’ bole’ ḍāko.* Not that everyone has to live in the temple. If he does not agree with other Godbrothers, friends, he can live separately, but he must follow the rules and regulations. That is wanted. But if you live with devotees, it will be automatically done.  
**Siddha-Svarūpa**: Easy.  
**Prabhupāda**: Therefore it is recommended that you live with devotees. But you cannot make a new opinion so far the process is concerned. That must be followed. This is not good idea: “Whatever I do, it is my independence, and I will chant.” So that is good in the sense that some day he will come to senses. Otherwise, for the time being, the chant is not very powerful. The fire in wet wood is not powerful. It will create some smoke. Although the fire is there. But if you put dry wood, immediately it will be blazing and your business will be quickly done. This is intelligence. There are many examples. A patient suffering from disease, a doctor said, “You should do; you should not do.” So if we follow “You should not do,” then it becomes quickly recovered. But if he becomes under the treatment of the doctor at the same time he does all nonsense, then how it can be successful? It will take time. That is stated in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta. Pāpi jane aparādha āchāya pracūra.* Offenseless chanting is the ultimate goal. In the beginning we are not offenseless, but by chanting, chanting, by practice, we gradually become offenseless. But this is necessary, that you should be offenseless.  
**Rameśvara**: Śrīla Prabhupāda, it seems unfortunate that if the devotees cannot live in the temples, then they have to work for some *karmī* just to support themselves and then they do not have time to go on the *saṅkīrtana* party. So it is such mercy to be on the *saṅkīrtana* party. it seems very unfortunate that they do not have the time.  
**Prabhupāda**: No, then they should live in the temple if they want to give service in the *saṅkīrtana* party.  
**Rameśvara**: We always try to encourage them to come back to the temple.  
**Prabhupāda**: Yes. if he does not join the *saṅkīrtana* party, that does not mean his spiritual life is hampered. He has to follow the rules and regulations. He may not be able to join the *saṅkīrtana* party, but he must follow the process, rules and regulations. That is wanted. And because he is living outside the temple, therefore he will forget all rules and regulations and do whatever he likes—then it will ruin the whole thing.  
**Devotee (1)**: Śrīla Prabhupāda, is it faster if one lives in the temple and goes on the *saṅkīrtana* party? Is it faster?  
**Prabhupāda**: That depends on him. Even in the temple, if his mind is in a different subject matter, then how it will help him?  
**Bali Mardana**: They like to preach that anyone who’s living outside the temple is going to hell.  
**Prabhupāda**: Generally.  
**Bali Mardana**: But even devotees who are following. They like to preach that.  
**Prabhupāda**: No, that is not. That is not. Just like even in ordinary business, if you transact business in the stock association, you get good business. And outside the stock association you don’t get. Because association is there, there are many purchaser and many seller. So if you have to sell, you get immediate purchaser, and if you have to purchase, there is immediate seller … Therefore the stock exchange is there. That is the way, that if we live together in the stock exchange of devotional service, then you can help me, I can help you. So our business will go on nicely. And outside the market, you can live three hundred miles away from the stock exchange. You will not get so much business. Like that.  
**Devotee (2)**: You’ll miss the opportunities.  
**Prabhupāda**: Yes, therefore it is helpful. if you want to do business, you must take the first opportunity, the greatest opportunity, do your business. That is intelligence. And if we think, “All right, I shall do slowly. In seven hundred lives I shall become perfect,” that is another thing.  
**Bali Mardana**: It is riskier to stay outside.  
**Prabhupāda**: Oh, yes. Otherwise why you are opening so many centers and making arrangements: “We shall provide you with shelter, with food. These are the facilities. You live here, do whatever is your capacity. Don’t sleep, but work.” This is our teaching. *Satāṁ prasaṅgān,* this is also, and Rūpa Gosvāmī says, *sato vṛtteḥ sādhu-saṅge ṣaḍbhir bhaktiḥ prasidhyati:* “If you live with the association of *sādhu,* devotee, then it will be quickly fruitful.” And if you live with these ordinary men, then whatever you have got will be finished very soon. There is another verse. It is said there that it is preferred to live within a cage surrounded by fire than to live with the nondevotees. It is preferred.

## Going Through the Gate First

Living outside the temple and being somewhat at odds with the local management, I was not allowed easy access to Prabhupāda. I was kept at a distance and neither invited for *darśanas* nor for morning walks.

Prabhupāda wanted me to return to Hong Kong because no steady replacement had been found, despite the year that had passed. I gained strength by reading the second half of the Eighteenth Chapter of *Bhagavad-gītā* and resolved, like Arjuna, to surrender. Although I was willing to return to Hong Kong, however, I realized that Jagattāriṇī could not. It was simply too difficult for her. While I would go, she could remain behind in Australia. I collected these thoughts and, not having the facility to speak with Prabhupāda, typed them into a letter. After *Bhagavad-gītā* class that evening I handed the letter to Prabhupāda as he stepped down from his *vyāsāsana.*

The next morning I rode my Suzuki motorcycle behind the motorcade of cars heading for the Melbourne Royal Botanical Gardens. By the time I arrived, the devotees were already walking through the lush, green garden paths. They clustered protectively around Prabhupāda like bees around their queen. I could not get close.

Suddenly I remembered my “waiting at the bottom of the escalator” trick from Prabhupāda’s 1974 visit to Melbourne. “When Prabhupāda leaves the gardens,” I thought, “the devotees must allow him to walk first through the exit gate. I’ll wait on the other side of the gate.”

I rushed through the gate just before the walk was over and waited. The devotees soon came into sight. As they neared the gate, I watched Prabhupāda break from the cluster and walk through the gate. When he emerged, I greeted him with a smile and my obeisances. He looked at me and said, “I have received your letter. Come and see me at any time.”

I was not strong in my Kṛṣṇa consciousness at this time. I mostly saw others’ faults and neglected my own. We can just imagine how Prabhupāda must have viewed his young disciples who at times were childishly divided into factions, thinking that they were more pure or sincere than others. Childish as they were, Prabhupāda maintained, counseled, and either directly or indirectly trained his disciples in spiritual life as long as they honored the sanctity of the relationship.

## For the Future

Since Prabhupāda said I could see him at any time, I went to his room late one afternoon just as a social worker was leaving. Prabhupāda handed him a piece of *sandeśa prasādam* as the man mumbled something about a previously made appointment, offered his thanks, and left. Prabhupāda and the devotees—Madhudviṣa Swami, Ugraśrava, Amogha, and others—laughed.

Prabhupāda then looked at me. “I have read your letter. You should not take *sannyāsa* now.” The devotees’ laughter increased, thinking I had asked Prabhupāda permission to take *sannyāsa* in my letter.

“How old are you now?” Prabhupāda asked.

“Twenty-eight.”

“How old is your daughter?”

“Only two.”

Prabhupāda smiled and replied, “Householder is also an *āśrama.* It is also meant for spiritual advancement. You should remain a householder, and when you are fifty, your daughter will be twenty-four and can be married. Your wife can live with her. When you are fifty you can think about taking *sannyāsa.”*

\* \* \*

In this conversation, Prabhupāda did not mention anything about my preaching in Hong Kong. Instead he ordered me not to take *sannyāsa* until I was fifty. I understood from his reply that I should not at present return to Hong Kong, but I was nevertheless puzzled after hearing his words.

Weeks later, as I walked down Flinders Street in downtown Melbourne, it hit me. “Prabhupāda said I should continue in householder life until I am fifty. Then at that time, but not before, I can consider changing my household situation. Prabhupāda is instructing me now that when I am fifty, my situation and service may be changed, including the possibility of leaving my wife and family for preaching.”

## Interplanetary Eagles

During one evening lecture, Prabhupāda boldly spoke of giant eagles that fly from planet to planet, eat elephants, and hatch their eggs in outer space with the help of atmospheric friction. As he spoke, sitting tall and fully confident on the purple velvet *vyāsāsana,* his eyes blazed, challenging any doubters. “Any questions?” he asked at the end of his lecture. Silence.

One lone hand hesitatingly sprouted from the crowd of sixty-five devotees and guests.

**Raghunātha:** Śrīla Prabhupāda, in your lecture you mentioned, um, um, giant eagles who, um, fly from planet to planet.  
**Prabhupāda**: Yes.  
**Raghunātha:** Well, um, it is, um, a little hard to believe.  
**Prabhupāda**: *\[Sitting still taller, with his eyes almost defiant.*\] What do you know? You are still within the womb of your mother!

\* \* \*

The conditioned soul’s conception of reality is incomplete and imperfect. We will readily admit that no two persons perceive the world in exactly the same way, yet we nevertheless consider our own perceptions as reality.

Prabhupāda pointed out that Raghunātha’s illusory perception of reality, which was limited by Australian cultural biases, finite intelligence, faulty cognizance, and spiritual babyhood, prevented him from understanding the goings-on within a material universe, what to speak of in Goloka Vṛndāvana.

## An Open Darśana

Before my “you should not take *sannyāsa”* meeting with Prabhupāda, I had arranged through the temple authorities to privately meet Prabhupāda, but now that I had gone to see him on my own, I didn’t need that appointment. Devotees begged me to be allowed to come during my scheduled meeting. My private *darśana* became an open *darśana* and all the local devotees filed into Prabhupāda’s room.

The parquet floor in Prabhupāda’s room quickly became crowded with devotees, but still more entered. Prabhupāda sat on his plush, couch-like *āsana* as he watched his disciples enter. Madhudviṣa saw the devotees’ enthusiasm and remarked, “Prabhupāda, it seems that the devotees want to get more of your association.” Prabhupāda smiled affectionately. I am also wanting that.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda’s preaching mission required constant travel. Most of his disciples, therefore, lived in separation from Prabhupāda, although he remained the center of their lives. They rarely saw him more than a few days each year. Even during those few days, most devotees’ association with Prabhupāda took place in his morning or evening classes. Empowered by the inspiration they derived during those times, however, the devotees continued throughout the year trying to please him by their services.

Still, the devotees always wanted more of Prabhupāda’s association. Because Prabhupāda was attracted by their sincerity, he also relished their association.

## Why Open Darśanas Were Rare

One young woman, who was neither initiated nor married, stepped up to Prabhupāda. She smiled and took out some drawings her five-year-old son had scribbled. Prabhupāda looked at the first one and the woman explained, “This is Kṛṣṇa with His cowherd boyfriends.” Prabhupāda smiled lightly. “Very nice.” She then pulled out a second scribbled picture: “Kṛṣṇa holding Govardhana Hill.” The third, fourth, and fifth pictures followed in quick succession. Prabhupāda’s appreciations turned from words to nods as his smile dropped toward a frown. The audience of devotees began to fidget and their eyes lowered in embarrassment as the woman continued producing picture after picture.

\* \* \*

The devotees felt pain watching this woman take advantage of Prabhupāda’s patience and tolerance. They knew the value of each moment of his time.

## “Offer Me with Devotion”

The devotees in Melbourne had cultivated a flourishing *tulasī* garden in a round greenhouse near the back of the temple property. The *brahmacārī* “in charge” of the *tulasī* plants was an expert gardener, but he was eccentric. He also seemed to think his service was most glorious and that other services were inferior.

Madhudviṣa introduced him to Prabhupāda as several devotees sat at Prabhupāda’s feet.

**Madhudviṣa**: Our *tulasī* gardener has a question for you, Prabhupāda.  
**Prabhupāda**: He should ask.

The devotee gardener, in an affected accent, asked Prabhupāda how many *tulasī* leaves should be placed on Kṛṣṇa’s plate for the offering.

With his service as the center of the universe, his intonations and hand gestures revealed his vision of mountains of *tulasī* leaves on Kṛṣṇa’s plate that would dwarf the tiny helpings of rice, *dāl*, *capātīs*, and sweets.

We expected Prabhupāda to praise the gardener for his ability to grow luxuriant *tulasīs*, but Prabhupāda simply looked at him for several seconds and then said, “One.”

\* \* \*

In the Ninth Chapter of *Bhagavad-gītā*, Kṛṣṇa asks His devotees to offer Him a leaf, a fruit, a flower, and some water with love and devotion. Kṛṣṇa’s mention of a leaf (*patraṁ*) is in the singular. He is satisfied only by devotion, not the quantity or size of the offering.

Prabhupāda was reminding the devotee-gardener that devotion is the essence of our accomplishments in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

## A Chance Taken for a Gesture of Affection

The devotees accompanied Prabhupāda to the airport when he left Melbourne. Prabhupāda sat on the plastic chairs of the airport lounge, and tens of devotees sat on chairs and the carpeted floor near him.

Several devotees stepped forward to speak a few final words or offer a departure letter, apple, or flower. I watched everything from the back. I wasn’t in good consciousness or spirits. I felt distant.

The boarding call for Prabhupāda’s flight was announced. Prabhupāda stood up and the devotees offered their obeisances. A *kīrtana* started and the atmosphere filled with anticipation. From the crowd, I noticed that Prabhupāda’s hat sat on his head at an awkward, almost comical angle. A thought raced through my mind. “What if people think it funny and laugh at Prabhupāda?” I tried to decide whether or not I should straighten his hat.

The internal dialogue began: “Prabhupāda doesn’t care what others think. But it will be offensive for them to laugh. They should offer Prabhupāda all respect. How can I be the one who straightens it? Is it offensive to think others may look at Prabhupāda’s tilted hat as comical?”

Prabhupāda walked slowly toward the gangway, allowing his disciples a few more moments of association. I stepped directly into his path. “Allow me to straighten your hat, Prabhupāda.” He looked steadily at my face as my hands moved his hat from left to center on his freshly shaved head. I then quickly stepped back and Prabhupāda walked up the gangway to the sound of the *kīrtana.*

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda did not acknowledge my “straightening,” yet I immediately felt that I had acted properly. My analysis of the possibly unfavorable reactions of others may not have been accurate, but my motivation was simple—to protect Prabhupāda from an embarrassing situation. I had acted as his servant.

## A Servant Serves

Due to the heavy judgment of ISKCON of the 1970s on devotees living outside the temple, and due also to my own shortcomings, I found it difficult to associate with the devotees and do temple service in Australia. Yet serving Prabhupāda remained the only meaningful desire in my life. My first plan was to offer Prabhupāda $1,000. For weeks and weeks I tried to accumulate money for my offering, but money seemed to vanish as fast as I made it.

From the inception of my handbag business, I considered Prabhupāda its owner and myself its manager, so instead of saving to send Prabhupāda $1,000 in one lump sum, I began to send him checks each week.

My first offerings were only $25, but they gradually grew to $50, $100, and finally $250 a week. (Later, when Prabhupāda toured Australia during his final visit in 1976, I was able to fulfill my desire to give him $1,000.) Regardless of the size of my donation, each week’s focus was my trip to the post office and my purchase of a money order in the name of His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda. I would also write him a note expressing my affection and a prayer asking him to accept my insignificant offering.

\* \* \*

Devotees doing business tend to make the financial success of their business a prerequisite to giving donations. I find this mentality counterproductive in my own circumstances, and in the circumstances of many devotees with whom I have spoken.

I learned that a donation—regardless of its size—must be offered for Kṛṣṇa’s service each week. The size of the donation may vary according to the success of the business, but serving Kṛṣṇa has top priority, alongside paying for food, clothing, and shelter.

With every weekly check, I could think that at least some concrete service was completed, as opposed to hoping the business would one day be successful so I could one day serve.

By regularly offering donations to Prabhupāda and by thinking of the business as his, I was forced to work hard, depend on Kṛṣṇa, and consider my business as devotional service. Prabhupāda appreciated my mood, especially the regularity of my offerings.

## Letters from Prabhupāda

After a month or so, I started to receive letters of acknowledgment from Prabhupāda. One of Prabhupāda’s replies instructed me that a disciple must somehow or other always serve:

My dear Bhurijana dasa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated September 12, 1975 with enclosed check for $127.68. In this way you are rendering very nice service and the more you render service, the more you advance. This is the only way. Therefore the Vedic regulation is that whenever one goes to see some saintly person or to visit a temple, he must offer something. That will help him. It is enjoined that one should first of all dedicate his life, and if it is not possible to do that, that one should dedicate his money. If he has no money, then he should utilize his intelligence how to spread Krishna consciousness. Or if he has no intelligence, then he should utilize his words that, “Yes, they are doing nicely This is a nice movement. “ This is the process for advancement in Krishna consciousness.  
I hope this meets you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

Practically speaking, the essential quality of a devotee, or of someone sincerely aspiring to become a servant of a devotee, is his serving mood. Regardless of external circumstances, therefore, a devotee always—somehow or other—renders service.

Devotees at times find themselves without service. Without service—and Prabhupāda indicated that service has a broad scope—thinking of oneself as a devotee has no meaning.

## The “Best Useful” Service

My visits to the post office and the ensuing checks and letters to Prabhupāda continued week after week, month after month. Generally, Prabhupāda kindly acknowledged receipt of each letter and check with a letter of his own. In one letter to Prabhupāda, I had expressed that I was not able to serve him very well at the present, and I asked that he accept my donations until I could properly serve him. Prabhupāda’s reply encouraged me:

I am in due receipt of your letters dated August 22, 29th and September 5th with enclosed Dollars 50.00, 159.61, and 126.50. You say that someday you hope to be useful, but you are already useful—you are sending checks. This is the best useful. You are remembering me and chanting Hare Krishna. This is Krishna consciousness.

\* \* \*

My insignificant donations may not have fully deserved Prabhupāda’s generously terming them “the best useful,” nevertheless, Prabhupāda expertly fanned the spark of my desire to serve.

## The Responsibility of a Householder

One night I dreamed that I was walking with Prabhupāda on his morning walk. We were in Melbourne. Suddenly he stopped, turned to me, and asked me to buy a farm for the temple. The dream put me in anxiety, so I wrote to Prabhupāda and asked whether or not he considered it my responsibility to buy a farm. In his reply, Prabhupāda did not mention my dream:

My dear Bhurijana dasa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letters dated September 19 and October 17, 1975 with enclosed checks for $100 and $500 respectively. So this is very nice that your checks are getting bigger. You are one of my older experienced students and I am always praying for your advancement in Krishna consciousness. As the husband of your wife, you should see that she is trained up nicely in Krishna consciousness. You also have a child, so this is your responsibility to see that your family no longer again has to come back and take a material body in this world of birth and death. This is the responsibility of a father in Krishna consciousness.  
I hope this meets you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

And in a later letter:

Naturally your daughter is chanting the transcendental Name of the Lord. It must be so, because unless one is pious in previous life, one cannot take birth in a Vaishnava family. Give her good chance to become Krishna Conscious more and more, and offer my blessings to your good wife Jagatarini.  
I hope this meets you well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

A prime duty of a family man is to make his family Kṛṣṇa conscious. Mahārāja Ṛṣabhadeva describes this in the Fifth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam: pitā na sa syāt.* In Kali-yuga, this is easier said than done. Regardless of the outcome, however, we should do our duty with full conviction in the importance of helping our family members become Kṛṣṇa conscious.

## Māyāpur, 1976

Māyāpur, 1976

## Entering Beautiful Māyāpur-dhāma

I arrived in Māyāpur-dhāma just before the Gaura-Pūrṇimā festival in 1976. Entering the holy *dhāma* by boating across the Ganges River soothed my mind and eyes. Māyāpur’s rarefied spiritual atmosphere was apparent and its beauty—the broad rushing Ganges River and the vast lush, green fields—was stunning. Since my last visit, the devotees had begun constructing a long, double-storied building “as big as two trains,” as Prabhupāda described it. I stayed in my one-man tent behind the temple.

I was inspired to visit Māyāpur and hoped to gain spiritual strength in Prabhupāda’s association. I planned to chant sixty-four rounds a day, so I brought a tent, thinking that it would help me to spend more time alone chanting *japa* and less time socializing. I also hoped to avoid the festival fees.

## Hearing as Never Before

In the mornings, Prabhupāda lectured on the teachings of Prahlāda Mahārāja. My life in Māyāpur centered on hearing his classes. At first I tried taping the lectures, but when my tape recorder failed, I took notes. I tried to write down each word.

Between my note-taking and chanting of sixty-four rounds a day, my hearing and concentration became acutely focused. Prabhupāda’s words deeply penetrated my consciousness. I perceived that they came directly from the absolute platform. By Lord Caitanya’s mercy, I was able to hear Prabhupāda better than I had ever heard him before.

## Becoming a Servant, Excerpts from Prabhupāda’s Lectures

In his lectures, Prabhupāda often spoke of the need to give up the mood of being master and to accept the mood of a servant.

**Prabhupāda**: Disease is disease. Actually that is the fact. You say that, “We are suffering from malaria. It is better than to suffer from syphilis.” No. Disease is disease. Similarly, either Brahmā or the ant, the disease is how to become master. This is the disease. Therefore, Kṛṣṇa comes to cure this disease. He says plainly, “Rascal, you are not master, you are servant. Surrender unto Me.” This is the cure of the disease. If one agrees, “No more trying for becoming master,” that is the cure of disease. Therefore Prahlāda Mahārāja says *nija bhṛtya-pārśvam:* “Engage me as the servant of Your servant.” So this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement means we have to give up this nonsense idea of becoming master. This is Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We have to learn how to become servants. Not only servant, but the servant of the servant. That is the cure.

\* \* \*

Spiritual life means to change our mood and activities from enjoyer to servant. Maintaining the mood of master in spiritual life is as contradictory as lighting a fire under water. Engaging our “master” tendency in Kṛṣṇa’s service may work for a limited amount of time, but it still skirts the essence of Kṛṣṇa consciousness—becoming a servant of God. We have to give up the idea that we are the master.

**Prabhupāda**: Prahlada Mahdraja said, “If You kindly want to give me some benediction, make me the servant of Your servant. “ This is an actual benediction. One who has learned to become the servant of Kṛṣṇa’s servant is perfect. Therefore Caitanya Mahāprabhu says *tṛnād api sunīcena, taror api sahiṣṇunā.* A servant has to tolerate. Sometimes a master orders so many things, so the servant becomes disturbed. But still he has to execute and tolerate. That is perfection.

\* \* \*

It is easier to agree to serve God than it is to serve God’s servant. Serving the servant of God requires humility. We are truly fortunate if Kṛṣṇa sends us His representative and allows us to recognize and serve him.

**Prabhupāda**: When Kṛṣṇa with His sixteen thousand wives went to Hastināpura, Draupadī was inquiring from each and every wife of Kṛṣṇa. Not actually all of them, but at least the principal queens. So every one of them was describing their marriage ceremony. Rukmiṇī explained that “My father wanted to hand me over to Kṛṣṇa, but my elder brother did not agree. He wanted to get me married with Śiśpāla. So I did not like this idea. I wrote Kṛṣṇa a private letter, that ‘I have dedicated my life to You, but this is the situation. Please come and kidnap me.’ So in this way Kṛṣṇa kidnapped me and made me His maidservant.”

Everyone of them were king’s daughter. They were not ordinary person’s daughters. But they wanted to become maidservant of Kṛṣṇa. This is the idea, to become servant and to become maidservant. Every woman should try to become maidservant of her husband, and every man should try to become a hundred times the servant of Kṛṣṇa. This is Indian civilization. Not that “Husband and wife, we have equal rights.” That, in Europe, America, movement is going on, “equal rights.” That is not Vedic civilization. Vedic civilization means that the husband should be a sincere servant of Kṛṣṇa and the wife should be a sincere maidservant of the husband.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda pointed out that Western women would rather act as equals to their husbands than as assistants. He equated that mood with our dissatisfaction with the servant role and our impossible desire to become master. Although the wife should think of herself as a servant, the husband should not think of himself as her master. Kṛṣṇa is the master, and both husband and wife should peacefully adopt their appropriate roles as servants.

**Prabhupāda**: There is no such thing to become master. it is useless. You cannot become master. *Jīvera ‘svarūpa’ haya—kṛṣṇera ‘nitya-dāsa’.* Either man or woman, everyone is servant of Kṛṣṇa. We have to be trained up in that platform to become the best servant. Not only servants directly, but servants of the servant.  
This is called *paramparā* servant. My spiritual master is the servant of his spiritual master, and I am also servant of my spiritual master. Similarly, we think, “servant of the servant.” This is material disease. *Kṛṣṇa bhuliya jiva bhoga vancha kare, pasate māyā tare japatiya dhare.* As soon as we become puffed up—“Now I shall become master. I shall be simply giving order. I shall not follow anyone.” That is *māyā. So* that disease is going on beginning from Brahmā down to the ant.  
Our institution is for that purpose. If somebody comes here thinking this is a free hotel and free sleeping accommodation, then his coming to this association is useless. He must learn how to serve. If we take it that “Here is an institution where we can have free hotel, free living, and free sense gratification,” then the whole institution will be spoiled. Be careful. All the GBCs should be careful that this mentality may not increase. Everyone should be very eager to serve and to learn how to serve. Then life will be successful.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda cautioned the devotees to beware of the disease of getting a position in ISKCON and then imagining we have become the master instead of the servant. it is far better to always remain the servant of the servant of the *guru-paramparā*. A devotee must maintain this consciousness regardless of his external position.

**Prabhupāda**: Everywhere you see it. People work day and night, but the purpose is how to become master. They cannot become master, but their ambition is how to become master. How to become the richest man like such-and-such big man. This is called the struggle for existence. Everyone is trying to become the master. Nobody is trying to become a servant. Ask anyone, “Why you are working so hard?” They will answer, “I shall get so much money, I shall become very wealthy, I shall have so many servants, so many workers, and I shall have control over them.”  
I am trying to become master instead of becoming a servant of Kṛṣṇa, but I have simply become the servant of my senses. So my position as servant is there. Is it not? I cannot change it. Just like a woman. If a woman dresses like a man, does it mean that she has become a man? No. A woman is still a woman. A man is a man. Simply by changing dress does not change the fact. Similarly, our constitutional position is to serve. And to serve whom? Kṛṣṇa. That is our constitutional position. But falsely we are trying to become master. Therefore, instead of becoming master, I have become the servant of my senses. This is our position.  
The tongue is asking, “Why don’t you come to this restaurant.” “No, no, my belly is already filled up.” “No, you must come.” \[Laughter.\] You’ll see. A man is coming from home after sumptuously eating. As soon as he comes to the street, immediately he enters a restaurant and drinks a cup of tea and a few biscuits. Why? What is the necessity? You are already filled up in your belly, and still, again immediately you are eating. “No, it is very palatable.” So you are servant of your tongue. “Because it is palatable. Although my belly is already filled up, I must satisfy my tongue.” So am I master? I’m not master; I’m the servant of my tongue.  
You cannot understand by erudite scholarship what is Kṛṣṇa. It is not possible. Then Kṛṣṇa would have instructed *Bhagavad-gītā* to a great Vedāntist. No. Kṛṣṇa chose Arjuna to understand. Who was Arjuna? Arjuna was a *gṛhastha,* a *kṣatriya,* a soldier. A soldier is not expected to become a Vedāntist. Arjuna was not very qualified in that way to understand *Bhagavad-gītā.* But Kṛṣṇa said, “Yes, I will speak to you this *Bhagavad-gītā.* The *paramparā* is lost, so I shall again speak the old thing to you.” “Why to me?” *Bhakto ’si priyo ’si me.* “Because you are My *bhakta.* You are My *priya*.” So by scholarship one cannot understand *Bhagavad-gītā.* That is not possible. One must become a servant of the servant of Kṛṣṇa.

\* \* \*

We remain servants regardless of our masquerades. Even if we are able to avoid serving others, we fall to serving our own senses. No one succeeds at becoming the master.

## Asking to Meet Prabhupāda

After being in Māyāpur for a few days, I decided to visit Prabhupāda. I saw Prabhupāda’s secretary, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa, near Prabhupāda’s second floor room and asked him for an appointment. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa looked at me skeptically. We didn’t know each other and he was unaware of my service to Prabhupāda in Hong Kong. He said, “Look, five hundred devotees are at this festival and every one of them wants to meet with Prabhupāda.

I became nervous, but I avoided the temptation to retreat.

“Maybe five hundred devotees want to meet Prabhupāda, but I’m the only one asking.” Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa acknowledged my point and left me to inform Prabhupāda of my request. He returned a minute later and said I could see Prabhupāda the following morning.

My meeting with Prabhupāda was short. I paid my obeisances and offered Prabhupāda two traveler’s checks of $100 each and several bags of cashews, almonds, and raisins. He expressed his appreciation and well-wishes, and I left, feeling satisfied at having offered a little service.

## The Dependence of the Disciple on His Guru

Prabhupāda continued to give the morning *Bhāgavatam* lectures and I continued to listen attentively. Prabhupāda spoke about the dependence of the disciple upon his *guru*.

**Prabhupāda**: Why the spiritual master’s position is so great? Because he is very, very confidential servant of Kṛṣṇa. So this is the process. A*dau guru-pādāśrayam*. The first beginning of Vaiṣṇava life is to accept a *guru*, a spiritual master. Then other things. Because who will teach you? So we have to approach a pure Vaiṣṇava as a spiritual master and follow his instruction.

And what is his instruction? His instruction is as he was instructed by his spiritual master. The pure Vaiṣṇava does not invent anything new. *Yāre dekha, tare kaha ‘kṛṣṇa’-upadeśa.* Caitanya Mahāprabhu says that “You all, every one of you be come *guru.* Everyone. *Āmāra ajñāya guru hañā tāra’ ei deśa.* Wherever you are living, it doesn’t require that you have to travel all over the world. If you have no such capacity it doesn’t matter. You remain wherever you are, but you become a *guru.* “How can I become a *guru?” Yāre dekha, tare kaha ‘kṛṣṇa’-upadeśa.* Very simple thing. You instruct only what is instructed by Kṛṣṇa. That’s all.

\* \* \*

Becoming a *guru* requires that we be a pure servant of our own *guru.*

**Prabhupāda**: Because we are bewildered, we are misdirected, so we have to follow *guru.* “How I can find out the real *guru* by whose words I’ll be nicely directed and achieve?” That is not very difficult. You read *Bhagavad-gītā*. You’ll understand. Just like Arjuna. When he was perplexed, he accepted Kṛṣṇa—guru. He said, *śiṣyas te ’ham:* “I now become Your disciple. We were talking as friends and our argument will not end, but I now become Your disciple.”

*Śiṣyas te ’ham.* As soon as one becomes a *śiṣyas … Śiṣya* means under the order, regulation. A person cannot disobey the order of *guru*. Then he is *śiṣya.* if he argues, he’s not *śiṣya.* Therefore Arjuna says, *śiṣyas te ’ham:* “I surrender. I voluntarily surrender to become your disciple. Now I shall not argue.” That is called *śiṣya.* If you argue, then you are not a *śiṣya*. *Guru-mukha-padma-vākya, cittete koriyā aikya, ār nā koriho mane āśā.* This is *tapasya*, that “I shall not act anything which is not ordered by my *guru*.” We will then be nicely guided and our existence will be purified. And as soon as our existence is purified, we will realize what is God, what is our relationship with Him, and what are our activities.

\* \* \*

It is rare, especially in Kali-yuga, that two people fully agree on anything. If we wish to advance in spiritual life, we must suspend our tendency to disagree and surrender to a guru. Only then can we become a servant and purify our existence.

## The Mercy of More Association

Prabhupāda again allowed me his association late one morning during his massage. Seeing Prabhupāda’s golden skin glistening with mustard oil as he sat on the veranda made me feel like an intimate disciple. His servant worked the oil into Prabhupāda’s body with strong movements as Prabhupāda sometimes remained silent and at other times spoke.

Nitāi, who had previously traveled with Prabhupāda as his Sanskrit editor, sat with me in front of Prabhupāda. Nitāi had recently gone to Vṛndāvana to organize the academics for the Bhaktivedanta Swami Gurukula and was now telling Prabhupāda of his plans.

Although I was not aware of the background, Prabhupāda clearly wasn’t satisfied with Nitāi’s proposal. He sat in the hot, mid-morning sun, his eyes closed, as his servant massaged his slender legs. “What is the use of making Sanskrit scholars? So many Sanskrit *paṇḍitas* are loitering on the streets with no engagement. No. We don’t want our students to become Sanskrit scholars. We simply want them to become attached to the Vedic culture.”

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda had already reviewed Nitāi’s curriculum plans and was now analyzing them as too academic. He did not approve. Prabhupāda’s instructions on *gurukula* clearly indicate that his concern was that the students become devotees. Academic education is essential, but secondary to training in devotion. Prabhupāda wanted the children to have association with devotee adults and to follow the basic program for hearing and chanting.

## Buying a Basket at Prabhupāda’s Price

Prabhupāda began his morning walk surrounded by senior disciples. They walked towards the Jalāṅgī River on an embankment at the edge of a rice field, and I joined the group, walking in the field to the side of the path. Prabhupāda headed left when he reached the dirt road leading to Tarunpur. Soon, a farmer carrying a wicker basket of vegetables on his head passed and Prabhupāda stopped him. The farmer eased the basket from his head and allowed Prabhupāda to inspect the fresh *bindī* (okra). Prabhupāda and the farmer haggled back and forth in Bengali for a few minutes, leaving Prabhupāda smiling, for he had purchased the entire basket for twenty rupees.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda didn’t like to see his disciples cheated by the local businessmen. He once remarked that whomever allowed himself to be cheated was as guilty as the cheater. Prabhupāda was expert in all dealings and he expected us to follow his example.

I watched Prabhupāda enjoy his dealing with the farmer and then watched him direct the farmer to deposit the *bindī* at the ISKCON kitchen. Prabhupāda indicated that the farmer should be paid, and I seized the opportunity to serve Prabhupāda by handing the farmer twenty rupees.

## Cultivating Goodness

I continued living in my tent, chanting sixty-four rounds a day, and listening attentively to Prabhupāda’s morning classes. Prabhupāda spoke on several occasions from a practical viewpoint about the necessity of devotees cultivating goodness and resisting the domination of the lower modes of passion and ignorance.

**Prabhupāda**: Just like I was rebuking you about the condition in that toilet. This is so nasty, tamo-guṇa. If I have no response to such *tamo-guṇa* place, that means I am also of that quality. Like fire and fire, there is no reaction. But in fire and water there is reaction. Similarly, sattva-guṇa, sattva-guṇa, there is no reaction. *Tamo-guṇa, tamo-guṇa,* there is no reaction. In English it is called incompatible. When different qualities mix, there is reaction, chemical reaction. Acid and alkaline, if you mix, there will be immediately effervescence.  
If one is *sattva-guṇa*, then he cannot tolerate. Immediately obnoxious: “Oh, such a nasty condition.” So I am very sorry there was no reaction in such a nasty toilet room. And you are getting sacred thread, which is for one with the quality of a *brāhmaṇa—sattva-guṇa.* It is very regrettable. Nobody reacted. So this is the position. We have to curb down *rajo-guṇa* and *tamo-guṇa*. Otherwise, there is no improvement.  
If a person in Kṛṣṇa consciousness is found to have no reaction to *rajo-guṇa*, *tamo-guṇa*, then he is not improving. It is simply showbottle. So showbottle will not help. But if one actually develops Kṛṣṇa consciousness, then it is to be understood that he has surpassed *sattva-guṇa,* the brahminical qualification. Why do we offer sacred thread to a person who is coming from very, very low family? Because it is to be understood that by chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra,* by following the regulative principles, he has already come to the platform of *sattva-guṇa.* But if it is a false thing, there is no need of second initiation.  
Our process is “Don’t do this and do this: chant Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra* sixteen rounds, and don’t do this—no illicit sex, no meat-eating.” That means he’s becoming purified from the rotten condition of *rajo-guṇa* and *tamo-guṇa.* But if he does not become purified, then there should be no second initiation. This should be the rule.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda expected that the brahminically initiated should act to the brahminical standard of *sattva-guṇa.* He herein indicates that brahminical initiation should *only* be offered to those qualified by brahminical purity.

**Prabhupāda**: This material world has three qualities, so there is always a struggle. If you want to remain in the *sattva-guṇa,* then the *rajas-tamo guṇa* will give you trouble. *Tadā rajas-tamo-bhāvāḥ kāma-lobhādayaś ca ye. So* we are trying to introduce this *sattva-guṇa* and above *sattva-guṇa, śuddha-sattva.*  
Sometimes we experience that one person is elevated to the brahminical principle, *sannyāsī* principle, but all of a sudden he falls down. He becomes a demon. We have got experience. He becomes demon. This material world is so contaminated that even if you are on the *sattva-guṇa* platform, there is a chance of being contaminated by the *rajas-tamo guṇa.* The struggle is there. Therefore we have to become very, very careful.  
But Kṛṣṇa will help. If you want to remain in *sattva-guṇa,* in purity, then Kṛṣṇa will help. just like here, as soon as Brahmā was disturbed by the demons full of *rajo-guṇa* and *tamo-guṇa,* immediately the Lord came in His Hayagrīva *mūrti* incarnation.  
If you remain a pure devotee, always surrendered to Kṛṣṇa, know very well that Kṛṣṇa will give you protection in any calamity. Don’t be worried. Simply we must have faith. That is surrender. Surrender means *avasya rakhibe kṛṣṇa viśāsya pālanam*. You must be faithful that “I am engaged in Kṛṣṇa’s service. I may go to hell or heaven. It doesn’t matter. I am going to serve Him. It is sure that Kṛṣṇa will give me protection.” So there should be no hesitation. If somebody is ordered, “Go to hell and preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness,” he should remain faithful to Kṛṣṇa and Kṛṣṇa will give all protection. That is the principle.

\* \* \*

Devotees who take risks on their *guru’s* behalf quickly learn of Kṛṣṇa’s personal protection. Prabhupāda encouraged the devotees to be bold and faithful in their preaching services on behalf of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

**Prabhupāda**: All these books which we are distributing and publishing, what are they? They are Kṛṣṇa and His activities. That’s all. So if you simply read our books very carefully, then immediately this disease of heart palpitation, anxiety, will cease because it is the dirty things, this *tamo-guṇa* and *rajo-guṇa*, that are disturbing us.  
We are covered by the *tamo-guṇa* and *rajo-guṇa. Rajas-tamaḥ* means greediness and lusty desires. This is *rajas-tamaḥ*. So if we actually want to become free from anxieties, then first of all we have to come to the platform of goodness.  
There are three platforms: *tamo-guṇa, rajo-guṇa,* and *sattva-guṇa*. So immediately we cannot come to the pure *sattva-guṇa śuddha-sattva,* the *vasudeva* platform. Just as Vasudeva is the father of Kṛṣṇa, so there is *vasudeva* platform also. Unless one comes to the *vasudeva* platform, there is no question of knowing Kṛṣṇa because only Vasudeva can beget Kṛṣṇa, or Vāsudeva. So we have to come to the platform of *vasudeva, śuddha-sattva.* But it is not immediately possible. Therefore, gradually, from the platform of *tamo-guṇa* and *rajo-guṇa,* we have to come to the *sattva-guṇa.*  
In other words, we have to acquire the qualities of a *brāhmaṇa.* Therefore this initiation is to bring one to the platform of *brāhmaṇa. Cātur-varṇyaṁ mayā sṛṣṭaṁ guṇa-karma-vibhāgaśaḥ.* This is necessary. From this material platform to come to the spiritual platform one has to become a qualified *brāhmaṇa.* We first of all accept anyone who is prepared to chant and give him *hari-nāma.* What is the idea? The idea is that he’ll gradually become *brāhmaṇa.*  
This Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is trying to raise the standard of the human society to become *brāhmaṇas* from their position of miserliness, *kṛpanā.* This is our attempt. It may be a tiny, humble attempt, but our aim is how these dogs and cats will become *brāhmaṇa.* This is our aim. We do not want to keep them in the position of cats and dogs and pigs. This is our attempt. So we may be successful or not successful, but our endeavor is very, very great.

\* \* \*

Lord Rāmacandra engaged monkeys in His service. Similarly, Prabhupāda engaged his disciples who often came from backgrounds of complete *rajo-guṇa* and *tamo-guṇa.* His purpose was to exalt their consciousness to *sattva-guṇa* and finally to *bhakti.* Many disciples, even trusted servants and *sannyāsīs,* fell due to the difficult struggle with past conditioning. Prabhupāda had to display incredible affection, patience, tolerance, and determination as he pushed ISKCON forward. In the above lecture, Prabhupāda said that our materialistic culture is comprised of humans acting like cats, dogs, and pigs. Although his words pinched my false ego and attacked the pride of my American birth, I could not but agree with him.

## Two Letters from Prabhupāda

One afternoon, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa casually mentioned that Prabhupāda had written me a letter which he had decided not to send. Naturally, I wanted to see that letter. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa opened his file and pulled out not one, but two recent letters to me from Prabhupāda. One was not to be sent; the other had been sent to my address in Australia, but I had not yet received it. I first read the letter Prabhupāda had written but not posted:

Letter one, service brings service

My dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I have recently thought of a scheme to increase funds in our BBT account. I would like to send Indian goods to you for sales to the devotees and others. In the beginning I can have cloth sent, especially sarees. So many others are doing it successfully, we can also be successful. I will have the papers sent through Lloyd’s bank as I have an account with them in London. So you can find out the Lloyd’s branch there. You can deposit all monies received from sales of the sarees in the Lloyd’s Bank. You may also keep a percentage of the sales for yourself. Please advise me if you are able to execute.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\* \* \*

The project to produce and export cloth never manifested, but I was happy that Prabhupāda had remembered me. I was also proud that Prabhupāda was indirectly acknowledging my service of regularly sending him donations and that he had enough confidence in my business acumen to ask me to serve him in this way.

Letter two, love means service

Devotional service is not mechanical. Prabhupāda continuously and patiently instructed and inspired me to advance further in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I felt the need to reciprocate with him through service. In the following letter, Prabhupāda confirmed my understanding of our relationship as correct:

My dear Bhurijana,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you for your letter dated 16th January, 1976 along with the enclosed donation. Unless there is loving feeling, how is it possible for you to always make offerings to me? The spiritual master is always instructing his disciples and they in turn are always trying to serve their spiritual master. It is a reciprocal relationship of love.

\* \* \*

This letter struck me deeply because Prabhupāda acknowledged my affection for him and my gratitude for all he was giving me. Without developing affection based on our appreciation of our guru’s mercy toward us, our service to guru and Kṛṣṇa will neither deepen, nor expand, nor become *apratihatā,* “uninterrupted.” Prabhupāda’s words melted my heart.

## A Darśana

Prabhupāda held open *darśanas* every evening and his room filled quickly. One devotee showed Prabhupāda photographs of Nitāi-Gaurasundara from ISKCON Baltimore. Prabhupāda examined the photo with appreciation and expressed his desire to personally visit Baltimore and view these Deities.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda later fulfilled his desire when one morning, he “took his walk in a car” and drove from ISKCON Washington, D.C. to Baltimore during his final tour of the United States in 1976.

A female devotee informed Prabhupāda about her preaching attempts in Cleveland, Ohio. Prabhupāda listened. Although it sounded like her preaching was independent of the ISKCON center, he heard of her successes and encouraged her.

\* \* \*

This *mātājī* was older than most of Prabhupāda’s disciples and didn’t take association from other devotees or from Prabhupāda’s GBC representative for Cleveland. It also appeared that her philosophical understanding was not exactly correct. Still, Prabhupāda genuinely appreciated her independent attempt to encourage others to chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* and to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness in her home city.

Another *mātājī,* Candravāli, who was one of Prabhupāda’s senior disciples and who had pioneered ISKCON’s preaching in Mexico with her husband, voiced a complaint.

Candravāli: Prabhupāda, we hear that in our philosophy the men should treat the women as mothers. But actually, the men, especially the *sannyāsīs,* don’t treat the women as mothers. Instead they treat them as *māyā*. Their attitude, not the women, seems more like *māyā*! It doesn’t seem proper.

Prabhupāda heard her complaint carefully. He smiled softly and began to reply. His words dropped from his mouth as if they were sweet ripe fruits falling from a tree.

**Prabhupāda**: Yes, the men do not treat the women as mothers. Nor do the women act like mothers. Neither do they dress as mothers.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda answered this delicate question with directness, acknowledging the “butter and fire” nature in the dealings between men and women. Vedic culture compares men to butter and women to fire, not vice versa. In Kali-yuga, women seem determined to break from their traditional roles in social dealing. They seem to be thinking, “If the butter-like men are disturbed, then that’s their problem, not ours. Why should we be penalized and our position restricted or minimized?” Prabhupāda, however, was not inclined to compromise the Vedic standard.

It is true that our bodily identity as male or female is illusory, but we still have to deal practically with the temporary nature of that illusion. ISKCON’s men and women, therefore, need to conscientiously adopt the activities, restrictions, and responsibilities of their temporary, bodily roles and become sympathetic, not antagonistic, to the position and needs of others.

## Developing Love for Rādhā-Mādhava

As Gaura-Pūrṇimā approached, Prabhupāda continued to lecture every morning and pointed us toward developing loving service to Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava.

**Prabhupāda**: Without having a loving connection with Kṛṣṇa, it is not possible to see Him. You may practice this *haṭha-yoga* or gymnastics *yoga* for many, many births, but you cannot see Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa can be seen when you smear love ointment in your eyes. *Premāñjana-cchurita-bhakti-vilocanena.* And that is possible through *bhakti.* Therefore, why not practice *bhakti-yoga* from the beginning if you want to see Kṛṣṇa? In our *bhakti-yoga* we can teach this art of seeing Kṛṣṇa within the core of the heart in one minute. It is so simple.  
You are seeing Kṛṣṇa here \[in the form of the Deity\]. Let this make an impression and try to keep that impression within your heart always. Then you become a first-class *yogī.* Why so much gymnastics and pressing the nose? No. Take directly. If you are engaged twenty-four hours in the service of the Deity, you cannot see anything except the Deity. This *bhakti-yoga* practice is so simple.  
Therefore it is the duty of *guru* to engage the neophyte devotee always in Deity worship.

śrī-vigrahārādhana-nitya-nānā-  
śṛṅgāra-tan-mandira-mārjanādau  
yuktasya bhaktāṁś ca niyuñjato ’pi  
vande guroḥ śrī-caraṇāravindam

It is the duty of the *guru* to engage his disciples in Deity worship, in cleansing the temple, in dressing the Deity, in decorating the Deity, and in so many engagements.  
So everyone should be engaged. If one is not competent to dress the Deity, he can simply wash the temple and cleanse it. That will also help. There is no difference between one who is cleansing the floor of the temple and one who is engaged directly in the Deity worship. … There is no difference. They are all the same. Don’t think, “I am engaged in cleansing the floor of the temple and he is engaged in decorating the temple. I should be envious.” No, there is no question of one duty being better. Any kind of duty you are engaged … *Sva-karmaṇā tam abhyarcya …* if you cannot do something which is hard for you, you can do anything for Kṛṣṇa under the direction of the spiritual master. You’ll get perfection. It is not that “I have to do this. I have to do that.” No. Kṛṣṇa’s service is manifold. If one is unable to take one kind of service, the spiritual master can give him another kind of service. Both services are accepted by Kṛṣṇa.

\* \* \*

The essence of our devotional service is our devotion and the external results of the service are secondary. It is devotion that pleases Kṛṣṇa when one devotee shelters another; it is devotion that brings Kṛṣṇa happiness when we present Him a donation; and it is devotion that elicits Kṛṣṇa’s appetite when we offer Him food.

## Humility

**Prabhupāda**: If you want to chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra,* then you have to take to the principle of *tṛnād api sunīcena.* You have to become humbler than the grass. Grass is lying on the street. Everyone is trampling down the grass. There is no protest. *Taror api sahiṣṇunā.* And more tolerant than the tree. The tree is giving us so much help. It is giving us fruit, flower, and leaves. And when there is scorching heat, it also gives shelter, and you may sit down underneath. So beneficial, yet we still cut. As soon as I like, I cut it down. But there is no protest. The tree does not say, “I have given you so much help and you are cutting me?” No. The tree is tolerant. Therefore Caitanya Mahāprabhu has selected the tree: *taror api sahiṣṇunā.* And *amāninā mānadena.* For oneself one should not expect any respectful position, but the devotee should offer all respects to everyone. *Āmāninā mānadena kīrtanīya sadā hari.* If we acquire these qualifications, then we can chant Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* without any disturbance. These are the qualifications.

\* \* \*

Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu listed these qualities as the prerequisite for constant chanting of the holy name. Humility and tolerance are present only in one cleansed of all sinful and pious reactions. Being fixed in these qualities is real fortune. Reaching this platform requires not only our earnest endeavor, but also Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, especially in the form of association with His pure devotees.

## Fixed-up Thinking of Kṛṣṇa

**Prabhupāda**: Prahlāda Mahārāja says that the perfection of life is *tava kathāsu vaikuṇṭha-nātha:* one should be interested in *kṛṣṇa-kathā*. We are interested in so many *kathās.* We purchase a newspaper in the morning. Such a big volume, and so many *kathds* are there. And we read many material dramas and novels and fictitious stories and killing stories. We are very much attached to these.
