But big moralist, they cannot understand. They’ll see, “Why the father has spoken lie to his son. He’s not a good father.” They’ll mistake. Father is always friend. Father cannot be enemy. But for the benefit of the rascal child, sometimes he has to say like that, that “If you take the medicine, I’ll give you cake.” Those who are mundane moralist, they cannot understand because they are on the mundane platform.  
Another example is Yudhiṣṭhira Mahārāja. He was asked by Kṛṣṇa, “You speak a lie to Droṇācārya, 'Your son is dead.’” Yudhiṣṭhira Mahārāja refused. For this he had to see hell. He was more a moralist than Kṛṣṇa. For this moral activity he had to visit hell.

\* \* \*

I had previously read a different version of why Yudhiṣṭhira had to see hell in an English translation of the *Mahābhārata.* That translation said that Yudhiṣṭhira had to see hell because he lied on Kṛṣṇa’s order. Obviously, that did not concur with the Kṛṣṇa conscious conclusion. I had been meaning to ask Prabhupāda for the correct understanding, and Kṛṣṇa arranged for Prabhupāda to answer my question without my having to ask it.

**Prabhupāda:** This philosophy cannot be understood by neophyte devotees. Our purpose is to push so much this sales of books, because through it our missionary activities will be very widely known. That we want. Somehow or other let him purchase a book. That is our mission.  
There is no question of transgressing moral principles. Arjuna was arguing with Kṛṣṇa that killing my relatives is horrible, abominable. Better I beg and maintain myself than I kill my relatives and become a king. Did he not say? That is an ordinary man’s argument. But as Yudhiṣṭhira Mahārāja refused to speak lie, Arjuna wanted to refuse to fight. But Arjuna, in spite of his conviction that it is sinful to kill my relatives, he still agreed to kill. Why?  
**Bhūrijana:** That is the supreme morality, following Kṛṣṇa’s desire.  
**Prabhupāda:** What Kṛṣṇa desires is above all this material. But it should not be manufactured. The thing is that our spiritual master wants to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness and he has repeatedly stressed distribution of books. We shall distribute books by any means. That is good. And if you become moralist that “Oh, they’re taking this means, that means for selling of book. I’m a big moralist. I’m bigger than him.”  
**Bhūrijana:** I don’t think there is so much enmity because of the books themselves.  
**Prabhupāda:** No, I do not know what they are doing, but I have information that they’re not selling books.  
**Bhūrijana:** Some I think. I think some.  
**Prabhupāda:** But not …  
**Bhūrijana:** Comparable amount.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Others who were transgressing the moral principle, they were selling more books. And those who are big moralists, they could not.  
**Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa:** They’re also distributing one pamphlet printed by Siddha-svarūpānanda. Then if they like, they can buy one of Prabhupāda’s books. Prabhupāda’s book comes second.  
**Prabhupāda:** Sometimes I’ve heard so many things.  
**Bhūrijana:** But they give those away for free. I mean just in the sense that someone maybe doesn’t want to purchase anything so they give him something for free about Kṛṣṇa.  
**Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa:** First they do that.  
**Bhūrijana:** Only because it’s free as far as I know.  
**Prabhupāda:** If they can get knowledge for free, then why they should purchase? If you give them the opportunity that we take knowledge free, then why they will purchase?

\* \* \*

At another time, Prabhupāda told me he didn’t object to free book distribution. Here he did not agree that distributing a free introductory book was an effective way to get people to buy his books.

**Prabhupāda:** They should follow what their spiritual master says: book-selling. There is no question of moral and immoral. They must sell. Just like in fighting. Where there is fight, the soldiers, to gain victory, there is no question of moral and immoral.  
**Bhūrijana:** Śrīla Prabhupāda, but I think the thing I understand least is that if they are not doing what you want … I mean … I’m sitting before you now and you’re telling me and I’m listening, and you are my spiritual master. I must accept.

\* \* \*

I began to ask Prabhupāda why, since he was their spiritual master, he didn’t simply and definitively order them to do what he wanted. I couldn’t get my question out. I did not know about their conversation the day before, but as I spoke, I realized that as Siddha-svarūpa’s spiritual master, Prabhupāda was instructing him in the best possible way, even if I could not understand it.

**Prabhupāda:** We want that book-selling must be increased as much as possible. This we want. The same thing. Let the child take medicine. Never mind the father is speaking lies. … Because as soon as he takes the medicine, he’ll be benefited. End justifies the means. End is that everyone should have a Kṛṣṇa literature. Doesn’t matter what is the means. Because he has taken one Kṛṣṇa literature, that justifies everything. This is the principle.  
**Bhūrijana:** How about just collecting without giving any literature? Just collecting. Three months ago I saw it in Australia, with a lying line too. Not telling the truth. And that wasn’t very long ago.  
**Hari-śauri:** Sometimes the devotees are just distributing incense.  
**Bhūrijana:** Nothing.  
**Hari-śauri:** They collect and distribute incense. Sometimes they don’t distribute books.  
**Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa:** Maybe they have to pay off a loan on the temple. All the devotees are well aware that Prabhupāda’s books have first priority. Everyone knows it.  
**Hari-śauri:** Prabhupāda’s point is that if you see fault with the method of implementing the spiritual master’s instructions, you’ll fail to follow the instructions.  
**Prabhupāda:** No, the point is not that they have not done any wrong. Don’t think like that. But my point is that my instruction is to sell books.

\* \* \*

This mood had been a stumbling block for me—that I had to neglect my common sense and intelligence and accept what I perceived as a faulty following of Prabhupāda’s order as faultless. That type of acceptance was difficult, perhaps even impossible, for me.

But Prabhupāda’s answer satisfied me completely. “No, no, the point is *not* that they have *not* done any wrong. Don’t think like that.” His hair-splitting answer allowed me to accept the correctness of what the devotees did do without being forced to accept as correct what I understood as inappropriate.

Upon hearing Prabhupāda’s explanation, my mind became instantly illuminated and I understood.

**Bhūrijana:** I understand. In other words, if you think they’re doing it wrong, you do it better. If you see others are doing it wrong, make sure you do it, but do it without the wrong thing. Don’t stop doing it.  
**Prabhupāda:** Yes. Besides that, if you do not take to Deity worship, then you shall remain unclean. That’s a fact.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda was satisfied that I had properly understood, and he immediately left the book distribution point to cite a second “fault” in the devotional stance adopted by the outside devotees: neglecting the temple means neglecting Deity worship, which is meant to keep us internally and externally pure.

By the end of this conversation, I felt a more honest and deepened relationship with Prabhupāda. I had lost my fear, and my affection, devotion, and surrender to Prabhupāda as father and wise master had increased. Prabhupāda’s calling me into his association had removed both misconceptions and ignorance about his actual desire. He had corrected me, but in an exacting way: he removed that which he judged inappropriate yet left that which others had judged inappropriate. He made clear a principle and offered me a template to make further discriminations. He had exposed the error in my thinking without judging *all* my thoughts as incorrect.

Later, I saw one of Prabhupāda’s *sannyāsī* disciples after he had just heard the tape of my conversation with Prabhupāda. His eyes were dreamy and star-filled, like the young in love. He obviously felt fully sanctioned and said, “Prabhupāda said the end justifies the means. We should distribute books by hook or by crook.”

Prabhupāda had said it. Years later when I reminded that *sannyāsī* of his words he remarked, “And then I proceeded to wreak havoc.” Prabhupāda had ordered his books distributed. The *sannyāsī* had tried, thinking that as long as the number of books he distributed increased, his actions were pleasing to Prabhupāda. He had, however, caused difficulties both within the temples and with the public. How mysterious is spiritual life—completely incomprehensible to a materialist. We must serve our spiritual master sincerely and abide by his order without hesitation, even if we are unable to do it perfectly. Nevertheless, mistakes, even those committed in all sincerity, may prove costly both to ourselves and to others.

Prabhupāda had rejected my claim of increased humility and thus advancement because it was not accompanied by the performance of my duty (remaining in Hong Kong). Yet the mixed results accruing from the immature performance of duty leads us to recognize that not only must we do our duty, but we must learn to do it in mature consciousness, for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure. This takes advancement in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. By following the standard *sādhana,* especially hearing and chanting about Kṛṣṇa without offense, our subtle and gross material tendencies naturally decrease and our desire to act simply for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure increases. Thus as we advance, the increased devotion to Kṛṣṇa with which we perform our duties further empowers those duties—especially our preaching—with spiritual potency, *bhakti.*

Although at this time I had neither the scriptural knowledge nor the maturity to grasp the depths of Prabhupāda’s point, I eventually understood it more clearly. We must continue doing our duty, even though we may perform our duty acting from a self-centered, selfish platform. That is natural because we are fresh from material life (Bg. 2.31–37). As we advance, we become less self-centered and selfish (Bg. 3.10–16). Later, as our transcendental knowledge and goodness increases, we perform that same duty but with detachment (Bg. 2.39–53; 3.16–25; 9.27). Still later, while performing the same activity, we act beyond detachment, for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure on the platform of full surrender (Bg. 9.26; 18.65–66). This platform of activity is true *bhakti,* although the other platforms should neither be minimized nor criticized. Those preliminary stages are also *bhakti* in the sense that they lead to *bhakti.* Renunciation of duties without causing ourselves an adverse effect may take place only on the most advanced platform, the *uttama-bhāgavata* platform, at either *bhāva-bhakti* or *prema-bhakti* (Bg. 3.4–8).

Kṛṣṇa Himself cautions Arjuna to neither criticize nor disrupt the minds or the activities of those who are materially attached but who are nevertheless working dutifully (Bg. 3.26–31). It is by offering their work along with the fruits of that work that they will advance. These points had clearly flowed from the mouth of Śrī Kṛṣṇa as a river of nectar in the *Bhagavad-gītā* and Prabhupāda had elaborated on them in his purports.

Due to my lack of realization at this time, I reacted improperly to the immature application of Prabhupāda’s order—the book distribution and other activities of the devotees. Prabhupāda was displeased by my critical attitude and over the years, he had found me guilty of losing my perspective on three counts: neglecting to follow the standard ISKCON program, becoming critical of devotees who were performing their duty on the order of their spiritual master, and giving up my own duty.

In addition, Prabhupāda clarified Siddha-svarūpa’s position and made clear his desire that the “outside” devotees remain in ISKCON with his other disciples. Personality clashes, overly self-assured opinions, hot tempers, and arguments will always be part of institutional life. Yet, despite that fact, through devotion to Prabhupāda—his society, his order, and his mission—we should somehow cooperate. As difficult as it may be, we should take advantage of each other’s realizations and strengths. We should actively seek to understandand benefit from the validity of each other’s points of view, even if those opinions oppose our own, and even if we are “more correct” than they. “Discuss,” Prabhupāda said. Remain united to invigorate and push on Prabhupāda’s mission, which is the mission of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

## Answering Kṛṣṇa’s Call

I mulled over Prabhupāda’s words again and again as the days passed in Honolulu. I wanted to understand them fully. One day, after being in Honolulu for about ten days, I was called to the pay phone outside the temple complex. “Hurry! It’s Jagadīśa calling from the mainland.”

I ran to the phone and heard Jagadīśa’s familiar voice. After we exchanged obeisances and hellos, Jagadīśa said he had something to tell me before I arrived in California. ISKCON Laguna Beach no longer needed a temple president. Revatīnandana needed to be encouraged and would now perform that service.

“Thanks,” I shouted into the phone. “I’m in the middle of jumping from Australia to Laguna Beach and just before landing, you tell me I shouldn’t have jumped. Glad you wanted to tell me before I arrived.” Jagadīśa remained calm as I stormed on for a few moments. Then he suggested several alternatives. I could open a new temple in Santa Barbara, California, I could help him in his *gurukula* service in Dallas, or I could travel with him on his preaching rounds. When I hung up, I wondered what Kṛṣṇa had in store for Bhūrijana dāsa.

My wondering started me wandering and I met Bhaktījana, my eccentric devotee-friend whom I had last seen in Māyāpur. I told him about my situation. Bhaktījana gave me the same advice he had offered in Māyāpur: “Why not go see Prabhupāda and ask him what you should do?”

So obvious! Why hadn’t I thought of it? Go to Prabhupāda and ask him. I thanked Bhaktījana and ran toward Prabhupāda’s rooms.

\* \* \*

Prabhupāda had once told me, “I wish I could train all my disciples personally, but what can I do?” The great fortune of having such an exalted and powerful spiritual master did carry for most disciples the “price tag” of infrequent physical association. Of course, we understood even then that *“vāṇī* is more important than *vapu,*” and that we should simply serve Prabhupāda in his vast mission. We never begrudged our circumstances, which were overwhelmingly fortunate, yet since my conversation with Prabhupāda in Honolulu, I found myself always longing for more of his personal association.

## An Order from Guru

I entered Prabhupāda’s rooms and offered my obeisances. He sat alone at his low desk, the yellow glow of his desk lamp highlighting his already golden face.

I explained my dilemma to Prabhupāda and relayed the choices I had been offered. Prabhupāda, his face grave, listened and considered the options. I felt as if he was examining my file. “No new temples,” was his first statement. Then, after thinking for a few moments more, he told me how to serve him.

“You and Jagadīśa organize *gurukulas.* Not just centrally in one place, but all around the world. We don’t want these children to become technicians or factory workers. Make sure that they are properly taken care of.”

“With love,” I added, and Prabhupāda nodded his head in agreement.

Prabhupāda told me that our temples can also have nursery schools wherein one mother could supervise many children. “I have seen it,” he added. “One can care for fifty. If a mother only wants to take care of her own children, then she should not live in the temple.”

\* \* \*

My first reaction to Prabhupāda’s instruction that I work in education was negative, but within moments, I surrendered and became enthusiastic. It was a relief to again have Prabhupāda’s order to serve.

I offered my obeisances and left the room. Within a few days, I said good-bye to Prabhupāda, and flew with Jagattāriṇī and Vrindaban Leela to Los Angeles. I could not know when I left Hawaii that Prabhupāda would depart from this world in November of 1977 and that I would not have the fortune of seeing him again.

## Epilogue

I met Jagadīśa in Los Angeles. After a few weeks there, my family and I moved to Dallas, and I assumed the duties of headmaster of the small school that remained after ISKCON’s original *gurukula* had closed. I also had the opportunity for the first time since joining ISKCON to serve installed Deities.

I remember the shock of first entering Rādhā-Kālācāndji’s altar. The atmosphere on the altar was exactly the same as being close to Prabhupāda. I then understood that Prabhupāda had captured Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in his heart.

Prabhupāda wrote to Jagattāriṇī and I in Dallas. He said he was pleased that we had settled into a steady ISKCON service. That service in the Dallas *gurukula,* however, was an administrative post, nota teaching post. One day I received a phone call from Jagadīśa requesting me to visit ISKCON’s farm in Port Royal, Pennsylvania, and possibly become the sole teacher-supervisor of that school. When I spoke to him on the phone, I intuitively knew that Port Royal would become my home.

Jagattāriṇī and I went to Gītā-nāgarī and gradually took up the responsibility of educating the ten devotee children who were in residence with their parents at the farm. As I knew nothing about teaching, these students became my teachers at “The School of Essential Experience.” I had no books, no curriculum, no training, and no one with whom to consult. My students, on the other hand, were all experienced *gurukula* veterans, capable of pushing a novice teacher far beyond his limits. Yet we continued, based on Prabhupāda’s order and a desire to please him.

While I was in Dallas, Prabhupāda traveled first to Los Angeles, then to New York, then to India. His final illness had begun. From Los Angeles and a few months later from Vṛndāvana, Prabhupāda wrote and encouraged me to be steadily engaged in teaching and to “Please take this movement very seriously.”

Gradually his health deteriorated. When I traveled from Australia with Prabhupāda, I was able to give him the $1,000 I had hoped to give him the year before. Over the next year, I continued to send Prabhupāda donations in my regular letters to him. When I understood Prabhupāda was ill, I sent him half of all the money I had left, about $1,000. When Prabhupāda left Bombay for Vṛndāvana in May 1977, I understood that my only shelter would soon be leaving. I cried like a small child who knew he was soon to become an orphan, although I could not really understand what that meant.

Prabhupāda’s health didn’t improve and I again sent him half the money I had. By this time (June 1977), Prabhupāda’s secretaries were answering his mail on his behalf. I knew it was inevitable that Prabhupāda would leave, but I could not actually conceive of a life without Prabhupāda’s strong and concerned shelter.

Prabhupāda’s health continued to decline steadily. Fearing that my opportunities to offer him donations were running out, I sent Prabhupāda my final donation. I sent every penny I had.

Again I received a reply from Prabhupāda’s secretary.

In November 1977, three days after observing Śrī Govardhana-pūjā, I was with Dhṛṣṭadhyumna in Harrisburg, Pennsylvania. We heard that Prabhupāda had left this world.

We cried, we consoled the younger devotees, we lectured about the passing of our beloved Prabhupāda, but we could not conceive of the significance of Prabhupāda’s departure. We knew the philosophy. We had studied, practiced, and lectured on it for years. We knew *vāṇī* was more important than *vapu*—that imbibing the spiritual master’s instructions was much more important than associating with the spiritual master’s physical form, from which we may be separated from at any time. We knew this, but when Prabhupāda actually left, no philosophy gave us shelter. He was our shelter. He was the person *bhāgavata,* the giver of the book *bhāgavata,* and he had set like the sun from this world.

Who would pull us out of *māyā* when we stumbled? Who would give us another chance? To whom could we write and be overjoyed at being handed a yellowish aerogram, striped in orange and blue, with Tridandi Gosvami A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami written on its back in that special, purple-red ink? Upon whose feet could we place our heads as he patted our *śikhās* with the affection of a loving father?

We cried intermittently for days and weeks and months. We clung to his instructions and our memories of him. We had no choice.

Oh, how we miss that personality, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, our Guru Mahārāja, our lord and holy master, our dearest friend, our most concerned father. How we miss him. We were mere children when he left this world. Although his blessings continuously rain upon us as he promised, and although we do not feel he is actually gone, how we miss that personality. While he was in this world I asked him about the trivial, the foolish, and the mundane. What could a baby, with his small hands and limited consciousness, beg from Kṛṣṇa’s dearest devotee?

It is now more than eighteen years since his departure. I have been his disciple for twenty-seven years. Still, I can only glimpse his greatness, which seems, regardless of how I grow, to always loom ahead like a great mountain peak. Feeling like an elderly orphan, I long for his association more and more. I pray for his eternal service.

Śrīla Prabhupāda *kī jaya*!
